В хода на изложението подробно се описва ролята, която играят и ще играят различни вътрешнополитически и външнополитически фактори. Акценти са поставени върху позициите на най-важните полити- чески партии в страната, феномена „югоносталгия“, влиянието на войните, съпровождащи разпадането на СФРЮ, и косовския въпрос дотолкова, докол- кото последният официално в Сърбия се счита и за проблем от вътрешно естество. Разгледани са стопанските аспекти, свързани с кандидатурата на Сърбия за пълноправно членство. Текстът обръща внимание на ролята на най-важните международни фактори като ЕС, САЩ, Русия, Китай, както и на балканските страни, Унгария и Турция в процеса на получаване на пълно- правно членство на Сърбия в ЕС. Представена и анализирана е официалната българска позиция по европейската интеграция на Сърбия, като е обърна- то внимMаIнNиISеT RнYа OмFя сFOтRоEтINоG н AаF FбAъIRлгSа рското малцинство в Сърбия и на образа REPUBLIC OF BULGARIA 113 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY The Taliban bomb that can sweep up the regional and the global forces Prof. Vladimir Chukov In his Twitter account, the mufti of Hay’at Tahrir al-Sham (formerly al-Nusra) ruling the Syrian province of Idlib, the Tunisian al-Idrisi has exclaimed, “After the liberation of Afghanistan, the world is no longer the same. The Taliban have decided to redraw once again the political map of the world.“ A poem about the Taliban founder – Mullah Omar, entitled “The Guardian of the Sharia”, has appeared on the social media close to Al-Qaeda. In addition, congratulatory telegrams are being sent to the Taliban leadership from entities that can be classified as belonging to both the political and the radical Islam. The “basket” of congratulatory addresses abounds with social media postings by varied Al- Qaeda branches (such as the Ansar al-Sharia in Yemen, the Harris al-Sharia and the Huras ad Din in Syria, the Al-Qaeda Khorasan cell in Syria, etc.), as well as with congratulations from individual pro-Al Qaeda supporters, among these one of Osama bin Laden’s sons – Omar, the former leader of the Shura, the Al Nusra Advisory Council – Abu Maria al-Qahtani, and the Muslim Brotherhood, the most active of these being the ones in Egypt and Syria. A number of analysts have predicted that as an already official ruling force in Afghanistan, the Taliban are going to take over the baton of a most influential Islamist power, thus replacing the Muslim Brotherhood organization, which has become mired in an internal strife and an organizational crisis. At the same time, the ISIL-Khorasan terrorist organization, which is considered a component element of the ISIL, has directly attacked the Taliban by calling them “murdatin” (apostates). According to this organization, the peace talks in Qatar’s capital – Doha, have turned the “fighters” into “negotiators”, which is bound to only mean “a defeat”. In an August 21, 2021 issue of its weekly Al- Naba magazine, the ISIL accused the Taliban movement of having established an “incomplete” Sharia. According to the author of this article, this had been the reason for ISIL’s defeat by the United States in 2001. In a completely logical way of thinking, the ISIL terrorists have accused the Taliban of having got into “the bosom of tyrants.” The ISIL ideologists argue that “the current victory of the Taliban has been coordinated with the United States thus meaning that Washington has been the genuine constructor of the new governing model of this movement“. The author of this article also maintains the following: “The victory of Islam does not pass through the Qatari hotels or through the embassies of Russia, China or Iran! The victory that America had expected has been sponsored by Qatar and its media. This victory has been blessed by the “Sururi” movement (an Islamist movement founded by the Syrian Muhammad Surur, which had been seen as a deviation from or an instrument of the political version of Wahhabism), by the “Murjiism” (a 7th-century legal-and-dogmatic movement of a sort of an 114 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY ecumenical genesis containing non-Sunni ideological elements), and by the Muslim Brotherhood. All this has been an illusory victory.” The ISIL has also attacked the Palestinian Hamas radical movement, which has welcomed the Taliban rule over all of Afghanistan. The relevant article emphasizes the following: “Even the apostate Hamas movement has greeted and blessed, for the first time in its history, the Taliban. It has published photos of a meeting in Doha between the Hamas delegations and the Taliban. The new Taliban are no longer officially branded terrorists, they are no longer a source of danger, they are only an object of praise.“ On the other hand, ISIL’s position on the Taliban has not been surprising. As it comes to some outright ideological differences dating back to the time when the Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan (1996-2001) granted asylum to the Al-Qaeda terrorist organization in this country. Even at that time, the spiritual leaders of the two organizations used to have violent dogmatic clashes. Which are, in effect, discrepancies between the orthodox Salafi interpretation of the Islam and its predominantly Sufi version. The Al-Qaeda’s mufti used to directly accuse the Taliban of following Sufism (the Islamic mysticism) rather than the “pure” Islam – Salafism. The withdrawal of the United States and NATO from Afghanistan has been a tool for the most serious shift within the geopolitical strata in Central Asia. It has contributed to a new dynamics in the complex relations between the United States and Russia, on one hand, but also in the relations between the United States and China. If we make an analysis of the way the Taliban had been conceived, of the history of their behavior, of the governance formula during their period of ruling in 1996-2001, of the model of their relations with the minorities, of the long negotiations with the United States in 2011, of the final agreement reached, and of the regional situation, we will see that the Afghani insurgent movement has been a long-term project that could have affected greatly the balance of powers within the race of the superpowers. The collapse of the Afghan army within ten days in the face of the Taliban army has been no surprise to Washington. In a detailed study conducted in January 2021, the US expert – Dr. Jonathan Schroden, has made it clear that, in the event of a US military withdrawal, the Afghan National Army (the ANA) would not have been able to withstand the Taliban attacks. The military expert has also noted that Afghanistan’s defense and police forces, totaling 352 000 people, had no chance against 60 000 Taliban field fighters. These figures, however, did not reflect the real situation on the ground, as the Taliban had more than 200 000 people recruited into the local militias as helpers and ready-made “reservists”. The American expert has also quoted a Taliban field commander saying: “We see this battle as a prayer. If one brother gets killed, then the other brother is going to put on his shoes in order not to disappoint Allah.” Particularly detailed is the section about the assistance provided by Pakistan (material, logistical and intelligence assistance), which had been carefully concealed over the years. On the other hand, the Director of the Tehran International Studies & Research Institute – Samia Marouati, is of the opinion that the major mistake of the ANA 115 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY political and military leadership has been that mostly Pashtuns had been involved in the Special Forces. In fact, the Pashtuns themselves had not complied with the unwritten principle that “Pashtuns never fight against Pashtuns.” The specific Afghan tribalism has imposed the logical rule of the classic military game of “Pashtuns and non-Pashtuns”, which has been played in the country in the course of decades. The traditional success formula of governing the country involves a rotation between the Pashtuns and the minorities (mostly Tajiks and Uzbeks). The violation of the May 24, 1992, agreement in Peshawar by the main jihadist groups that had waged the war against the USSR by the Tajik – Burhanuddin Rabbani, who had refused to hand over the power to the Pashtun – Sibghatullah Mojaddedi, had unleashed a devastating civil war. The leading feature in this war had been the tribalistic affiliation, along with a parallel, but not such influential involvement of the religious factor. The Tunisian researcher – Farid Kuduma, has been claiming that “the Taliban is a Pashtun nationalist movement, which takes a purely tribal approach to the governing of politics, of the judiciary, and of all other areas of life.” Some experts refer to it as the “Pashtunwali”, thus distinguishing between the large ethnic community and the individual components within this community. It is a prerequisite for the Pashtuns not to elect a strong authoritarian leader, but to rather maintain a plurality of leaders of the individual tribes, clans and militias. As for the religious origins of this movement, they are characterized by its outright denominational affiliation with the Deobandi school, the latter being a mixture of Mawlid Sufism, of the Hanafi school of the hadiths, and of some Salafi dogmatic principles. The latter had been done quite on purpose with a view of attracting some Saudi financial aid, as this particular type of dogma still dominates in the oil-rich Saudi kingdom. This is the reason why some Salafist ulemas define the Taliban as being a Takfiri (apostate) movement implementing “incomplete Sharia“. In his in-depth analysis, the Tunisian scholar also claims that the real founder of the Taliban had been Mullah Mohammed Omar (1960-2013). In an article signed by the latter called “Balances within the Taliban Movement”, the first leader of this movement has described how he and his first fourteen followers, also educated at a madrasa and also practicing the religious Deobandi philosophy in Pakistan, had been outraged by the vicious corrupt practices, which had been an element of the actions of some jihadist formations that had been fighting against the Soviet army. In October 1996, however, in a BBC broadcast, the former Pakistani Prime Minister – Benazir Bhutto, had taken a different view. Ms. Bhutto had talked in detail about the role of Pakistan and also about the Saudi-Emirati funding and the position of the West (the US and Britain) on the then emerging religious movement. There are varied versions of the creation of the Taliban, in which the faith and fanaticism of the people around Mullah Mohammed Omar and the diverse material assistance they used to receive from external factors intersect. In his book entitled “The Taliban Movement: From Creation to Fall”, the Afghan expert – Mohammad Sarafraz, quotes General Akhtar Abdur Rahman of the 116 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY Pakistani Military Intelligence (S.A.S.) as being a key player in the emergence of this particular Islamist movement. According to the author, it had been this particular S.A.S officer who had persuaded the Americans to supply the Afghan mujahedeen with the Stinger missile, which later caused a turning point in the battle against the Soviet army. An interesting detail here is that the Pakistani military intelligence is composed mainly of Pashtuns, which is why it supports, in the first place, the jihadist formations set up by the same ethnic group in neighboring Afghanistan. Thus, the S.A.S. has stood behind the political project of the Islamic Party leader – Gulbuddin Hekmatyar, against the Islamist network with the Tajik leader Burhanuddin Rabbani at the head, along with its first military leader – Ahmad Shah Massoud, from the same ethnic group. Pakistan has always watched over and has behaved like a “Big Brother” in relation to neighboring Afghanistan. A similar profile of Pakistan’s national security system dates back to the time of former President – Muhammad Zia-ul-Haq (1924-1988), who had not only openly supported the Afghan mujahedeen against the Soviet army. Ul-Haq had believed that the Afghani jihad had to destroy the Soviet Union, which, according to him, had illegally occupied the Central Asian Islamic republics and had humiliated the Islam. The former president argued that Pakistan had its historic rights over Afghanistan and, for this reason, he had come up with the idea of a political unification between the two neighboring countries. Once established by the S.A.S., a similar strategy continued during the reign of Benazir Bhutto. The core of this strategy involved the perception of Afghanistan as a province of Pakistan, with the governor of Kabul being a Pashtun, subordinate to the government in Karachi (the then capital of Pakistan). Benazir Bhutto had even come up with a chimerical proposal for forming a political alliance between Pakistan, Afghanistan, and Iran. The Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan (IEA), as is the official name of the new state in Central Asia, was a huge challenge to this region. It is going to be in a position to not only reformat the very region, but also to have an unexpected impact on the world powers, including Russia and China. It is inevitably going to have an impact on Iran, as well. This effect is going to be caused by the proposed “lustrous model of Sharia”, which was going to be completely different from the “Islamic Emirate” that had existed in the period 1996-2001. Respect for human rights, normal life (not different from the era of the Islamic Republic of Afghanistan), free media, a normal banking system, cooperation with the world, attracting foreign investment, and respect for the rights of minorities (the first version of the current government had envisaged the Tajik – Ahmed Massoud, located in the Panjshir Valley and having declared a revolt against the Taliban in Kabul) – all these have been the ambitions of the “new Taliban”. At that stage, of course, these used to be only declared intentions, which were not put into effect. However, they sounded ticklish to all those who were followers of the political systems or formations professing a political or “a softened version” of the radical Islam. The big challenge to Russia, however, have been the minorities in Afghanistan – Tajiks, Uzbeks, Turkmen and some others, which have had a pivoting role to play 117 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY between the Russian Central Asian belt and Afghanistan. Until recently, these nations from the former Soviet republics had been seen as a model for their Afghan counterparts. However, the ambition in Kabul today is to change the situation on an Islamic basis, i.e. to shift the attracting pole from the North to the South. If this happens, this kind of filter for the Russian security is going to fail. Further, on, if we take a closer look, nearly one third of Russia’s own population is Muslim, composed chiefly of people of Central Asian descent. Moscow’s energy resources, especially the oil resources the country has been exporting to Europe, are located in areas with indigenous Muslim population. An illustrative example of this is Tatarstan, in whose capital city – Kazan, the largest mosque in Europe was built (having disputes regarding this indicator with the mosque in the city of Grozny in Chechnya). In the 1990s, when the Russian Federation was still looking for its post-Soviet appearance and stability, the local Muslims even demanded that the position of a Vice President be given to them on a quota principle. Most probably, the “Achilles’ heel” of security in the Central Asian republics is Tajikistan. It is no coincidence that a number of personnel of the Tajik Interior Ministry have sided with the ISIL by going to Syria. After August 15, 2021, when the Taliban captured Kabul, a couple of large-scale military exercises were held, jointly with the Russian army. No less important has been the online meeting, held on October 16, 2021, between the intelligence chiefs of the former Soviet Central Asian republics. The Russian President, Vladimir Putin, also attended this meeting and, according to him, there had been “an active movement of some members of the radical Islamist groups from Syria and Iraq to Afghanistan.” In addition to Mr. Putin’s statement, the Chairman of the Tajik National Security Commission – Simomin Itimov, stood in favor of the opinion that “the trafficking in weapons, ammunition and drugs from neighboring Afghanistan to his country had intensified.” Unlike all Afghanistan’s other neighbors, Tajikistan refused flatly to accept the new governing realities in Kabul and to send traditional good-neighborly greetings to the Taliban. This provoked a gathering of troops along the Tajik-Afghan border. The Islamic radicals accused the Tajik capital, Dushanbe, of having hosted a significant number of former Afghan soldiers who had deserted Afghanistan, along with their weapons. The Taliban demanded that Tajikistan hand over the fighter jets and helicopters (more than 50 aircraft), which some pilots from the former ANA had landed at the air bases in this neighboring country. The US response was not very long to come as they managed to map out a plan for the evacuation of 143 military pilots who had taken refuge in the post-Soviet Central Asian republic, insisting that they had been trained at US military facilities. As if by chance, this event coincided with the visit to Moscow of the US Under Secretary of State – Victoria Nuland, in charge of Eurasian Affairs at the US State Department. Obviously, Ms. Nuland’s personal presence in the Russian capital had been very important. In this context, the United States removed from the list of bans on visits to the United States three high-ranking Kremlin officials for the simple reason that Ms. Nuland herself had been on a similar Moscow blacklist of US senior officials. She was allowed to stay on Russian territory for only a couple of hours. In fact, one of the topics she discussed with her Russian counterpart – 118 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY Deputy Minister Sergei Ryabkov, was fairly interesting. Along with the issue of the state of the two countries’ bilateral relations, she had also raised the issue of admitting American service members at the Central Asian countries’ military bases. Moscow reaffirmed its long-held position that this was unacceptable. However, the situation evolved further. The Kyrgyz President – Sadyr Japarov, stated categorically that he was not going to allow a US military base within the Kyrgyz national territory. The novelty in the complex relations between the US and Russia had been the persistent emergence of the Central Asian issue in the area of coordination (or of lack thereof) between Washington and Moscow in the field of security. Russia had strongly viewed the post-Soviet Central Asia as being its own sphere of influence, but with the advent of Afghanistan of the Taliban the situation had already changed. The Tajik ethnic community in Afghanistan is the second largest, with 27 % after the Pashtun community, which accounts for 42 % of the population. This community is even more numerous in Afghanistan (about 10 million) than in Tajikistan itself (with about 7 million people). Traditionally and, regardless of the situation, the representatives of this community have been having an important part in governing the country. In the period 1992-1997, Tajikistan had been torn apart by a civil war. An active part of President Emomali Rahmon’s opposition (ruling the poorest Central Asian country since 1992) had been played by the religious radicals of the Islamic Party of Tajikistan’s revival. The latter had been banned in 2015. While the main Northern drug channel from Afghanistan to Russia and Europe still goes through Tajikistan. The emergence of the Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan has been no less a challenge for China, which has a 76-kilometer common border with the country. It has been unlikely that the central government in Kabul would send suicide bombers to its own neighboring country. Sufficient has been the effect of the Islamic Emirate model on the irredentist Uighurs inhabiting the Afghan province of Xinjiang. If the Taliban succeed in creating an at least partially idealized Sharia- based Sunni state, this is going to be a powerful incentive for the radicalized Uighurs to rise in revolt against the repressions of communist China. Hence, the dilemma. On one hand, Afghanistan could turn out to be a great transport link within the ambitious global Chinese Belt and Road Initiative, as well as a suitable place to invest in this country’s large-scale underground resources. It has been rumored that the large deposits of lithium minerals needed for the ambitious program of the Chinese telecommunication companies are of particular interest. Professor Daniel Markey from the Johns Hopkins University – a South Asian issues specialist, has noted that the US have always encouraged the Chinese investment in Afghanistan. This investment, however, has remained fairly modest thus far. At the same time, Markey believes that the Chinese enthusiasm over the withdrawal of the United States from this Asian country has cooled quickly after the Taliban’s excessively rapid takeover of the entire territory. Beijing has realized that the opening of a wide corridor for the new Silk Road through Afghanistan could turn out to be a trap in which the national security will suffer more than the economy could benefit from it. 119 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY In addition, the very fact that the strongest terrorist organization operating in Afghanistan is ISIL has been extremely sensitive to China and this country’s own security. Furthermore, it is very unpleasant for Beijing that the main ethnic group within the ranks of the radical Islamic fundamentalists is the Uighurs’s group, followed by the Pashtuns and the Tajiks with an Arab leadership. It is also an interesting fact that the leadership of ISIL-Khorasan has revealed the nationality of the perpetrator of the suicide bombing on October 10, 2021, against a Shiite mosque in Kunduz, being an Afghan Uighur. Such a practice is usually rare for the ISIL terrorists. However, the terrorists’ desire to attract the attention of the international community, first and foremost, the attention of China, has been really obvious. The objective being to instill fear in neighboring China in relation to the “potential” of the terrorist-trained Uighurs, who have been repressed by the communist regime in the neighboring totalitarian state. Numbering only some 2000 people, this ethnic group is relatively small in number in Afghanistan. At the same time, these people are second-generation Chinese Uighurs whose parents had fled to the neighboring country as a result of the Chinese repressions in their homeland. Now the fears that the Taliban could really establish good-neighborly relations with China have been forcing these people to seek “protection” in the ranks of ISIL, which is, in fact, an enemy of the ruling circles in both Beijing and Kabul. For its part, the Islamic Republic of Iran is also going to be put to the test. As a minimum, there is going to be some competition between Iran and the Afghani Emirate composed mostly of Sunnis, as the governing structure of the Taliban is going to be similar to that of the Shiite state. The reason being that the source is the same, i.e. the Sharia in its two main dogmatic varieties. The two countries constitute an amalgam of ethnicities and linguistic communities while the theocratic governing formula is based on different interpretations of the two main Sharia sources – the Qur’an and the Hadith. It is a bit too early to outline the specific practical differences, however, only on the second day following the capture of Kabul, the Taliban ordered their governors in the Shiite-Hazara provinces to leave the people there to practice their religious cult without any interference. On the ground, however, things did not turn out to be that way. As each and every individual governor was left to interpret such orders the way he wished to. On the other hand, they cannot boast about the same attitude towards the Sunnis in Iran as they have been doing towards the individual ethnic minorities – the Arabs, the Kurds and the rest of them. So, it is more than obvious that there is going to be competition between the Shiite and the Sunni Islam, on the one hand, and the etatist establishment they have created, on the other. Therefore, the ultimate question is whether the Taliban will manage to set their international situation in order and will finally break out of the isolation in which they now find themselves. It is no coincidence that the Taliban Foreign Minister – Amir Khan Muttaqi, has sent a letter to the UN Secretary-General, Antonio Guterres, asking him for the floor during the 76th session of the UN General Assembly so that one of the movement’s spokesmen – Muhammad Suhail Shaheen, could speak there in public there. At the same time, Muttaqi has stated in his letter that the 120 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY mandate of the representative of the Islamic Republic of Afghanistan – Ghulam Isaczai, has already expired and that he no longer represents this Asian country. The international community, represented by the European Union and the G7, has already specified clearly the criteria in relation to the recognition of the new regime in Kabul. The latter include respect for the rights of women and minorities, along with the provision of conditions for all those wishing to leave the country, rather than creating conditions in the said country to become a haven for some terrorist organizations, such as ISIL, Al-Qaeda, and some others. If the Taliban manage to regain their former international recognition, they will be able to get ahead of their Shiite rivals. It is clear that, after 2021, the Taliban state is not going to be a new version of the one that had been set up by the radical movement in the period 1996-2001. In the first place, they have already announced that they basically accept the 1964 Royal Constitution, approved at the time by the latest Afghan king – Mohammed Zahir Shah (1933-1973). Only the texts, which are at variance with the Sharia, are going to be removed. However, the representatives of the new government have not specified which particular texts they mean. In response to the Iranian Foreign Minister Hossein Amirabdollahian’s criticism that the two countries cannot be compared because no genuine elections can be held in Iran, a Taliban representative has left the door open for such elections in his country, as well. The structure of the new Taliban state remains still unfinished and, also, largely unpredictable. The setting up of a Supreme Court could probably be interpreted as a “secularization” of the future Afghan jurisprudence. In this context, one could arrive at the conclusion that the Taliban intend to “humanize” the execution of the death penalty: “We are going to restore the death penalty and the cutting of hands. But this will no longer be made in public.” These had been the words of the former Minister of Justice and Head of the moral police “al-amr bi-l-maʿrūf wa-n-nahy ʿani-l-munkar” (“Enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong”), Mullah Nooruddin Turabi. In the period 1996-2001, when the Taliban used to rule Afghanistan, these punishments used to be administered and carried out in stadiums. The publicity of the execution of the death penalty stems from the pure Sharia. As it is a tool for intimidating the potential perpetrators of the same crime. On October 24, 2021, the Afghan social media presented a video of a hitherto unknown Shiite armed group. It is called the Hazaristan Unknown Front Forces (HUFF). In this video, one of the masked potential members of this formation declares that “they will fight against repression to the last drop of blood.” However, a Taliban spokesman of the new government – Zabiullah Mujahid, declared afterwards that the new government has had no information about such a group. The Hazara are a Shiite minority in Afghanistan, which accounts for some 9 % of the country’s population. Asked by the Chairman of the International Union of Muslim Scholars – Ali Muhiuddin al-Qaradaghi, who was heading a delegation in Kabul, why no Shiite ministers have been appointed (so far, only one Deputy Minister of Health), a Taliban representative replied that, since 1979, there has been not a single Sunni minister in Iran. 121 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY It is not clear what the impact of the HUFF has been among the Afghan Shiites. However, if this were a parallel mirror of the Sunni irredentists in Iran (especially of the Jundallah group, which recruits mostly people from the Sunni minority in the Sistan and Balochistan provinces of Iran), the Taliban are going to face serious internal problems. The emergence of the Islamic Emirate of Afghanistan has been an event whose effect could be compared only to the one of the September 11, 2011, attacks. Some experts believe that the consequences of this event are going to be even longer when compared to the past 20 years during which the West used to rule Afghanistan. The different behavior of the Russian President Putin in relation to the two events has also been somewhat ambiguous. In 2001, the Kremlin boss used to be among the first ones to congratulate the then US President – George W. Bush, on his country’s intervention in this Asian country. Today, however, the same Russian head of state has been far less enthusiastic and openly wary of the new, genuine situation in this part of the world. The Chinese diplomacy has traditionally been frugal in its own assessments by bringing some moderate optimism to the fore. During the regular meetings held between the two countries at different levels (in China, Qatar or Afghanistan), there has been a somewhat curious event: an emerging barter agreement of “material assistance against security”, namely “the elimination of any presence of the Uyghur Turkistan Islamic Party in Afghanistan”. Out of the above-mentioned three countries, only Iran seems to have been the most vocal one, but also most controversial in relation to the sudden appearance of its brand new neighbor. If the Islamic Republic’s ambassador to Kabul has been speaking quite diplomatically about “the great prospects for working together and the expected investments in Afghanistan”, his chief – the Foreign Minister, Hossein Amirabdollahian, has been of the opposite opinion. It is no coincidence that the Taliban did not send a diplomatic envoy to the meeting of the representatives of Afghanistan’s neighboring countries, which took place on October 27, 2021, in Tehran. It is obvious that they have been applying the approach of differentiation between the individual foreign policy subjects, which is obligatory in the foreign policy domain. As they differentiate between their “great powers” neighbors (China and Russia), their rival neighbors (Iran), and their benevolent neighbors (Pakistan). Prof. Dr. Vladimir Chukov is an Arabist, as well as a university lecturer and analyzer in the area of the Middle East policy and the Islam. He is also the author of a number of books in this particular domain. Mr. Chukov has graduated from the Faculty of Social Sciences at the University of Damascus in Syria. Currently, he is a lecturer at the “Angel Kanchev” University of Ruse and a researcher at the Institute of Philosophy and Sociology at the Bulgarian Academy of Sciences. In the period 1995-1998, he used to be Editor-in-Chief of the International Relations Journal. He is a member of the editorial boards of the “Geopolitics” and the “Philosophical Forum” magazines. Mr. Chukov is also a member of a number of international research organizations, among these the European Association for Middle East Studies, the Central Eurasian Society Studies, etc. He is also co-author of the specialized online edition entitled “The Foreign Insider”. 124 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY Resource conflicts in the context of modern international relations Dr. Ivo Tsekov Summary The role of natural resource scarcity has been among the most pressing problems in recent times and occupies a special place in the specialized literature on security issues. Currently, the global demand for a great number of key raw materials has been growing incessantly. However, it is equally important to take into account the fact that industrialization has been spreading over an increasing number of parts of the globe, thus boosting steadily the world’s wealth, which has, in turn, been leading to a steady demand for energy, cars, construction materials and household appliances, as well as for some other commercial goods that depend on the availability of natural resources. Therefore, the problem related to the natural resource scarcity and the environment has to be regarded as a major challenge to the world’s security while the study of this problem should become of particular interest to the international relations and security scientists, to the politicians and to the relevant experts. Identifying the link between ecology, resources and security The word “security” (security; secure – in English) is of Latin origin where “securus” (“se” standing for “without” and “curus” standing for: 1. “care”; 2. “anxiety” or “distress”) signifies as a whole “freedom from anxiety and distress” or “peace of mind”, without any risk or threat involved. On one hand, security can be presented as an indisputable value, indispensable for life as such; while, on the other hand, it could be presented as a widely discussed concept, which has been receiving multifaceted interpretations from antiquity to the present day. The controversy over the nature of this term is related to the varied theoretical schools and research areas in the political science domain, as well as to changes taking place in the development of international relations, the changes in the security environment, and the global threats. Since the time of the ancient philosophers – Plato and Aristotle, security, as a generic term, has been defined as the impossibility to inflict any harm in terms of protecting the individual person and his/her environment against extreme danger.1 While in the classical sense, this concept is presented as “peace or relief from worries”, which Cicero used to define as “the absence of anxiety on which the realization of life depends”2. Security is a complex concept made up of the absence of danger and of the confidence of the individual, the society and the state that they are protected against all possible dangers; while, according to some other authors, security stands for a set of political goals, strategies and methods serving to prevent a war while maintaining, at the same time, the capacity of political self-determination. 1 Miloushev, Vassil. National security in the wake of World War II. In: “Current National Security Problems”. Yearbook of the “G.S. Rakovski” Military Academy. Sofia, 1995. pp. 15-21. 2 Liotta, P. H. Boomerang Effect: The Convergence of National and Human Security, in: Security Dialogue, vol.33, No 4, pp. 473-488. 125 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY Security is also regarded as the absence of any threat or a situation in which the emergence of a threat could be either prevented or isolated from any effects that it could have on a particular state (union or region). The expansion of the neo-realistic security concepts involves a wide range of potential threats, ranging from the economic and environmental issues to human rights and migration. Any “deepening” of the security issues is bound to lead either downwards – to the level of individual and humanitarian security; or upwards – to the level of international and global security, coupled with the regional or public security as an intermediate level. National security could be also defined as a relatively dynamic state of the nation, characterized by objective external and internal circumstances in which the vital national interests are sufficiently protected against any modern risks or threats and due to which this state inevitably gives rise to a dominant sense of security in the public sentiments.3 The main characteristic features of the security system of each and every organizational unit are strictly specific and are bound to change over time; this requires a timely analysis of the influencing factors in order to be able to identify the vulnerabilities, as well as to determine the nature and the sources of the threat, along with the degree of endangerment. If we look back at the history of mankind, we will see that almost each and every great civilization in the past used to ultimately collapse by losing its social, political and economic stability. And this has eventually been accompanied by a dramatic drop in the population. Some of these civilizations, e.g. the ones of Egypt and China, managed to later recover from these collapses, at various stages; while others, such as the ones of the Easter Island or the Maya civilization, remained in the distant past forever. All of these collapses in the distant past used to be either local or regional; while some other societies and civilizations in many other geographical locations remained unaffected. At particular places, for example in the valleys of the Tigris and the Euphrates, the new civilizations emerged in the place of the previous ones. And in many, if not in most of the cases, the overexploitation of the environment had been the immediate or the ultimate reason for this. However, for the first time nowadays, the global human civilization, i.e. the global, increasingly interconnected and high-tech society in which we all participate to one degree or another, is threatened by a collapse as a result of a number of natural problems. As mankind has found itself in what the Chief Scientific Adviser of the United Kingdom – John Beddington, has referred to as “the perfect storm” of environmental problems.4 The most serious of these problems indicate a rapidly increasing severity related, first and foremost, to some climate disturbances. However, some other elements could also contribute potentially to this collapse, e.g. the speeded extinction of some animal and plant populations and species, which could result in the loss of some ecosystems that are essential to: the human survival; the soil degradation and the changes in the land use patterns; the spread of toxic compounds from pole to pole; the ocean 3 Angelov, А., The National Security System under the New Conditions. Sofia, 2000. р. 18. 4 Top scientist appeals for better communication of risk, United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction, 126 June 21, 2012, at: https://www.undrr.org/news/top-scientist-appeals-better-communication-risk ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY acidification and eutrophication (the formation of the so-called dead zones); the deterioration of some aspects of the epidemiological environment (e.g. the factors that make the human population susceptible to infectious diseases); the depletion of the increasingly scarce resources, including, in the first place, the groundwater, which is being overused in a great number of key agricultural areas; as well as the wars over the said resources.5 All the above are not individual problems as they tend to interact within two giant and complex adaptive systems, namely the biosphere and the human socio-economic system. The negative manifestations of these interactions are often associated with the human activity. Therefore, the identification of some ways to prevent the generation of a global collapse is perhaps the most important challenge facing up humanity today. The footprint of human activity has been the result of overpopulation, of the excessive consumption of natural resources, and of the use of unnecessarily harmful technologies and socio-economic and political arrangements to serve the general consumption of Homo sapiens. Even today, the size of the human population exceeds by far the long-term capacity of our planet; which shows that a nearly half of an additional planet is going to be needed for the purpose of sustaining the number of today’s world population of some seven billion people (with all their ordinary businesses, modern technologies and modern living standards). Finally, if all the Earth’s citizens consume resources at the US level, we are going to need another four to five Earths.6 If by the year 2050 we add the estimated 2.5 billion more people, this is going to make the human impact onto the systems of sustaining the life of civilization disproportionately worse as almost everywhere the people come across nonlinear reaction systems whereby the environmental damages increase with a speed that gets increasingly faster on account of each and every additional person. Of course, there have been often some assumptions, e.g. that humanity is going to expand drastically the capacity of the Earth by means of its technological innovations, however, it has been recognized on a wide basis that technology can both add to, as well as subtract from the load capacity.7 How probable is it that this set of interrelated difficulties could lead to a global collapse in the current century? There have been numerous definitions, as well as a lot of discussions about the civilizations’ collapses in the past but the future global collapse does not call for a precise definition. As it could be caused by either a global resource conflict or by a “small-scale” nuclear war whose environmental effect can quickly put an end to today’s civilization.8 If we take it globally, a minimum of two billion people today are hungry or malnourished. The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations has estimated that, by the year 2050, a food production increase of about 70% is going to be needed in order to be able to adequately feed a 35% larger and still 5 Daily GC, Ehrlich PR., Impacts of development and global change on the epidemiological environment. Environ. Dev. Econ. 1, 309–344, 2006. 6 Holdren JP, Ehrlich PR., Human population and the global environment, 2017. 7 Alcott B., Impact caps: why population, affluence and technology strategies should be abandoned, pp. 520-566, 2016. 8 Ehrlich PR, et al., Long-term biological consequences of nuclear war. Science 222, 2013. 127 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY growing human population.9 What are the prospects for the global economy of being able to produce and distribute enough food? In order to be able to achieve this, it will be probably necessary to achieve a great number of the following, if not – of all of the objectives listed below:  reduce drastically the climate disturbances;  limit the expansion of the agriculture areas (in order to preserve the ecosystem services);  increase the yields wherever possible;  put an additional effort to protect the soil;  raise the fertilizer, water and energy efficiency;  increase the number of vegetarians;  grow more food for the humans;  impose alternative fuel for the motor vehicles;  reduce the waste of food;  put an end to the ocean degradation and regulate better the aquacultures;  increase significantly the investments in sustainable agriculture and aquaculture research.10 Unfortunately, the agricultural system is complexly linked to all the major drivers of environmental degradation. Agriculture itself is a major source of greenhouse gases and is thus an important cause for climate disruption, being at the same time extremely vulnerable to the effects of climate change. The temperatures have been reaching record values and we are still expecting ever more severe storms, droughts, heat waves and floods, which seem to be an obvious threat to the agricultural production. Land is a major resource for agriculture and is, therefore, faced up with a great number of threats. In addition to the serious and widespread problems related to soil degradation, a rise in the sea levels (the surest consequence of global warming) is going to take away from production lots of important areas, either by backfilling (an increase of 1 meter is going to flood 17.5% of the area of Bangladesh11); and lastly, by exposing all these areas to more frequent surges of mountainous waves, or by salinizing the coastal aquifers that are essential for the irrigation water. In addition to all the above, agriculture has been among the major reasons for the loss of biodiversity and, therefore, for the critical ecosystem services provided to agriculture itself (e.g. pollination, pest control, soil fertility, climate stability). Agriculture is also a major source of global toxicity thus exposing the human population to countless fine poisons. The threat of climate change in relation to food production alone means that the whole system of mobilizing the energy of humanity has to be rapidly transformed. The warming has to be kept well below the potential increases in the average global temperature by 5°C – a level that is in a position to destroy civilization. Today’s best estimate is that the world is already committed to raising the average global 9 Foley JA. 2011. Can we feed the world and sustain the planet? A five-step global plan could double food production by 2050 while greatly reducing environmental damage. Sci. Am. 305, pp. 60–65 10 Hayes B., Computation and the human predicament, 2012. 11 Szczepanski, Marcin, Sedlar, Frank, Shalant, Jenny, Bangladesh: A Country Underwater, a Culture on the Move, Natural Resources Defense Council, September 13, 2018, at: https://www.nrdc.org/onearth/ 128 bangladesh-country-underwater-culture-move ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY temperature by 2.4°C. This is well above the 2°C estimated a decade ago by the climate scientists as a “safe” limit but today it is considered that the old limit is no longer sufficient given the effects observed even before an increase of one degree has been reached.12 It is difficult to assess the exact threats of anthropogenic climate disturbances, ranging from deaths and the spread of tropical diseases to the rise of the sea level, the crop failure and the severe storms. One of the key factors in avoiding a global collapse, and therefore a topic that requires great effort and caution, is the avoidance of mass climate-related famine. Today’s human agricultural system has developed in a geological period with a relatively constant and benign climate and has been well-adapted to the conditions of the twentieth century. This alone could be considered to be a cause for serious concern, as the planet’s climate has been rapidly shifting to new, less predictable regimes. Therefore, it is essential to slow down this process. This is going to involve a dramatic transformation of most of the already existing energy mobilization infrastructure, as well as a change in the human behavior in order to make the energy system much more efficient.13 The major challenge, of course, involves the phasing out of more than half of the fossil fuel use by the year 2050 in order to prevent the worst effects of climate disruption – a challenge that the latest edition of the International Energy Agency’s “World Energy Outlook” has made look more serious.14 This, however, highlights yet another dilemma. Currently, the fossil fuels are essential in the area of agriculture for the production of fertilizers and pesticides, for the operation of agricultural machinery, for irrigation (all too often wasteful), for animal husbandry, crop drying, food storage, transport, and distribution. Thus, this phasing out will have to include at least a partial replacement of the non-fossil fuels in these functions, without a significant increase in the food prices. Unfortunately, however, any significant steps including the limitation of the global emissions to their peak value by the year 2020 and their reduction to half of the current levels by the year 2050 are more than problematic, both economically and politically. The fossil fuel companies will have to leave most of their proven reserves in the ground, thus destroying much of the economic value of this particular industry. However, as the ethics of some businesses involves the deliberate pursuance of some deadly but lucrative activities, it is not surprising that the interests involving large financial stakes in the burning of fossil fuels are an important negative factor in the public dialogue on climate, as well as in the blocking of any attempts to tackle the climate changes. Economic aspects of the depleting natural resources Quite recently, some fundamentally new ecological risks have been cropping up with respect to the economy, related to the formation and the development of the technogenic environment, i.e. the so-called technosphere (covering industrial sites, energy sites, economic infrastructure, military sites, etc.) This is a determining 12 Godfray HCJ, et al., Food security: the challenge of feeding 9 billion people. Science 327, 812., 2010. 13 Klare MT., Rising powers, shrinking planet: the new geopolitics of energy. New York, NY: Henry Holt and Company, 2018. 14 World Energy Transitions Outlook: 1.5°C Pathway, International Renewable Energy Agency, March 2021, at: https://www.irena.org/-/media/Files/IRENA/Agency/Publication/2021/March/IRENA_World_ Energy_Transitions_Outlook_2021.pdf 129 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY environmental factor that has a negative effect on society. Economic growth stands for an increase in real production (i.e. the genuine GDP). Therefore, any increases in the production or the consumption are bound to involve a leap in the negative effects on the environment. The impact of economic growth on the environment includes increased consumption of non-renewable resources, higher levels of pollution, global warming, and a potential loss of habitats in the environment. On the other hand, any increases in consumption involve opportunity costs implying lower reserves of non-renewable resources. For example, the rate of global economic growth over the past century has resulted in a decline in the availability of natural resources, such as forests (cut down for the purpose of providing more agricultural land or timber); reduction of the oil, coal and gas sources; loss of some of the fishing stocks due to overfishing; and the loss of the species diversity as a damage to the natural resources, which has led to the extinction of some species.15 Below, find a list of the economic growth major negative effects:  Pollution. The increased consumption of fossil fuels could lead to some immediate problems, including poor air quality and soot (such as the London smog in the 1950s or the smog in New Delhi today). Some of the most serious problems involving the burning of fossil fuels have been mitigated by the clean air legislation that restricts coal burning in the urban centres.  Less visible pollution. Although smog is a very clear and obvious danger, the effects of increased CO2 emissions are less obvious and are, therefore, a lesser incentive for the politicians to cope with them. Scientists claim that the great amounts of CO2 emissions have contributed to the global warming and the more volatile climatic conditions. All this suggests that economic growth increases the long-term environmental costs – not only currently, but also with respect to the future generations.  The damages to nature. Air, land and water pollution causes health problems and could harm the productivity of the land and the sea.  The global warming and unstable weather. The global warming leads to a rise in the sea levels and volatile weather patterns, which could entail significant economic costs.  The soil erosion. The deforestation resulting from economic development damages the soil and makes the areas more susceptible to drought.  The loss of biodiversity. The economic growth leads to the depletion of resource and the loss of biodiversity. This could harm the so called future “bearing capacity” of the ecological systems for the economy.  The long-term toxins. The economic growth creates long-term waste and toxins with unknown consequences for the time being. For example, economic growth has led to an increase in the use of plastics that do not degrade when disposed of. So, there has been an incessantly growing supply of plastics to the seas and the environment, which is both ugly and harmful to wildlife.16 15 The Effects of Economic Growth on Pollution, St. Louis Fed On the Economy, September 21, 2017, at: https://www.stlouisfed.org/on-the-economy/2017/september/effects-economic-growth-pollution 16 Ali, Saleem and Jose A Puppim de Oliveira, Pollution and economic development: an empirical research review, Environmental Research Letters, Volume 13, Number 12, November 29, 2018, at: https:// iopscience.iop.org/article/10.1088/1748-9326/aaeea7 130 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY Not all the forms of economic growth, however, cause damage to the environment. The rises in the real income are going to make the people more inclined to allocate resources for the protection of the environment and to mitigate the harmful pollution effects. Also, the economic growth caused by improved technologies could allow for higher production with much lower levels of pollution. According to one of the more optimistic theories about economic growth and the environment, the economic growth is bound to cause damages to the natural environment until a certain point, which is going to be followed by a transition to a post-industrial economy and, as a result, to a better environment. For example, in the early days of economic growth, the individual economies tended to burn coal and wood, which caused obvious pollution. However, on the basis of higher incomes, the economy could promote much cleaner technologies to reduce the air pollution. However, the reduction of pollution in one country could lead to the transfer of pollution to another one. For example, we import coal from the emerging economies while effectively exporting the waste for recycling and disposal elsewhere. Protecting the environment, of course, is expensive. However, with a sensible and cooperative policy, the benefits will outweigh the costs. As the investments in the integrated environmental technologies and the efficiency measures usually lead to a lot of cost saving on the operational level, e.g. via lower material and energy costs or reduced waste disposal costs. There is, of course, a great number of some other benefits of practicing environmental protection on the corporate level that are difficult to quantify, some of which incorporate an improved image and a slim chance of accidents. The implementation of environmental and energy management systems is an opportunity for a systematic use of economic opportunities for operational environmental protection, as well as for the continuous improvement of the corporate environmental performance. Protecting the environment has also fairly often some positive effects on the economy as a whole, e.g. by bringing down the material and the health damage, as well as some other environmental costs. Last but not least, the high environmental quality level could turn out to be attractive for the very location of the businesses, which is going to make it possible for the individual regions to attract skilled labor on the basis of their own good environmental image. Environmental migration as a threat to stability and security Environmental migration is a manifestation of a deteriorating ecological situation and occupies an important place in the area of human security. As the people’s vital interests can only be addressed by improving their right to a safe and favorable environment, including by forcible or voluntary relocation to an area with more favorable living conditions. In recent years, the environment as a migration factor has been at the center of international debates in terms of definitions, data, situations and policies regarding the environmental migrants.17 Such analyses indicate how the environmental factors often underlie all other factors. This becomes much clearer if we distinguish between the following two main categories: on one hand – the sudden and abrupt environmental events, and on the other – the gradual and 17 Migration Dictionary, 2nd edition. International Law in Migration № 25, IOM, Geneva, 2013, at: http:// publications.iom.int/bookstore/index.php 131 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY slow climatic processes. Data on the first type of ecological migration are easy to find, while the possibility of reliable forecasts is fairly limited. Defining the second category is even more difficult as the relevant reasons are often gradual in nature and occur in the background. But the effects of climate change on the living conditions of humans are also as extreme as the effects of natural disasters. Some areas of the world are considered to be “climatic hotspots” as the climate change there is bound to boost environmental degradation. Migration has an awful lot of environmental consequences. As climate change and the depletion of natural resources affect both the richest, as well as the most vulnerable areas of the planet. As shown by Harrington and King in their model entitled Temperature of Equivalence, not only the impact of the future climate change is going to vary from region to region, but this is also going to affect most severely the lowest income groups of the population.18 The most obvious and worrying effect, as far as the richest sectors of the population are concerned, is a reduction in the quality of life and an increased differentiation in the living conditions between the richer and the poorer sections of the population. Thus, the topic of energy transition and energy justice comes to the fore, as does the wide-ranging debate on the impact of new technologies designed to reduce the greenhouse effect on the weakest sections of the population. It is obvious that the problems of climate change are related to the unfair distribution of risk within the capitalist economic system: when we look at the economically, socially, politically and ecologically vulnerable regions of the planet, it becomes obvious that the impact of climate change is felt most strongly in these areas, in all four of these dimensions. In these vulnerable areas, climate change, when its effects are felt, quite often is bound to lead to more heated disputes and conflicts over the control of the ever-declining resources, as in the widely studied cases of Lake Chad.19 The most popular and most widely studied one is the South-North direction, i.e. from the less developed to the more developed countries. This direction is associated with the large discrepancies emerging in the postwar years, first and foremost, in the income per capita levels, in the human development index, and in the general living conditions. Given the reasons why the people generally leave their homes, we could arrive at the conclusion that environmental migration is a broader concept, often associated with some other factors, such as the deteriorating economic conditions or the political instability. The share of migrants leaving their usual place of residence has been on the increase as a result of the gradual deterioration of the ecological, sanitary and epidemiological situation, the changes taking place in the climatic conditions, the habitats, etc. Conclusion Entering the 21st century, humanity finds itself faced up with a very important choice: to either continue along the path of a fierce race for resources that will inevitably lead to frequent outbreaks of violence in different parts of the world, or 18 King, Andrew and Luke J. Harrington, The Inequality of Climate Change From 1.5 to 2°C of Global Warming, May 29, 2018, Wiley, at: https://agupubs.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1029/2018GL078430 19 Bila, M.D. Stringer, L.C. Okpara, T.O. Dougill, A.J., Conflicts about water in Lake Chad: are environmental, vulnerability and security issues linked? Sustainability Research Institute, 2014. 132 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY to choose to cooperate in the allocation and the use of the world resources. The latter choice will certainly not be an easy one as an awful lot of actors – individuals, groups of individuals and entire regimes – are going to oppose the establishment of such a system of international cooperation. But the questions, which every politician, expert or scientist should ask himself, are the following: “Wouldn’t it be better to allocate resources fairly when needed?” Is it not in our interest to do everything possible through joint action and research to avoid shortages?” Each and every global mechanism of interaction is bound to have its own “leakages”. The question, however, is not whether this mechanism can be fully error-tolerant, but whether, despite its weaknesses in the long run, it will be more successful than the current system that relies primarily on the use of force to resolve the battles on resources. From this viewpoint, an interaction-based strategy is going to have very clear advantages. Although the use of force by one country could temporarily avoid a shortage of resources for its own use, it is only going to provoke hostility on the part of the opposite country and thus lead to further clashes. In addition, the difficult task of transporting water or oil from one area to another cannot be accomplished in a situation of constant violence as the risk of sabotage, accident, spillage or technical damage is really too great. On the other hand, the use of force leads inevitably to the waste of resources, which could be used much more beneficially for the benefit of entire society. As far as the civilization is concerned, the natural resources are the underlying ones and are, therefore, essential for our daily existence. The inhabitants of the planet Earth enjoy a huge supply of the most important and fundamental raw materials. Unfortunately, these resources are not used adequately today for which reason humanity runs the risk of being threatened by their rapid depletion. While, on the other hand, if we rely on a war to resolve our resource disputes, the material costs and the human sacrifices will be too great. Therefore, in order to avoid the gloomy predictions that increasingly appear in the specialized circles, we have to work today for the setting up of a global system for the discovery, the conservation and the management of the currently available natural resources. Within the framework of the summit held prior to the opening of the General Debate during the 75th regular session of the UN General Assembly (the UNGA) on 21 September, 2020, a declaration was adopted rendering an account of the United Nation’s central place within the system of international relations since World War II (WWII), as well as of the significance of the UN Charter as a corner- stone of international law. This Declaration emphasized the irreplaceability of the rules-based multilateralism, along with the need to renew and enhance this multi- lateralism with a view of effectively addressing today’s challenges.2 This topic also continued to be central during the 76th regular session of the UN General Assembly, held in September, 2021, as in his report the UN Secretary-General Antonio Gu- terres, entitled “Our Common Agenda” stated that humanity is in a fateful moment in history in which a great number of threats to the future (such as the COVID-19 pandemic, the climate crisis, the conflicts, the poverty and the lack of solidarity) could force us into choosing between adaptation and a breakthrough or a degra- dation. Six main lines of action had also been identified to address the amalgam of challenges, with effective multilateralism being one of them.3 The need to uphold and further strengthen the rules-based multilateralism is often highlighted in a number of political declarations and statements. An example of this are the recent contributions of the President of the European Commission (the EC) – Ursula von der Leyen, and the President of the European Council – Charles Michel, after their meetings with the UN Secretary-General – Antonio Guterres, held on June 23-24, 2021. They all reaffirmed their support for the UN in strengthening multilateralism and the rule of law4 and also stated that “a multilateral approach is the best way to address the global challenges“5. All the attention paid to the issue of multilateralism and the need for its consolidation has been conditioned by the fact that the well-established and rules- based multilateral system and its institutions are currently in the deepest crisis 1 UN General Assembly, A/RES/75/1 Declaration on the commemoration of the seventy-fifth anniversary of the United Nations, 2020, p. 2. 2 Ibid. 3 United Nations, Our Common Agenda. Report of Secretary General Antonio Guterres, 2021. 4 A joint statement made by the EC President Ursula von der Leyen and the UN Secretary-General Antonio Guterres, June 23, 2021. 5 President Charles Michel’s comment in relation to the European Council’s meeting having taken place on June 24-25, 2021. 134 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY since WWII.6 This crisis has been the result of a great number of intertwined and sometimes mutually reinforcing processes and factors. The main ones involve the overall shift in the balance of powers internationally resulting in the emergence of new centers of influence that claim a regional and a global role (such as China, Russia, Turkey, Brazil, India, etc.), as well as the rise of some non-liberal, nationalist and protectionist attitudes and forces, which are hostile to multilateralism in these very countries and are combined with the negative effects of the ever increasing use of aggressive militaristic rhetoric7. It should also be noted here that the key relations within the triangle among the US, Russia, and China have seldom been so dysfunctional as they are lacking in balanced policies and are rather based on reactionary actions that rest upon instincts and comfort zones.8 At the same time, the medium-sized forces have been increasingly resorting to autonomous action and using military force without any accountability to the international community. It is impossible to look at the situation in Syria, Yemen, Libya, and Nagorno-Karabakh without taking into account the role played by the regional forces. In addition, an enormous stress on the ability of the existing system has been exerted by a set of increasingly complex and pervasive challenges that face up all of humanity and recognize no boundaries. Just a couple of examples include the climate change and its detrimental impact on the environment; the economy and the security; the growing economic interdependence; the armed conflicts (e.g. in Ukraine, Syria, Libya, Yemen, the Gaza Strip, etc.); the waves of migrants and refugees; the technological development and the artificial intelligence; the ubiquitous poverty; and the ever widening gap between the rich and the poor worldwide (within and between the countries).9 The destructive effect of the recent behavior of the US retreat from the multilateral formats during Donald Trump’s administration has also had its special role to play. The State, which is at the center of and has contributed greatly to the consolidation of modern multilateralism round a UN center after WWII, has become a major opponent of this multilateralism while its behavior has become a serious threat to the well-established principles and rules. The withdrawal of the United States from a number of agreements and structures that exemplify the foundation of the rules-based cooperation and multilateral diplomacy, such as the Paris Climate Agreement, the Treaty on Open Skies10, the Joint Comprehen- 6 Maas, H. & Le Drian, J., Who if not us?, Joint article at the start of the Munich Security Conference, Süddeutsche Zeitung, 2019. 7 Philipps, L. & Braun, D.
