The pandemic caused by the SARS-CoV-2 virus has brought to the fore the deficits of the existing UN multilateral structures and even beyond them. In its initial stages, the pandemic led to an increase in the mistrust and selfishness between the countries and turned out to be to the detriment of cooperation. Borders were closed and the export of medicines was blocked. Some of the most severely affected countries, such as Italy and Spain, were left unable to cope without timely support. The inequality in the ability of the poor and the rich countries to secure for their health systems medical supplies and subsequently – vaccines, as well as the lack of common strategies to deal with the crisis also came to the fore.12 The COVID-19 pandemic was used for propaganda and mutual accusations in relation to the origin of the virus, as well as to the responsibility of limiting its further spread. Some of the major players on the international arena used the COVID-19 pandemic as a tool for hybrid and disinformation campaigns with a view of weakening and discrediting their opponents, as well as of pursuing geopolitical interests (e.g. Russia, China, etc.).13 Iinitiatives aimed at strengthening multilateralism In the context of this dynamism, instability, growing geopolitical multipolarity and cross-border challenges, a number of initiatives have been taken to strengthen the rules-based multilateralism having UN as its center. In the first place, we should mention here the German-French Alliance for Multilateralism Initiative. Launched in 2018 and further supported, in 2019, in a joint article by the German Foreign Min- ister – Heiko Maas, and the French Foreign Minister – Jean-Yves Le Drian, on the occasion of the Munich Security Conference, this initiative was constituted during the General Debate of the 74th regular session of the UN General Assembly. The Alliance for Multilateralism is an informal network of countries united by the conviction that the rules-based multilateralism is the only reliable guarantor of international stability and peace and that the common challenges can only be addressed through cooperation based on principles and rules, such as the rule of law, the human rights, democracy and freedom. The objective of this multilateralism is to stabilize the rules of international order and the UN as its center, to pay respect to its principles and, where necessary, to adapt it by reforms. The Alliance for Multilateralism has been working in 6 thematic organization and forum for decision-making in the field of security within the meaning of Chapter VII of the UN Charter. 11 The JCPOA, which is aimed at curbing Tehran‘s nuclear program, was not achieved within the UN, but was subsequently approved and complied on the basis of a UN Security Council Resolution S / RES / 2231 (2015), and the key role for compliance in the course of its implementation had been assigned to the International Atomic Energy Agency, which is part of the UN system. 12 Donati F., Rethinking Multilateralism in Times of Crisis, in Cadmus, 2020, volume 4, issue 3. 13 European External Action Service (EEAS), Special report on disinformation about the COVID-19 pandemic, 2020. 136 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY fields, which reflect the contemporary challenges (including the human rights, the international law/responsibilities, the disarmament and the arms control, the cyberspace, the global public goods, the climate, and the institution building).14 The objectives of the Alliance for Multilateralism are also shared by the European Union (the EU), where, as early as June 2019, the Council of the European Union adopted some conclusions in relation to strengthening multilateralism, which emphasize that the EU interests are tied to the multilateral system that stands firmly on rules. The following three guidelines for strengthening the multilateral system were outlined in these conclusions: 1) upholding the international norms and agreements; 2) adapting multilateralism to the new realities; and 3) reforming the multilateral organizations. Specific steps have been taken to achieve these objectives, including compliance with the Paris Climate Agreement, as well as some work to be done to uphold the human rights, the rule of law and democracy within all the relevant formats, including the UN General Assembly, the Security Council and the Human Rights Council, along with all other relevant UN agencies.15 On February 17, 2021, the European Commission, jointly with the EU High Representative for Foreign Affairs and Security Policy (the HR), came up with a Joint Communication entitled “Strengthening the EU contribution to the rules-based multilateralism”, as well as an EU agenda for „Renewed multilateralism, fit for the 21st century.” The EU has reaffirmed that the UN is at the heart of the multilateral system and draws at- tention to the need to make this multilateralism more effective and more inclusive.16 Nature and achievements of the multilateral system with a UN center Why is the crisis in the multilateral system a problem and what does the modern, rules-based multilateralism stand for? What are its achievements and its place within the mechanism of the international system? The answer to these questions is particularly important in the search for arguments for strengthening and adapting multilateralism, as well as in the analysis of different approaches aimed at undermining it. Although being at the heart of international life today, multilateralism is a concept difficult to grasp and explain in its entirety. This is due to its nature per se. In a functional sense, it represents diplomatic cooperation between individual states (multilateral diplomacy); while, in a theoretical sense, it is a concept on which the architecture of the modern international system rests. Last but not least, multilateralism is a dynamic historical process that cannot be detached from the context in which it manifests itself. Often defined as being in opposition to bilateralism (the bilateral approach) and unilateralism (the unilateral action), it presupposes, in the strict sense of the word, cooperation between at least three countries. This quantitative definition is not sufficient to explain it, as multilateralism also implies adherence to a common politically institutionalized project based on the observance of a system of values a nd norms. The norms 14 What is the “Alliance for multilateralism”? Official web-site of this initiative. 15 Council of the European Union. EU action to strengthen the rules-based multilateralism, Council conclusions, 2019. 16 European Commission, Joint communication to the EP and the Council entitled „Strengthening the EU contribution to the rules-based multilateralism, 2021. 137 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY or the rules determining the effective functioning of multilateralism ensure that all the participants have the same rights and obligations, constantly observed (rather than ad hoc, in relation to different issues or topics). Therefore, it can be concluded that multilateralism is both a method of cooperation and a form of organization of the international system.17 Before having reached its current evolutionary stage, namely a number of regional international organizations and institutions with established values and rules, with their own center – the multilateral framework of the United Nations, multilateralism has come a long way. From the Peace of Westphalia (1648),18 which marked the beginning of the modern international system of sovereign states, multilateralism has developed gradually through the Treaty of Versailles (1919), which established the League of Nations. The latter is considered to have marked the beginning of modern multilateralism19 and is a prototype of the United Nations. The League of Nations is a key phase in the development of multilateralism as it has provided a permanent forum and a framework for cooperation between the individual states, thus aiming to prevent any future conflicts.20 Multilateralism, however, had had to wait for the end of WWII in order to be able to reach its full potential.21 Thus, we get to the signing of the UN Charter on October 24, 1945, which marked the establishment of the United Nations. This universal international organization par excellence, whose founders were determined to turn it into a multilateral forum for cooperation, now embraces 193 member states, along with over 1000 NGOs and international organizations with an observer status.22 The UN Charter not only defines the structure of this organization but it is also one of the main pillars of the international system and, as such, could be defined as being the first major achievement of modern multilateralism with a UN center. The UN Charter sets the fundamental norms and rules of the multilateral system, thus making legally binding the principles of the sovereign equality of states, the peaceful settlement of conflicts, and the refusal to use military force that is at variance with its own provisions.23 Art. 1 stipulates the major UN objectives in the area of maintaining international peace and security, of observing the international law, of developing friendly relations between the nations, coupled with respect for the right to self-determination, as well as of cooperation for the purpose of solving the international economic, social and humanitarian problems by respecting, at the same time, the human rights and the fundamental rights and freedoms. In order to achieve all these objectives, the UN should act as a “coordination center“,24 which is why it could be said with confidence that “multilateralism is part and parcel of 17 UN Geneva (2020), 100 Years of multilateralism in Geneva – anniversary website. A tentative definition. 18 Ibid. Supra note 8, Chikvaidze, D.A., 2020, p. 128 19 Ibid. Supra note 17, UN Geneva, 2020, The origins of contemporary multilateralism. 20 Ibid., Supra note 12, Donati F., 2020, p. 54 21 According to Paoli F. (2020), the League of Nations did not manage to set up any effective multilateralism as a couple of key countries, e.g. the US, did not join it and a little later, following the upsurge of the right nationalism in the 1930s, it had not been in a position to ward off the outbreak of World War II. Chikvaidze D. A. (2020) has also attributed this failure to the then dysfunctional financial and monetary system. 22 Ibid. Supra note 17, UN Geneva (2020), The United Nations, the multilateral framework par excellence. 23 UN Charter (1945), arts. 2.1, 2.3, 2.4 https://www.un.org/en/about-us/un-charter/full-text. 24 Ibid, art. 1.4 138 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY this organization‘s DNA“.25 As since the establishment of the United Nations, there has been no global military conflict between the great powers. Upholding and codifying the international law with respect to varied aspects of the international relations have been important merits of the UN in the field of multilateralism and the formulation of its own norms. In this context, the establishment of the UN International Law Commission with a mandate to develop and codify this particular law (1947), along with the adoption of the conventions confirming and supplementing the customary law, were of particular importance for setting the preconditions for cooperation and peace. It is appropriate to mention here, in the first place, the 1961 Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, the 1963 Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, and the 1969 Vienna Convention on the Law of Treaties – all of these compiled with the assistance of the United Nations’ International Law Commission and within the framework of the UN special conferences. They all had a special role to play in setting up the framework of common rules for the establishment, the maintenance, and the termination of diplomatic and consular relations, as well as in the formulation of the rules, the procedures and the guidelines for the conclusion of international treaties. In this context, they regulate the way the international relations function today. An example of a thematic convention is the UN Convention on the Law of the Sea (UNCLOS), in force since 1994 and having 168 states as parties. This convention defines the rights and the responsibilities of the relevant states in relation to their territorial waters, exclusive economic zones, and the use of the world‘s oceans. An important, albeit non-legally binding document related to the international law is the UN Universal Declaration of Human Rights (the UDHR), adopted in 1958 on the basis of a UN General Assembly resolution. This declaration outlines the fundamental rights and freedoms and, subsequently, serves as a basis for the international treaties and regional instruments and mechanisms aimed at the protection of human rights26 (such as the European Convention on Human Rights (the ECHR)). The legal regime established by the Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons (the NPT), drawn up in the period 1956-1968 within the framework of an UN-sponsored organization in Geneva, is also of prime significance. As it bans the non-nuclear signatories from acquiring nuclear weapons and envisages the future co-operation for full nuclear disarmament.27 Some specialized agencies within the UN system, such as the International Labour Organization (the ILO), the International Atomic Energy Agency (the IAEA), the International Civil Aviation Organization (the ICAO), UNESCO and some others constitute the framework of institutions, rules and minimum multilateral cooperation standards in their specific areas of activity. The World Trade Organization (the WTO), although not being an element of the UN system28, cooperates with this system and sets standards in the field of international trade thus monitoring the observance of trade agreements. 25 Ibid. Supra note 17, UN Geneva (2020) 26 United Nations website. Protection of human rights under universal international law. 27 Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons (1970), UN Office for Disarmament Affairs 28 Although the WTO is not a specialized UN agency, it is an important element of the multilateral system and maintains close relations with the United Nations, including through the participation of the WTO Director-General in the Chief Executives Board (a coordinating body between the UN agencies, WTO, the IMF and the World Bank.) 139 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY Through the work of the World Health Organization (the WHO) and its standards, multilateralism within the UN has achieved a lot in the health care domain. Some of these achievements involve the eradication of smallpox (in 1980) through a coordinated campaign, the development of a vaccine and the coordination of efforts to combat Ebola, the development of drugs to control the HIV/AIDS epidemic, etc. In addition, in 2021, through a couple of its own agencies, the UN has protected the life and the health on a daily basis by providing humanitarian assistance to some 156 million people in need in more than 56 countries. The UN International Peacekeeping Force (the so-called “Blue Helmets”) have been assisting the states in transition from a conflict situation to peace. The 14 UN peacekeeping operations in this field have been a collective contribution to peace.29 A relatively recent achievement of multilateralism within the UN, and in the face of the new challenges, has been the Paris Agreement on Climate Change, signed in 2015 and in force since 2016. The aim of this Agreement has been to strengthen the global response to the climate change threats in the context of sustainable development and poverty eradication by sustaining the rising global temperatures as a result of reducing the greenhouse gas emissions. Alternative views and approaches to multilateralism In the context of the shifting balance of powers on a global scale, the emergence of some new centers of regional and global influence and of the new overarching challenges, it is worth considering here some key alternative views that challenge the already established rules-based multilateralism. Russia While, in the West, there have been increasing calls and initiatives for upholding the rules of the post-war international order and while the G7 Foreign Ministers have been accusing Russia of “irresponsible and destabilizing behavior“30 on the occasion of the annexation of Crimea, given the Russian deployment of troops along the Ukrainian border, its subversive cyber and disinformation campaigns against the democratic states, the disrespect for human rights, etc., it is easy to assume that Russia is going to welcome a new era of unilateralism and competition between the great powers, in which multilateralism is going to be pushed to the background. In real fact, however, the Russian leadership’s view of multilateralism is a bit more complex and multifaceted. As when it comes to a “rules-based” international order or multilateralism, Russia’s reaction is to usually ask the question “Who actually lays down the rules?” According to Russia, the United States are not going to hesitate and will act unilaterally whenever it is in their best interest, and this is a double standard undermining the global system of rules. Further on, when the Russian Foreign Minister – Sergey Lavrov, makes speeches, he often emphasizes the alleged violations of international law on the part of the US (e.g. the 1999 bombing of Yugoslavia, the 2003 invasion of Iraq, the coups that helped overthrow Gaddafi in 2011, etc.). 29 United Nations Peacekeeping, Our history, at: https://peacekeeping.un.org/en/our-history 30 Foreign Office, G7 Foreign and development ministers’ meeting, Communique, 2021. 140 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY Therefore, Russia sees the Western debate on the rules-based multilateralism as being hypocritical or even as a complex conspiracy to undermine the international law codified in the UN Charter.31 An example of this position is Lavrov‘s speech on the occasion of the UN Security Council meeting related to the defense of multilateralism and the international system with a UN center, held on May 7, 2021. In his speech, Mr. Lavrov accused the Western countries, the United States, and the EU of having tried to circumvent the UN system because of their inability to assert their interests via this system and, in addition, he defined the concept of a rules-based international order as an attempt to replace the international law. Lavrov also defined President Biden‘s proposed Summit on Democracy and the Alliance for Multilateralism as initiatives bound to impose the Western supremacy and, also, as a commitment to abide by the Western rules and values, which are not universally accepted within the UN. To crown it all, the actions on the part of the EU and the US were described by him as an attempt at “totalitarianism in the area of some global issues“.32 Since Russia itself advances such a view about the rules-based multilateralism and the actions of its rivals, it does not feel obliged to comply with the international law when the Russian national interests are at stake.33 Some examples of this, of course, include Russia’s recognition of two regions that had seceded from Georgia in 2008, the annexation of Crimea in 2014 (both cases being legitimized as “defensive measures”), the poisoning of Russian political opponents on a foreign sovereign territory, the detention of the Ukrainian warships, the violation of NATO’s airspace over Estonia, etc. As the Russian diplomat and director of the Centre for Comprehensive European and International Studies in Moscow – Timofey Bordachev, pointed out, when asked “what did Russia do in 2014?” – “Russia violated the US monopoly over the violations of international law“.34 Russia does not want to weaken the central role of the United Nations because, as a permanent UN Security Council member, the country has the right to a veto, along with some particular influence. Russia would like a world in which all countries (first and foremost, the United States) abide by the UN Charter, but since it claims that this is not the case, the country prefers to be rather pragmatic by embracing the image of a great power (an empire) and to elbow its way through the international system and set up a sphere of influence within its own environment. However, when a particular case concerns it directly, Russia could be unusually valuable and beneficial to multilateral cooperation. An example of this is Russia’s role as one of the main countries having negotiated the JCPOA on Iran. When, however, Russia has neither any direct interest nor significant experience, it does not invest much effort in multilateral cooperation. An example of this is this country’s particularly slow accession to the World Trade Organization (the WTO), which took 19 years to complete. As Russia has been 31 Gabuev A. & Chernenko E., What Russia thinks about multilateralism, Project Syndicate, 2019. 32 Lavrov S., Remarks on the meeting of the UN Security Council: Maintenance of international peace and security: Upholding multilateralism and UN-centred international system, MFA of the Russian Federation, 2021 33 Ibid supra note 29 Gabuev A. & Chernenko E. Yet another example includes the climate change negotiations, where, although involved, Russia was clearly not at the forefront of the debate. The Russian version of multilateralism is a version of pragmatic cooperation whenever such cooperation is to its own benefit. For Russia, multilateralism is not so much a system of principles and rules but it is rather a strategy to serve the Russian national interests.35 As far as Russia is concerned, multilateralism should not be tied to any particular ideology or any values because, according to the country’s leadership, “there is no room for idealized positions or messianism in multilateralism.“36 China Theoretically, China also stands in support of the UN-centered multilateralism. In his first address delivered before the UN General Assembly in September, 2015, President Xi Jinping stated: “We should be committed to multilateralism and deny unilateralism.“37 In addition, during the Security Council meeting of May 7, 2021, on the defense of multilateralism, the Chinese Foreign Minister – Wang Yi, reaffirmed his country’s support for the principles of the UN Charter, for the authority and the central role of the UN in international relations.38 Despite all these claims, however, the EU has officially identified China, since 2019, as a systemic rival promoting alternative governance models. In a joint letter of April 21, 2021, from Ursula von der Layen and the EU High Representative Joseph Borrell, addressed to the European Council, there was some talk about the „authoritarian bias of this country“, as well as about the fundamental differences between the EU and China in terms of economy, the governance of globalization, the democracy, the human rights, and the approach to third countries. In addition, the EU imposed some sanctions on a couple of officials linked to the repression against the Uighur minority in China and China responded with sanctions against some MEPs and EU ambassadors.39 China was also criticized by the G7 countries during their latest meeting in London for human rights violations, for undermining the democratic elements in Hong Kong‘s electoral system, for the transparency of investment, the intellectual property theft, the disregard for the UN Convention on the Law of the Sea, and in relation to the rulings of the Permanent Court of Arbitration ruling on the South China Sea dispute.40 The Chinese speeches, even when relying on the UN Charter, are not always indicative of a rules-based order or multilateralism. Their actions on the ground – whether in the South China Sea or in Hong Kong, are in conflict with the statements they have made. 35 Maxine D., Whose multilateralism? There has also been some suspicion that China has been stepping up its influence in order to transform the UN operations in a way to serve its own interests. An example of such an action has been the inclusion of the Belt and Road Initiative (the B&R), seen by many as geopolitical, under the UN Sustainable Development Goals. This happened by silencing the criticism of the human rights violations, by providing financial incentives to some Member States, and by introducing an ever increasing number of Chinese representatives into the system.41 An example of China‘s tactics to try and “buy” influence at the UN has been the displacement of the Cameroonian candidate for a Director-General position of the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) in 2019, who withdrew conveniently his candidacy shortly after China had forgiven a $ 78 million debt of the Cameroonian government.42 China has also been exerting influence within the UN through the so-called “Group of 77” (the G77), which has set up a coalition with China called the „Group of 77 and China“ in order to boost its own negotiating and political weight. In this way, unlike any other UN member, China has the power of a veto within the UN Security Council, as well as significant influence over a great number of the G77 members, which account for 70 % of the UN member countries.43 So, through these developing countries, China is also said to have been influencing the decisions made at the HRC, by bringing together around itself authoritarian governments or governments with an own history of human rights abuses. The latter often block the decisions in defense of China’s Uighur minority and the rights of Hong Kong and Tibet residents, which justifies China’s repressive actions under the pretext of “fighting extremism, radicalism and terrorism“.44 In general, the country has been trying to impose its own terminology within the HRC, aiming in this way to change the very definition of human rights by shifting the focus from the political and the civil rights and putting the economic development, the security and the vague notion of “happiness” in the first place. In his speech, given at a HRC meeting on February 22, 2021, the Chinese Foreign Minister – Wang Yi, when enumerating the human rights, used the following wording: “peace, development, justice, democracy and freedom.” “Freedom” and “democracy” were pushed to the bottom, while peace, security, and economic development were quite eloquently put first, as ensuring order, peace, security and economic development in the first place and at any cost shifts the focus from the damages done to other rights.45 In his speech of May 8, 2021, pronounced within the framework of the UN Security Council meeting on the defense of multilateralism, Wang Yi also said that by respecting the diversity of each and every country, its history and culture, the 41 Cheng-Chia T. & Yang A., How China is remaking the UN in its own image, The Diplomat, 2020. 42 Ibid. 43 Heijmans M & van der Puten F., A United Nations with Chinese characteristics? Clingendael institute report., 2018, p. 2 44 Huang J., UN Human rights council divided over China’s Xinjiang policy, Voa news, 2019. 45 Tiezzi S., Can China change the definition of human rights?. The Diplomat, 2021. 143 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY world should not be divided along “any ideological lines, because this is contrary to the spirit of multilateralism.“46 Quite similarly to the Russian pragmatic view, China, on one hand, does not want to link multilateralism to any ideologies or values imposed on it from the outside while, on the other hand, it states that it supports the UN (but does not always follow the rules set within its framework) and, at the same time, works actively with a view of infiltrating it and remodeling its terminology in compliance with its own image and in its own interest. Given the Chinese Communist Party’s obsession with gaining control, it could be considered that, internationally, China has not been seeking an order based on the rule of law, or on the rules that could be used to criticize it from outside, but it has been seeking an order based on the rule of law by means of the law and the relevant rules over the formation of which it can exercise control. In this context, the Chinese alternative to multilateralism is somewhat reminiscent of Donald Trump’s alternative facts, which are not actual facts; and multilateralism, which is not truly based on constant values a nd norms (but is rather exerting some influence within the multilateral system, creating dependencies and introducing new terminology with a view of laying down the rules to serve the Chinese interests).47 The concept of “contested multilateralism” Proceeding from the tactics of both Russia and China, we cannot say that their views about an alternative to the rules-based multilateralism rest upon any bilateral or unilateral approaches. Yes, they both use similar tactics when they consider it fit, but they also use multilateral approaches to enforce their multilateralism, not so much based on common rules and values as on pragmatism and own interests. The so-called concept of “contested multilateralism” may turn out to be very useful here48, according to which the players on the international arena, who are dissatisfied with the status quo, which does not sufficiently serve their own interests and views, all too often resort to using the well-established multilateral formats or bring about new competitors for the purpose of mobilizing support and legitimizing their alternative vision of the status quo. Both Russia and China have been using the well-established UN multilateral framework in order to advance their views and interests there. On one hand, Russia – through its influence as a permanent UN Security Council member with the right to a veto, as well as through a firm position of its own that any initiatives to strengthen the rules-based multilateralism outside the UN have been attempting to replace the international law. For its part, China has been making use of the well-established system by strengthening its presence within the UN structures, by promoting helpful terminology and setting up coalitions with some other countries with similar views and ideologies. Both countries have been setting up parallel multilateral formats through which to exercise their influence. Russia, for example, is a kind of a leader of the Eurasian Economic Union (the EAEU), which is somewhat parallel to the 46 Ibid. supra note 38. Yi W. (2021) 47 Maull H. W., China’s alternative multilateralism, Mercator institute of China studies, 2019. 48 Morse J. C. & Keohane R. O., Contested multilateralism, Review of international organizations, issue 9, 2014. 144 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY European Union. The EAEU has been aiming to preserve in Russia‘s sphere of influence the countries of the former Soviet Union through economic integration but, unlike the EU, it does not link this integration to the rule of law or the human rights principles.49 China has also been building up multilateral cooperation formats, such as the Belt and Road Initiative, which is on paper a project for economic cohesion and development, but while bearing in mind the way it works (on the basis of a core and an dependent periphery), it creates for its partners economic dependence and ultimately increases the Chinese influence.50 Some other multilateral formats through which China could exert its influence include the “16+1”, the Asian Development Bank and the Forum on China-Africa Cooperation (a noteworthy fact here is that the latest summit of this forum, held in 2018, had been attended by more African Heads of State than the UN General Assembly).51 Arguments in favor of the rules-based multilateralism related to the future As the future is going to face up the world with a set of increasingly complex and overarching challenges beyond the capabilities of the individual states, the rules-based multilateralism is not just the best, but perhaps the only alternative to tackling the said challenges. Therefore, it is necessary to agree on some common principles and norms capable of framing the behavior of the individual states (and today, of the corporations, too) in a number of areas. Without any claims to be exhaustive, the section that follows is going to label only a couple of them. The climate change First comes the climate change, which could be defined as the key challenge of our time. No place on earth is protected against the effects of climate change. The rising temperatures as a result of human activity and the greenhouse gas emissions are leading to environmental degradation, natural disasters and extreme weather events, coupled with food and water insecurity, economic stagnation, conflicts and terrorism. The polar ice has been melting and the ocean levels are rising, which constitutes an existential threat to the existence of the group of the so-called Small Island Developing States (SIDS). The ocean reefs are dying, the rainforests are being cut down, some of them have been burning at an unprecedented rate, and the loss of biodiversity is enormous.52 What we need in order to be able to cope with all that involves only multilateral action based on common rules and objectives as the only way to avoid reaching the so-called “irreversible point” in the climate crisis, after which no action is going to suffice to ward off the negative consequences. The Paris Climate Agreement and cooperation within the framework of the UN Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change constitute the genuine basis for tackling this problem, however, a lot of work has still to be done in order to attain the relevant goals and ward off the catastrophe. The European Union has declared climate neutrality by the year 49 London School of Economics, The geopolitics of Eurasian economic integration. Special report, 2014. 50 Pal D., Multilateralism with Chinese characteristics, The Diplomat, 2020. 51 Dahir A., Twice as many African presidents made it to China’s Africa summit than to the UN general assembly, Quartz Africa, 2018. 52 UN at 75, The climate crisis – a race we can. UN official website, 2020. 145 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY 2050; under the Biden administration, the United States have promised a 50 % reduction in the emissions by 2030; China has announced neutrality by the year 2060, while the fastest-growing continent, Africa, has not stated yet any such targets, with the exception of South Africa.53 New fronts of conflict Yet another challenge calling for a rules-based multilateralism has to do with the development of new technologies and the artificial intelligence in the military domain. The Lethal Autonomous Weapons Systems (the LAWS) have already become part of the arsenal of an increasing number of countries in the form of drones. The International Committee of the Red Cross has defined these as autonomous weapons in terms of their key functions related to the targeting and attacking the target itself, on the basis of algorithms.54 The major problem with these systems of weapons, however, boils down to the fact that the development and the use of such weapons precede by far their regulation (as they are not covered by the Geneva Conventions that codifies the international humanitarian law). On the other hand, such regulation is very problematic because it is not clear how an autonomous weapon could abide by the principles of humanity and distinguish between civilian and military objectives, let alone the proportionality. The Alliance for Multilateralism has been actively working on the development of a relevant regulatory framework by having engaged for this purpose the group of experts working for the UN Convention on Certain Conventional Weapons, which group has already promoted, via a declaration, 11 principles related to the Autonomous Weapons Systems. The most important among these are the ones that should be also applied to these weapons by the international humanitarian law and, also, that any such regulation should take into consideration that the responsibility for the use of such weapons has to always be imputable to the individual, rather than to the relevant machine.55 Strategic resources and strategic space Last but not least, the future has been posing some challenges as to how the strategic resources and strategic spaces of our planet are going to be used. These include the strategic resources including the minerals and even the drinking water, which have already led to the conflict between Egypt, Ethiopia, and Sudan, on one hand, and some spaces or areas, such as Antarctica, the ocean floor, or the orbit of the Earth. As the latter are accommodating some satellite systems on which the economies, the banking operations, the communications, and the control of a large number of the countries’ defense systems depend. Irrespective of the fact that the use of mass destruction weapons in outer space is prohibited and regulated by the so-called Outer Space Treaty, signed in 1967, the use of conventional weapons, which include the increasingly problematic anti-satellite weapons, has not yet been regulated. 53 Goering L., Africa shrugs off net-zero emissions push without finance to follow. Reuters, 2020. 54 ICRC, Autonomous weapons: Decisions to kill and destroy are a human responsibility. Statement, 2016. 55 Alliance for multilateralism website. Action areas: 11 principles on Lethal Autonomous Weapons. 146 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY Significance for Bulgaria and conclusion In an environment of growing multipolarity, challenges and attempts on the part of some major powers to unbalance or change the international system in their favor, the starting point for any small country should be multilateralism – both in a regional, as well as in a global context. What we need, however, is not just “any” multilateralism, but a multifaceted approach based on rules, which are the same for everyone and at all times. In this context and, bearing in mind the fact that the country is part of the EU, which is the major regional organization defending multilateralism with a UN center, Bulgaria should work for the purpose of implementing the current rules and of codifying the ones that will be essential in addressing all the future challenges. The country has been member of the Alliance for Multilateralism since September 26, 2019, when the Bulgarian Foreign Minister – Ms. Ekaterina Zaharieva, announced Bulgaria’s accession to this Alliance’s 6 initiatives. Bulgaria has been contributing to the attainment of the goals of this Alliance in numerous formats and forums, including within the framework of the Bulgarian Foreign Ministry’s 2020 campaign in support of multilateralism and engaging the Bulgarian public under the title “75 years of UN and 65 years of Bulgaria within the UN”. This country has also been working on this Alliance’s efforts to strengthen the WHO in the context of the COVID-19 pandemic, as, in August 2020, it joined a special declaration entitled “Strengthening the Multilateral Health Architecture”. We should also be active in the future by cooperating strategically with all our allies with a view of further strengthening and adapting multilateralism by educating, at the same time, society about the benefits, which this multilateralism could bring to the country. Nikolay Karakolev holds a Master’s degree in Law and Politics of International Security from the Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam. He joined the EU Common Foreign and Security Policy Direc- torate at the Bulgarian Ministry of Foreign Affairs in 2019. Mr. Karakolev has attended the XVIth “Stoyan Petrov-Chomakov” Diplomacy Course for Trainee-Attachés, organized by the Bulgarian Diplomatic Institute at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in 2021. He currently works at the Foreign Economic Relations Directorate of the Ministry. The consultant of this political report is Ivo Mu- skurov, Head of the UN Political Affairs Department of the UN and Development Cooperation Directorate at the MFA. 147 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY The role of the OSCE in regional security and the settlement of ongoing conflicts Silvia Boyanova Introduction The modern world is faced up with a variety of global challenges including terrorism, cross-border crime, migration, nuclear proliferation, climate change, human rights abuses, and deepening of geopolitical rivalry. In varied regions, e.g. in Eastern Europe, the Caucasus and the Middle East, a number of unresolved conflicts have been generating a significant potential for destabilization and call into question the security architecture and the relevant security guaranteeing mechanisms. The increasingly growing number of transnational and regional threats, along with the ever dynamically changing international environment, has been determining the need for active cooperation between the individual states and international organizations for the formulation of effective policies. The Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe (the OSCE) has, for a long time, been making a contribution to the deepening of multilateralism in a wide range of areas by searching adequate ways to resolve the problems. Despite some difficulties and shortcomings in its operations and in its toolbox, the OSCE has remained one of the key security organizations as it has the development potential that depends directly on the commitment and the activity of its Member States. The OSCE nature and structure The OSCE is the largest intergovernmental regional security organization, which consists currently of 57 countries located on three continents1 (Europe, Asia and North America). All participating countries have equal status and the decisions – political in nature – are always made by consensus. Indicative of the OSCE is the term “participating states”, used in relation to its Member States, which reflects a direct commitment to intergovernmental cooperation, as well as the voluntary and somewhat informal principle of participation. On one hand, this means that there is some flexibility in the Organization, including the ability to negotiate and seek consensus between opposing interests – one of the most important features of the OSCE; while on the other – it is also a prerequisite for making much more difficult the decision-making process. This original principle has an impact on the OSCE effectiveness and should, therefore, be taken into consideration when analyzing the prospects for its improvement. As a soft power organization2, the OSCE has been working to ensure that its mechanisms and procedures are used by the participating countries to a maximum in order to achieve stability and security in the region. In the course 1 Geographically, however, it covers most of the Northern Hemisphere – something that can be seen on the OSCE slogan reading “From Vancouver to Vladivostok“. 2 The OSCE was established at the Conference on Security and Co-operation in Europe, held in 1975 in Helsinki. 148 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY of its development, the OSCE has managed to build up a significant institutional base through which it has established itself as a mediator – something that has been further enhancing and facilitating the dialogue and serving as a basis for meeting the common challenges. The structural formation of this organization was carried out gradually along three lines including the decision-making bodies3, the executive units4 and the network of field operations (missions). The latter are aimed at assisting the host countries in different aspects and cover the regions of South-Eastern and Eastern Europe, the Southern Caucasus and Central Asia. The development of the OSCE and thе organization’s institutionalization have been the direct result of the new international order in the wake of the Cold War period, which helped emerge the need to regulate the relations and to launch a dialogue between the East and the West. The OSCE contribution along these lines has really been significant, not underestimating its major significance in relation to the approach to security. The OSCE security concept The OSCE is based on the fundamental concept of security as a comprehensive area, which calls for a comprehensive approach 5 and is made up of three major dimensions (the so called “three baskets“): the political and military dimension; economy and the environment; human rights. Today, these three interrelated aspects continue to be at the core of the OSCE, as evidenced by the focus of Sweden’s 2021 program (holding the Chairmanship of the OSCE in 2021) on protecting European security by improving its comprehensive approach and its role in resolving the ongoing conflicts6. The first “basket” includes activities related to arms control, border management, counter-terrorism, police operations, conflict prevention and resolution, along with reform and security cooperation. The second dimension covers actions and initiatives for the sustainable use of natural resources, water management, safe disposal of toxic and radioactive waste; and in the economic domain – helping the participating countries to improve the business climate, as well as to fight corruption and money laundering. The third “basket” is related to the human rights activities, e.g. tolerance and anti-discrimination activities, minority rights, media freedom, fair and democratic elections, and the rule of law. In the OSCE founding document, i.e. the Helsinki Final Act, the protection of human rights and freedoms was included for the first time as an integral element of a regional security framework, along with the political, military and economic issues. 3 The key ones include the OSCE summits, the Ministerial Council, the Permanent Council, and the Forum for Security Cooperation. 4 Some of which include the Secretariat, the Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights, the High Commissioner on National Minorities, and the Office of the OSCE Representative on Freedom of the Media. 5 This understanding shaped up at the initial stage of the OSCE development, which – until the year 1995, used to exist as a Conference on Security and Co-operation in Europe. A number of experts interpreted as revolutionary the much wider understanding of security that was adopted via the 1975 Helsinki Final Act. 6 Programme of the Swedish OSCE Chairpersonship, https://www.osce.org/files/f/documents/2/f/476278_2. pdf 149 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY As far as the conflicts are concerned, the OSCE has taken up once again a comprehensive approach by adopting a couple of toolkits designed for each and every stage of the potential development of a conflict or a crisis7, including, at the same time, prevention in respect of which the OSCE has early warning mechanisms and timely measures. While with regard to the conflicts that have already erupted, the OSCE has been working to address the situation and to limit the escalation of tension and violence in terms of intensity and territorial distribution, thus facilitating a peaceful final solution. The third aspect involves post-conflict rehabilitation and the establishment of peace and stability. The OSCE approach described above indicates a capacity to work on a wide range of risk-involving conditions. All this is crucial in the context of today’s rapidly changing security environment, also due to the fact that a great number of conflicts do not develop linearly but rather fluctuate between periods of latent and acute crises. Among the fundamental OSCE principles is the peaceful resolution of conflicts and crises through mediation and dialogue on the community, the interstate or the regional level. While in view of the ongoing conflicts, the institutionalized and systematic support for a peaceful solution is vital. It should be noted here, however, that the conflict prevention and the escalation of tension preventing tools generate a potential for action that can be used to varying degrees. The implementation of this potential depends, first and foremost, on the preparedness of the participating countries to use the available mechanisms proactively, as well as to further develop and enrich them. At the same time, crisis management should be rather the exception while prevention should be the rule. Arms control is among the most important aspects of the establishment of preventive measures, in which the OSCE has been having a leading role. Arms control There are three major OSCE politico-military arms control regulating instruments including the 1990 Treaty on Conventional Armed Forces in Europe (CFE), the 2002 Treaty on Open Skies, and the 1990 Vienna Document (last updated in 2011 in compliance with the Confidence and Security-Building Measures – CSBMs)8. The 1990 Treaty on Conventional Armed Forces in Europe imposes quantitative restrictions on five conventional weapons categories9 and regulates the equal limits regarding the combat equipment designed for each one of the two blocks. Regional restrictions and unprecedented verification mechanisms have been agreed upon including the exchange of information between the contracting parties, along with mutual inspections. A major achievement of the implementation of the 1990 Treaty over the years has been the large-scale reduction of the conventional weapons in Europe. However, the implementation of this agreement was hampered by the Russian Federation in 2007 as it 7 The year 2021 has marked the 10th anniversary of the decision made by the OSCE Ministerial Council on the elements of the so-called conflict cycle. 8 That was the first international agreement introducing a Confidence and Security-Building Measures system in the military affairs area. 9 Battle tanks, armored vehicles, artillery systems with a caliber of 100 mm and more, combat aircraft and attack helicopters. 150 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY suspended its obligations to provide information and notifications, as well as to carry out inspections, suspending at the same time, in 2015, its participation in the CFE Joint Consultative Group (JCG), which provided an opportunity to discuss and resolve problems in the area of its application. The Vienna Document, on the other hand, was a political agreement containing politically binding CSBMs aimed at increasing the transparency of the military activities carried out within the territory, the surrounding sea areas, and the airspace of all OSCE member States10. In 2016, the updating of the Vienna Document was hampered again by Russia as it refused to accept an updated version of this document and to participate in any modernization, which largely blocked its further development11. Prior to 2011, this document had been amended a couple of times, which was indicative of the need for it to be open for constant updating in view of the dynamics and the modern requirements of the security environment. The third agreement, the Treaty on Open Skies, is a mechanism for establishing a regime for inspection flights over the territories of the participating countries. Currently, this agreement has been largely weakened as the United States withdrew from it in November 2020, and, in December 2021, Russia also officially withdrew. The withdrawal of both Russia and the United States from the Treaty on Open Skies has called into question the further existence of this agreement and has been another step in the direction of compromising the global security architecture. The ongoing development of arms control is indicative of a worrying trend in the declining potential of security cooperation. The European arms control system, developed in the wake of the Cold War period, has been collapsing and the strategic rivalry between the US and Russia continues to threaten the stability of the entire continent. In recent years, the above-mentioned three arms control-regulating documents have not only been seriously compromised but they also need to be modernized in line with the new realities, which, however, is not possible without a consensus. With Russia’s withdrawal from the Treaty on Open Skies, the OSCE is going to lose effectively all the three pillars of the conventional arms control regime. This could generate, in turn, a momentum for the creation of a new arms control regime, which will be possible only in the presence of a sufficiently active political will in the long run. Field missions (operations) Among the OSCE key instruments in the area of conflict and crisis management are the field missions, which are currently 16 in number and are located in South-Eastern and Eastern Europe, the Southern Caucasus and Central Asia (the missions in Bosnia and Herzegovina, Montenegro, Serbia, Kosovo, the Republic of Northern Macedonia, Moldova; a special mission and a coordinator in Ukraine, an observer mission at 2 border checkpoints along the 10 There is a mechanism in place for the exchange of information on the military forces, the armaments and the equipment, as well as their location, budget and supplies, along with a consultation mechanism through notifications of unusual military action. 11 In April 2021, Ukraine activated the Vienna Document by sending a formal request to Russia, in which it demanded an explanation of the increases in the Russian troops in the border areas and in the occupied territories. Russia refused to explain. 151 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY Russian-Ukrainian border, a personal representative of the Chairperson-in-Office of the Minsk Conference Organization; and, in addition, the OSCE is present in Albania, Uzbekistan, Kazakhstan, Turkmenistan, Tajikistan, and Kyrgyzstan). Such missions shall be established following the agreement of the Host Party and shall assist this party in its commitments to the OSCE and in the development of various projects of its own interest.12 One of the most important goals of such missions as a security tool is the early warning and the prevention of conflicts. As, on the field, the experts monitor and report on the development of the situation. In this sense, such missions are essential in obtaining timely information and an objective assessment. The Special Monitoring Mission in Ukraine has been the major challenge for the OSCE since the Kosovo mission in 1998 and it can also be considered a good example. Initially, this mission began as a preventive introduction to help deal with the deteriorating security situation in this country, but quickly turned into a full-fledged crisis management operation – a model for a comprehensive peacekeeping mission, relevant to the present day. The protracted conflicts and the OSCE role in their regulation The protracted conflicts in the OSCE region are among the major destabilizing factors threatening the sustainability of European security. The OSCE is committed to manage these conflicts for which purpose it has a set of mechanisms to support the mediation and the dialogue. Within the post-Soviet space, the OSCE is actively involved in a couple of key negotiation formats, such as the “5+2” in relation to the Transnistrian conflict, the Minsk Group on the Nagorno-Karabakh conflict, the Geneva International Discussions on Georgia, and the Trilateral Contact Group on Ukraine. In addition, the OSCE has its own missions within the conflict zones through which it monitors, helps find peaceful solutions and assists in limiting the negative impacts on the citizens’ lives, on their rights and freedoms. In the Transnistrian conflict, the OSCE has been having a key coordinating role in the process of finding a solution through a comprehensive approach, based on Moldova’s sovereignty and territorial integrity within its internationally recognized borders and the special status of Transnistria. The OSCE has been involved in these negotiations since its inception, when it became a mediator with Ukraine and Russia and the launch of the 5+2 format in 2005.13 Despite all the mediating efforts, this negotiation format has been directly affected by the differences between the parties to the conflict. The disagreements escalated after the Kozak Memorandum14, which was rejected by Moldova after some pressure exerted on the part of the OSCE. Later on, the OSCE assumed the leading role in the 2011 negotiations (following a 6-year break), and there was a breakthrough in the years 2016-2017, when the approach was already oriented towards formulating 12 These are all initiatives in support of law enforcement, of the minority rights and non-discrimination, of the legislative reform, the rule of law and the media freedom. 13 This format includes Moldova, Transnistria, the OSCE, Russia and Ukraine, as well as the EU and the US as observers. 14 Memorandum on the federalization of Moldova, a Russian initiative. 152 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY small steps in the direction of deepening the cooperation15. In addition, the OSCE Mission to Transnistria has been collecting data on what has been happening on the ground and has been informing the participating countries, including Moldova itself; this mission has also been setting the grounds for negotiations between Chisinau and Tiraspol, including through some expert working groups, which have already been set up for the purpose and meet regularly. This mission focuses on protecting the citizens’ rights by providing support and expertise16. The OSCE has been a mediator in the Nagorno-Karabakh conflict since 1992, when the Minsk Group was set up17 with the help of France, the US, and Russia as co-chairs, with the main goal of achieving a comprehensive and sustainable solution. Despite many years of efforts and the search for different solutions, the progress between Armenia and Azerbaijan remains a serious challenge, including due to the strong external factors and the possibility that a new escalation cannot be ruled out. In addition to the Minsk Process, the OSCE is also involved in the conflict through the personal representative of the OSCE Chairperson-in- Office; this representative and his team have been working towards mediation by implementing confidence-building measures and preparing the conditions for sending an OSCE mission to the field. The situation, however, remains volatile. With regard to Georgia, after the war with Russia and the recognition of the independence of Abkhazia and South Ossetia by the latter, a format called Geneva International Discussions was set up under the auspices of the EU, the OSCE and the UN, bringing together representatives of the conflicting countries18 and being the only platform for conversations. This format has been working in two directions, namely, one working group is engaged with the issues of security and stability within the separatist territories, while the second one has been dealing with some humanitarian issues, such as the return of refugees. The talks continue, largely owing to the international organizations involved but no agreement has been reached yet. The OSCE involvement in the Ukrainian conflict has been realized on a couple of levels, i.e. a special monitoring mission, a special coordinator, a Trilateral Group format19 and a mission at two border check points. In March 2014, the OSCE sent a special civilian monitoring mission immediately after the annexation of Crimea. This mission had been organized upon the request of Ukraine to monitor and report on the situation on the ground, to help reduce the tension, to promote stability and security, to support the implementation of the OSCE principles and to promote respect for human rights and freedoms. An important aspect in this case has been the establishment of contacts and the exchange of experience with the local, the regional and the national authorities, as well as with the civil society. The Trilateral Contact Group on Ukraine has been the official mechanism for seeking a diplomatic solution to the problem through the implementation of 15 The so-called “Berlin Plus” package, including 8 measures to build trust and improve the living conditions of the citizens on both sides of the Dniester River. 16 This mission has been helping the authorities to improve the access to justice, the non-discrimination, the free movement, the right to education, the fight against trafficking in human beings, etc. 17 This format has been the negotiating body set up by the OSCE and operating under its auspices. 18 From 2008 until today, the parties in this format have met more than 50 times. 19 In the period 2015-2018, more than 40 armistices were agreed upon. 153 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY the Minsk ceasefire agreements20. Participants in this format have been Russia, Ukraine and the OSCE, and the work has been divided again among a couple of working groups: on security, on economics, and on the humanitarian and political issues. In order to contribute as much as possible to resolving the conflict and limiting the negative effects on the population, the OSCE has also positioned a special coordinator in Ukraine, i.e. a representative of the OSCE Chairperson-in- Office for this particular country, and organized a fact-finding mission at Russia’s Gukovo and Donetsk border checkpoints. However, despite all the efforts, a solution to this conflict has been difficult to achieve, mostly due to the conflicting interests of the individual parties. However, it should be noted here that the OSCE mission has no monitoring functions for which reason it cannot “force” the parties to the conflict to put an end to the clashes. It does not conduct any investigations, as it rather collects on daily basis detailed information in the field and reports objectively on what has been going on. This mission also supports the restoration of the public infrastructure including the power lines, the bridges and the water filtration stations. It also monitors the implementation of Ukraine’s National Action Plan under the UN Security Council Resolution 1325 on Women, Peace and Security. The mission has also set up a couple of patrol bases within the territories controlled by both the government and the separatist authorities. In addition, the OSCE Coordinator in Ukraine is involved in the consolidation of society; he takes part in the dialogue promotion projects and organizes training for the mediators in this country. There have also been in Ukraine some efforts for the development of a system assisting the people who have been affected by the conflict and need both psychological, as well as socio-economic adaptation. There have been organized, too, trainings for the journalists; the discussions are encouraged and the research is being supported. The OSCE has also been of help in marking the mined areas (in the Donetsk and the Lugansk region)21. Another OSCE initiative is related to the settlement of the ongoing conflicts, i.e. the so-called Structured Dialogue (SD) on the security challenges within the OSCE22. The aim of this dialogue is to hold a forum on the exchange of views; to achieve a better understanding between the parties on the issues in the politico-military sphere; as well as to overcome any misunderstanding and outline common action23. The subject matter usually focuses on the varied security issues, including the military doctrines, the ongoing conflicts within the OSCE; the arms control issues, the greater transparency of the military exercises, the cyber threats, the risk of military incidents, and the movement and deployment of military personnel. On the Bulgarian side, the meetings are usually attended 20 Thanks to the mediation of the OSCE, France and Germany, a Protocol on the results of the consultations of the Trilateral Contact Group (the Minsk Protocol) and a Memorandum outlining the parameters for fulfilling the obligations under it were signed in September 2014. In addition, a package of measures for the implementation of the Minsk agreements has also been adopted. 21 The OSCE has been developing a multi-level approach to resolve the Ukrainian conflict – from interstate negotiations to exchanging good practices with the local authorities. 22 This format was launched in late 2016. Within it, the participating countries have recognized the importance of conventional arms control and the CSBMs. 23 The talks are organized in a way that engages the parties in a constructive dialogue, sharing opinions, positions and assessments while avoiding confrontation. 154 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY by representatives of the Bulgarian Permanent Representation in Vienna, as well as by seconded experts from the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the Ministry of Defense – depending on the topics of the individual meetings. However, the opposition between the individual countries and interests is reproduced directly in the work within the Structured Dialogue and hinders any progress on the individual topics. Notwithstanding the fact that the OSCE has a network of mechanisms for action in limiting the individual conflicts, none of the conflict situations in the post-Soviet space listed above has been resolved thus far. On one hand, this indicates a lack of sufficiently effective mechanisms and a certain inability on the part of the international organizations to exert the necessary influence to reach a solution. On the other hand, however, the key factor for resolving the ongoing conflicts has been the conflict of interests and the political will of the parties directly involved. The latter has been a genuine challenge for the OSCE, as the organization has tools, which can only be used in the presence of a desire to reach a consensus. The opposition between the West and the Russian Federation has been having a significant impact on the conflicts described above. There has been a trend in the Russian foreign policy to set up a buffer zone between itself and the West – from the Baltic countries, through Belarus and Ukraine – up to Central Asia. That territory is usually perceived as a sort of a security corridor. The conflict points in Nagorno-Karabakh, South Ossetia, Abkhazia, Transnistria, Crimea and Donbas are still under the direct influence of Russia, which is in a position to generate, at any time, destabilizing activities according to its own geostrategic interests thus preventing these areas from going beyond the Russian sphere of influence. In addition, the dynamic international processes have been increasingly shifting the Black Sea region (a kind of border between the Euro-Atlantic and the Russian security systems) from the periphery to one of the centers of geopolitical interests of some key international factors, such as Russia, Turkey, the United States, the EU and NATO.24 The annexation of Crimea in March 2014 changed drastically the security and the military balance in the Black Sea region. In an unregulated environment as this one, Russia quite purposefully continues to pursue a dynamic policy that has a direct impact on the region and increases the risk of further deepening the confrontation. The above trends indicate a lack of effectiveness on the part of the OSCE with regard to the security deterrent, as well as some sort of inability to involve the relevant stakeholders in seeking a consensus in a peaceful manner. The OSCE partnerships with neighboring countries and international organizations The understanding that security within the OSCE region is directly related to the security of the neighboring regions and that it can be developed based on a dialogue and of sharing good practices has also been fully in line with 24 The Black Sea delimitation began in 1987 between the USSR and Turkey, then between Bulgaria and Turkey. The problem today is that, following the collapse of the USSR, there has been no agreement between the three new states – Russia, Georgia and Ukraine on the maritime spaces, i.e. the three countries have no rights of national jurisdiction within the Black Sea. 155 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY the comprehensive approach. The OSCE has emphasized repeatedly this direct interconnection, along with the need to develop opportunities for cooperation and interaction. With a view of responding to the common security challenges, the OSCE has been actively developing relations with its neighboring countries and has already privileged relations with 11 partners, including 5 Asian cooperation partners (the so-called Asian Partnership for Co-operation, including Afghanistan, Japan, which has also been the first OSCE Asian partner, the Republic of Korea, Thailand, and Australia) and 6 Mediterranean countries (Algeria, Egypt, Israel, Jordan, Morocco, and Tunisia)25. The dialogue with these countries has been within the framework of the full range of OSCE competencies; however, as far as the individual groups of partners are concerned, the OSCE has been focusing on specific issues of common interest. For example, in the case of the Mediterranean countries, the focus has been on border protection and migration management, on counter-terrorism, water management, environmental challenges; on some cultural and religious issues, on tolerance and anti-discrimination; while as far as its Asian partners are concerned, the OSCE comprehensive approach has involved in the first place and, in particular, the mechanism for guaranteeing security and sustainability as a good example for the entire region. Cooperation with the Asian countries has been carried out in the field of transnational threats, border management, transport links, the fight against human trafficking, the building of democratic institutions and the management of elections. The efforts of the OSCE to boost its own capacity to work in all the phases of the conflict have been complemented by its cooperation with some other regional and international organizations. The United Nations have been one of the closest OSCE partners.26 Following the 2001 terrorist attacks, this cooperation intensified when the OSCE provided to the UN tangible support in line with thе organization’s global counter-terrorism efforts. The two organizations have been working together for developing a high-level dialogue while coordinating and sharing at the same time information on the working level. A good example of this has been the OSCE mission in Kosovo, which was, in effect, an integral part of the UN mission in thе country. The UN and the OSCE have also cooperated successfully during the missions of the two countries in Bosnia and Herzegovina, Croatia, Georgia, and Tajikistan. In 2019, the OSCE and the UN signed a joint statement on coordination and cooperation. Since the launch of the Helsinki Process, the European Union (at that time – the organization of the European Communities) has played a significant role in the work of the OSCE, and the interaction between the two organizations has deepened further in the course of time. In 2006, the European Union’s OSCE participation was formalized (in the OSCE Rules of Procedure). All the EU Member States are also OSCE members and their cooperation focuses primarily on some areas, including the judicial reform, the anti-corruption measures, democratization, 25 Jointly with its partner states, the OSCE Secretariat has been organizing regular meetings and some other events. 26 Ever since 1992, when the Member States defined the OSCE as a regional agreement within the meaning of Chapter VIII of the UN Charter. A little later, a framework for co-operation was signed in 1993 and, in the same year, the OSCE was granted the status of an observer. 156 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY institutional building, human rights, media development, the development of the SME, border management, the fight against human trafficking, and elections. NATO is yet another OSCE cooperation partner and has had a significant part in discussing and shaping the OSCE security model. The OSCE shares the view that the two organizations have largely complementary responsibilities, as well as common interests. On its part, NATO continues to develop its cooperation with the OSCE in areas, such as conflict prevention, crisis management and post-conflict rehabilitation. A good example of this has been the OSCE Mission in Kosovo, which managed to establish close relations with NATO’s KFOR. In addition, the SFOR in Bosnia and Herzegovina and the OSCE mission in the country have also been working closely together to implement the Dayton Accords. NATO, the UN, the OSCE and the EU are the key organizations in the security domain. Therefore, when speaking of the possible scenarios for the development of the international security environment, the most optimal one involves undoubtedly the deepening of cooperation between these organizations for the implementation of joint action with a view of addressing the global challenges. Conclusion and recommendations The OSCE is faced up with the challenge to intensify its activity and to update its mechanisms in order to maximize the preservation and the improvement of the European security architecture. The key OSCE feature is that it is, in fact, a platform for political dialogue on a wide range of security issues and, in this sense, it brings together countries with diverse and often conflicting interests. Although the OSCE does not provide any guarantees for the protection and the defense of its participating countries, nor does it have any military units, it has been developing a conceptual framework involving measures to build up mutual trust and transparency, to introduce verification and early warning procedures as well as flexible mechanisms to respond to each and every stage of the development of particular conflicts. Despite its stable institutional basis, among the OSCE difficulties has been the lack of strategic understanding between Russia and the West and, in addition, the existing differences between the rest of its participating countries and their understanding of the OSCE very nature27. In this sense, the problem of the OSCE is political in nature and has to do with the lack of full commitment on the part of its participating states. However, in the context of the still protracted and ongoing conflicts, the role of the OSCE has been somewhat compromised and its mechanisms of influence have been weakened. There are a couple of aspects on which the OSCE should intensify its work. The lack of a final solution to the still ongoing conflicts indicates that the OSCE, although developing a comprehensive activity in the conflict zones, does not currently have a full-fledged way to establish lasting peace and stability in 27 There are serious discrepancies in the engagement of the individual OSCE participating states. On one hand, there are a couple of countries, such as Germany and Switzerland, which are highly valued by the OSCE and whose foreign ministries have individual departments dealing with the OSCE affairs. While, on the other hand, there are some countries, such as Britain, the United States and Russia, for which the OSCE is not a priority. Thirdly, some countries like Ukraine and Georgia are experiencing some frustration, as the OSCE is unable to deal with the conflicts that directly affect them. 157 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY the affected zones. Therefore, in addition to continuing to defend its founding principles, the OSCE should make an in-depth analysis of what has been achieved, of its current field operations and of its negotiation formats in order to be able to identify opportunities for adequate updating, as well as to encourage the participating parties to engage in an active dialogue. The second aspect that the OSCE has to focus on is the arms control, especially given the technological advances in the area of military affairs. The three major principles set out in the three arms control instruments, i.e. transparency, confidence-building, and reducing the fear of a surprise attack, should be maintained but they should also be incorporated into a new political and military framework as the US and Russia continue to block the further development of the already existing contracts. There is currently no consensus on such an initiative. However, work needs to be done to discuss the elements that could possibly be included in a renewed and comprehensive arms control regime in the context of strategic stability28. The stability discussions could renew the OSCE capacity to innovate in the security area where the negotiations will have to include the Black Sea region, too. Being a part of the OSCE, Bulgaria has to defend and further develop its principles, to abide by the commitments it has made, and to pursue a methodical policy along the three security dimensions in cooperation with the NATO and the EU Member States. It is of particular importance for the country to be involved in finding an effective solution to the Ukrainian crisis, as well as to some other ongoing conflicts that have a direct impact on the Black Sea region. In this regard, the priority for the Bulgarian side should be the strengthening of security, especially through negotiation formats and field missions, as well as the restoration of the trust and the dialogue between the participating countries. The consultant of this political report is Mario Marinov, Head of the OSCE and Regional Security Department of the Security Policy Directorate of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. 159 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY Polish Chairmanship of the OSCE in 2022 Embassy of Poland in Bulgaria Poland will assume the one-year Chairmanship of the OSCE by decision of the Ministerial Council on December 5th, 2019, in Bratislava and will hold this function for the second time in history. The first time Poland chaired the Organization was in 1998. The Polish Chairmanship will last from 1 January to 31 December 2022. Poland’s decision to seek the Chairmanship of the OSCE was an expression of the importance Poland attaches to this organisation as an important forum for promoting co-operation and stability in Europe. Poland’s OSCE Chairmanship is also a logical continuation of Poland’s active foreign policy, which included non-permanent membership of the UN Security Council (2018-2019). The Chairmanship is also an opportunity to strengthen Poland’s role as an important human rights centre, creating synergies with the role of the Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights (ODIHR) host state, and with Poland’s membership of the UN Human Rights Council in 2020-2022. In 1998, when the Republic of Poland chaired the Organization for the first time, both Poland and the OSCE were in a much different moment in time. Today, after more than 20 years, circumstances are hard to compare. Poland is about to assume the Chairmanship at a time when the participating States face a multitude of serious challenges to peace and security. Protracted conflicts, military confrontation, radicalization, terrorism, together with ongoing erosion of confidence are leading to rising uncertainty, unpredictability, fragmentation and growing fears in our societies. As a Chair, Poland will refer to the guiding principle of Polish foreign policy, which is based on the primacy of international law in relations between states. Poland will call for strengthening of the OSCE and the United Nations’ founding principles – sovereign equality of states, territorial integrity and the inviolability of borders, non-interference in internal affairs and, freedom of choice of alliances and co-operation among states, refraining from the threat or unilateral use of force, and peaceful settlement of international disputes. Undoubtedly, common denominator of all OSCE Participating States is the pursuit of peace.
