In the Helsinki Final Act, the participating States expressed the explicit intention to ensure conditions in which people can live in true and lasting peace, free from any threat or attempt against their security, and the need to promote fundamental rights, economic and social progress and well-being for all people. Conflict and post-conflict periods deprive people of one of their basic needs and rights – security. Hence, it is the OSCE obligation to protect and support the weakest, civilians, women and children from consequences of conflicts. The Organisation has the necessary toolbox and long lasting experience in delivering assistance to the people in need. Since 1975, participating States have agreed on a number of legal and systemic improvements aiming at advancing the security and protection of citizens and all people in need. After almost 50 years, this 160 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY concept has not lost any significance and constant recommitment to the “OSCE acquis” remains of vital importance. What is needed nowadays is practical approach, for example, to the tensions arising as a result of differences in the historical memory of different nations. It is worth looking for inspiration in the 19th century by recalling Napoleon Bonaparte, whose 200th death anniversary we marked in 2021. For the French, Napoleon was the founder of the modern state, a reformer of the administration and a symbol of the greatness of the French nation. For the Poles – an ally bringing them hope for freedom. For some other European nations – a reformer of the civil code and, one might say, the forerunner of the struggle for equality, freedom and fraternity and women’s rights. For Americans a remarkable strategist. For the Russians and some others – a leader of an invading army. The point is that all these ways of remembrance of Napoleon function in parallel to each other, emphasizing different and important elements without resorting to distortion of history. Within the OSCE, all the actors enjoy flexibility to emphasize different elements of the three dimensions – according to their specific priorities or concerns. The common denominator however is freedom of speech and research, an open access to the archives, and on the state level, respect for the treaties, principles and commitments they signed up to. As the OSCE Chairmanship Poland will spare no effort to assist participating States in fostering dialogue while guarding OSCE’s principles and commitments. The upholding of the OSCE comprehensive concept of security with political and economic security, human rights and democracy will be at the center of action during Polish Chairmanship. As the COVID-19 pandemic perpetuates and exacerbates existing economic and social inequalities, the OSCE should get more sensible and seek efforts to mitigate its impact. Polish OSCE Chairmanship will be guided by three main objectives: • supporting the OSCE’s conflict resolution formats and processes, with special attention to promoting comprehensive assistance for the conflict-affected population; • responding to post-COVID challenges in the OSCE region through effective multilateralism; • fully utilising the OSCE’s potential through working in a spirit of co- operation towards implementation of our shared commitments. All agree the security situation in the OSCE area could and should be improved. In almost all of the cases of protracted conflicts, the ceasefire is fragile, the cycles of violence are repeated and the settlement process is painful, slow or even frozen. The states affected by the conflicts cannot fully pursue their sovereign aspirations in economic, foreign policy and security terms. The conflicts are a heavy burden for economies and for the daily lives of the population, often becoming a source of internal political turmoil. Against this background, Poland wants to contribute to the security and stability of our region. At the top of the upcoming agenda will be the progress in peaceful resolution to the conflicts in the OSCE area in line with the OSCE principles and commitments. 161 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY One of the most extreme examples of the recent challenges to the European security is the conflict affecting Ukraine’s security, sovereignty and territorial integrity. It has already led to the death of over 13,000 people. Poland will fully support all diplomatic efforts aimed at the peaceful resolution of the conflict in and around Ukraine, in compliance with the Minsk Agreements and in full respect of the sovereignty, territorial integrity and unity of Ukraine within its internationally recognized borders. Poland highly values the contribution of the Special Monitoring Mission to Ukraine (SMM). The SMM must be able to fully and effectively carry out its mandate throughout Ukraine. In the capacity of the OSCE’s Chairman-in-Office Poland will spare no effort to advance the process of the resolution of the conflict in Georgia, also keeping in mind its impact on the affected population. Poland will offer support in bringing the Transnistria settlement process closer to its resolution, to strengthen dialogue and confidence-building measures. The OSCE – with its autonomous institutions, structures and field operations – has a unique potential to deal with conflict situations. A joint reflection is needed how to fully utilize this potential and to look for synergy among different tools and instruments. The so-called conflict cycle concept provides unique tools enhancing the OSCE capabilities in early warning, early action, dialogue facilitation and mediation support, as well as post-conflict rehabilitation. Polish efforts will be oriented at strengthening overall security in the OSCE region but also at bringing real improvements to the conflict-affected population. Progress even in seemingly minor issues can make a big change to people living in conflict areas. Being aware that resolution of the conflicts in the region depends mostly on the political will of the participating States, Poland will encourage the use of OSCE as a forum for engagement and dialogue. In the first dimension, a genuine dialogue among the participating States must be based on the principles of international law principles and strong commitment to the existing OSCE instruments and tools. The arms control and confidence-and-security-building measures remain crucial for security in the entire OSCE area. As traditional threats persist, it is essential to continue our dialogue on increasing military transparency and risk reduction, and to emphasize the role of confidence-building measures. Poland will support every effort made in the Forum for Security Co-operation, as well as within the Structured Dialogue, to ensure that our instruments are fully applied, and – where appropriate – modernized. The full implementation of arms control agreements and modernization of CSBMs is and will remain essential for regaining politico-military stability within the OSCE area. This pertains particularly to the Vienna Document, which is the bedrock of common security system, but which, to be fully effective, needs to be updated to current realities. At the same time, Poland will pay close attention to non-military security challenges. In this regard, Polish Chairmanship will closely co-operate with the Chair of the Security Committee. The COVID-19 pandemic made us dependent more than ever on modern technologies, highlighting the importance of 162 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY cybersecurity. As the Chairmanship Poland wants to give this crosscutting topic due priority, and in particular focus on ways to improve social cyber resilience. At the same time, since old threats have not disappeared, Poland will continue OSCE’s efforts in countering terrorism and combating transnational organized crime. The second dimension remains a key component of the OSCE comprehensive concept of security. In 2022, the world will focus on the transformation and modernization of economies. Achieving climate neutrality and reduction of their environmental footprint remains a great challenge for all OSCE countries. It has also direct implications for the security, stability and sustainable development. The fact that the OSCE participating States are at various stages of economic development, with various energy mixes, should be treated as an opportunity for the OSCE, stimulating co-operation on transition to a green economy. In the way towards the green economy both environmental and socio-economic dimension needs to be addressed, mindful of regional variations. Nobody cannot ignore the negative impact of the ongoing pandemic, as the economies continue to struggle with its aftermaths. Post-pandemic recovery requires co-ordinated and targeted actions to ensure inclusivity and sustainability. Building on the previous debates, Poland wishes to stimulate further discussions in this respect. As the host country of the Office of Democratic Institutions and Human Rights Poland attaches special importance to the third dimension. Polish Chairmanship will act to strengthen and develop co-operation among the participating States in the human dimension, based on a rich menu of commitments and on the expertise and assistance provided within a sufficient toolbox by the OSCE autonomous institutions. Political support to the actions undertaken by the ODIHR, the High Commissioner on National Minorities and the Representative on Freedom of the Media will be therefore at the core of the Polish Chairmanship’s approach. It is in the interests of the OSCE to help each Participating state to be stable, democratic and to ensure a prosperous future for their societies. As the Chairmanship, Poland will stand ready to use the OSCE mechanisms to promote the respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms as well as to facilitate inclusive internal dialogue between the state authorities and civil society in any situation of crisis. The unique feature of the OSCE is its focus on the comprehensive concept of security. It is in particular in conflict areas where the interlinkage between security and human rights is more evident than elsewhere. Hence, the Chairmanship’s efforts we will be guided by a human-centered perspective, seeking to improve the conditions of the weakest and most vulnerable. It particularly applies to persons living in the conflict affected areas. Supporting them in full enjoyment of human rights and fundamental freedoms will be Poland’s priority. As a firm defender of the rights of the child and of persons with disabilities, Poland will particularly focus on these two groups of persons living in conflict areas and will highlight their complex needs stemming from both security and human rights challenges. 163 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL SECURITY Polish Chairmanship will also pay a special attention to the young generation, as youth ensures a vibrant character of our democratic societies. Addressing the needs of young people, as well as integrating their perspective into our actions, will be of particular significance for the Polish Chairmanship. It is common duty to develop initiatives addressing the needs of the youth, but also contributing to their civic education and digital literacy, in order to minimize their vulnerability to disinformation and other contemporary menaces to democracy. The COVID-19 pandemic has affected many spheres of public life. It has also speeded up the already rapid digital transformation. While this process should be considered a positive development, it deserves, however, to be thoroughly explored from the human rights perspective. Since human rights and fundamental freedoms are universal and do not know any borders, they have to be respected also in the digital sphere. The promotion of freedom of religion or belief remains the cornerstone of Polish foreign policy in the human rights area. Like other fundamental freedoms, it is impossible to protect it without an effective multilateral cooperation. Poland will also encourage close co-operation with the OSCE Parliamentary Assembly, which acts as a key link between Governments and Parliaments of the OSCE participating States. Polish OSCE Chairmanship will not forget about the Mediterranean and Asian Partners. Chairing the OSCE Mediterranean Partnership for Co-operation Poland highly values the vivid exchange with the Partner States and the participating States in this unique format. To recapitulate, the OSCE must again find responses to the rapidly evolving security environment. They should not be revolutionary as the fundamental principles and commitments guiding our Organization remain valid. Therefore, it is Polish ambition to ensure that the OSCE operates as efficiently as possible. To achieve this common goal all OSCE Participating States have to act collectively and to look for practical steps to ease the everyday lives of the conflict affected populations, to enhance dialogue in crisis situations, to promote post-COVID recovery and to fully utilize the OSCE potential to improve the conditions of the weakest and most vulnerable. 164 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW Council of Europe’s Monitoring and Bulgaria Katya Todorova 1. The Council of Europe The Council of Europe (the CoE) is an international intergovernmental organization established on May 5, 1949, under the Treaty of London, in a period in which Europe was gradually recovering from the World War II devastations and the great many human tragedies it had experienced. The establishment of the Council of Europe was dedicated to the long-standing need for preserving a peace based on justice and international cooperation. The idea of such European unification, which was supposed not to allow more fighting and a further war in Europe, was accepted with great enthusiasm. Despite the differences in their views and interests, some leading European politicians of the time jointly laid down the beginnings of the implementation of the idea of a united Europe. Today, they are all referred to as “the founding fathers” and at that time they included: Winston Churchill, Prime Minister of the United Kingdom, Konrad Adenauer, Chancellor and Minister of Foreign Affairs of the Federal Republic of Germany, Robert Schuman, Minister of Foreign Affairs of France, Paul-Henri Spaak, Prime Minister and Minister of Foreign Affairs of Belgium, Alcide de Gasperi, Prime Minister of Italy, and Ernest Bevin, Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs of the United Kingdom. The founding fathers shared the belief that unity was really needed, however, it had to be based on the principles shaping true democracy. On the basis of the spiritual and moral values constituting the common European heritage of the peoples of Europe and representing a true source of individual freedom, of political freedom, and of the rule of law. Maintaining and further developing these ideals in the interests of economic and social progress, however, required much closer cooperation between the European countries within the framework of a new international forum-platform for dialogue and understanding. Therefore, Article 1 of the Council of Europe’s Statute reads: “The aim of the Council of Europe is to achieve a greater unity between its members for the purpose of safeguarding and realising the ideals and principles which are their common heritage and facilitating their economic and social progress.“1 The CoE bodies established under the CoE Statute comprise the Committee of Ministers and the Parliamentary Assembly (PACE). Later on, the Congress of Local and Regional Authorities and the International Non-Governmental Organizations Conference were established on the basis of statutory resolutions.2 The Council of Europe also has a Secretariat and a Secretary General. The Treaty of London was originally signed by 10 founding European countries. Later on, in the wake of the Cold War period and, as a result of the democratic 1 See: the CoE Statute in: Council of Europe Law: Towards a pan-European Legal Area, Florence Benoit- Rohmer, Heinrich Klebes, Council of Europe Publishing, 2005, Balkan Publishing Company Ltd., a publication in Bulgarian, 2007. 2 For more details about the statutory resolutions, Ibid, p. 30. 165 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW changes taking place on the continent, the Central and the Eastern European countries gradually joined the Council of Europe. Thus, the ideal of European unification was put into effect in the Council of Europe and this organization won recognition as a platform for pan-European cooperation. Currently, the CoE brings together 47 Member States and more than 830 million European citizens. The advantages that distinguish the CoE from the other international organizations involve its pan-European nature and the system of more than 200 conventions developed and adopted within its framework. Among these, a central place is occupied by the European Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms, signed in Rome on November 4, 1950, and in force since September 3, 1953. Later on, the European Court of Human Rights was established and still operates on the basis of the above mentioned convention. The CoE has been having an essential role in harmonizing the law of its Member States. This harmonization is carried out, first and foremost, in accordance with the CoE conventions, as well as through the recommendations of the Committee of Ministers to the individual governments, through the advisory opinions of the Venice Commission on the draft laws, and through the debates on the national laws in the course of their drafting, including within the framework of PACE. As a result, a single legal space of democratic security was created and gradually upgraded, in which the harmonized standards in the field of human rights, democracy and the rule of law are applied and operate. The harmonization of standards is done both in the process of their establishment, as well as in the process of their implementation. This process is mostly helped through the main instrument designed for the purpose, namely the monitoring mechanisms operating within the organization. 2. The CoE monitoring system The CoE has a well-developed system of monitoring. There are two types of monitoring performed in the organization, namely: statutory monitoring (based on the CoE Statute) and conventional monitoring (based on the international legal instruments adopted within the CoE). The first type of monitoring is political in nature and is conducted by the two major CoE bodies in compliance with Art. 10 of the CoE Statute. The second type of monitoring is the conventional one and also includes the European system of human rights protection based on the Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms. This monitoring also includes the Committee of Ministers that effects control over the implementation of the decisions made by the European Court of Human Rights on the part of the individual CoE Member States. The objective of the two types of monitoring is to secure correct fulfillment of the obligations by the Member States, as well as to achieve greater political and legal unity between these states in the protection and the implementation of the ideals and of the principles that are a common heritage. (Art. 1 of the Statute). In this particular case, monitoring should be interpreted, more generally, as a systematic process of collecting, analyzing and using information resulting from monitoring of the progress of implementation of the obligations by the Member 166 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW States in relation to the achievement of particular objectives and, accordingly, to the formulation of decision-making proposals. 2.1. Statutory monitoring The topic of political statutory monitoring became particularly relevant in the period of admitting to the CoE the so-called new democracies when they were expected to comply with certain criteria, by virtue of the commitments they had made when preparing to join this organization3. Then, in the 1990s, the Committee of Ministers and the Parliamentary Assembly of the CoE adopted their own rules on which to base and further develop the monitoring procedures they were carrying out. Within their competences, the two main CoE bodies have different possibilities to react in particular circumstances. Each one of them has specific features of its own, which should be preserved; on the other hand, their capabilities should be combined for the purpose of achieving maximum effect. 2.1.1. The Committee of Ministers (the CoM) is the CoE main executive and decision-making body, which conducts political monitoring on the basis of the 1994 Declaration on the Implementation of the CoE Commitments. The CoM discusses all political issues (except for defense), the political aspects of European unification, the development of intergovernmental cooperation, and the protection of the democratic institutions, the human rights and the rule of law. Most of the CoM decisions are taken by a two-thirds majority of the votes cast (a minimum of 31 votes). The decisions made shall be binding on the Member States’ governments and shall be enforceable. Under the CoE Statute, in the event of a serious breach of obligations by a Member State, the CoM may suspend the relevant State’s right of representation, invite it to withdraw, or even decide to terminate its membership of the Council of Europe. However, such extreme measures could seriously upset the balance of the Organization and will negatively affect its pan-European nature. Therefore, the CoM methods are rather aimed at reaching a compromise. By using a constructive approach, the CoM encourages the Member States to work together in a dialogue and cooperation and to take all the necessary measures to comply with the principles set out in the Statute in all matters discussed within the Organization. There are several possibilities for the CoM to react. It can gather information from various sources, including from the CoE monitoring mechanisms, the CoE Commissioner for Human Rights, the European Court of Justice, etc., so that it can discuss and adopt decisions in the CoM format; or from the Permanent Representatives Committee where the decisions take varied forms, such as opinions, recommendations, etc., in order to communicate something to the Parliamentary Assembly or to address the Secretary General directly. 2.1.2. In June, 1993, the CoE Parliamentary Assembly (PACE) adopted the so called Halonen order/order 488 (named after the Finnish MP, Tarja Halonen), which established a parliamentary procedure for the fulfillment of the obligations 3 See: The monitoring procedure of the CoE’s Parliamentary assembly. Jan Kleijssen in International Human Rights Monitoring Mechanisms pp. 623-624, Gudmundur Alfrodsson et al. eds. 2001. 167 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW of the new Member States. In 1994, PACE adopted a new Halonen Resolution, which extended the scope of the monitoring procedure to the obligations arising from the Statute, the ECHR, and from all other conventions. This monitoring procedure evolved gradually in a way to cover all Member States4. PACE is more flexible in the situations where a rapid response is required. Within the Assembly, the diversity of opinions and the presence of representatives of 47 national parliaments, which participate in the debates on a party basis, allows for a broad discussion on the current political issues. Thus PACE acts as a CoE political engine. PACE provides a useful political impetus, which subsequently generates action on all levels of the Organization, thus setting its political agenda in line with the realities in Europe. PACE has developed its own regime of monitoring and sanctions against the Member States. It has also set up a Commission of its own to comply with the obligations and the commitments of the Member States (the so-called “Monitoring Committee”). The monitoring procedure involves: - Regular visits to the relevant Member State by two reporting members / rapporteurs/. This procedure currently applies to the following 10 countries: Albania, Armenia, Azerbaijan, Bosnia and Herzegovina, Georgia, Moldova, the Russian Federation, Serbia, Turkey, and Ukraine. - A post-monitoring dialogue, which is a less intensive procedure applied to the countries that have already made some progress. This currently applies to Bulgaria, Montenegro, and the Republic of North Macedonia. - Periodic reviews of all the countries over a period of five to six years; - Drawing up a report on the functioning of the democratic institutions in a Member State where some specific developments so require. Pursuant to Art. 8 and 9 of the PACE Rules of Procedure, the credentials of a national delegation could be challenged on the substance, i.e. on the grounds of serious breaches of the CoE fundamental principles and values; or of persistent non-compliance or lack of co-operation on the part of a particular country, which is at this moment within a PACE monitoring procedure. Although in the 1970s PACE used to use this opportunity cautiously and did not ratify/approve the credentials of a national delegation only twice, namely in relation to Greece (in 1969) and to Turkey (in 1981), it is precisely the exercise of this particular PACE competence (based on its own rules of procedure, and not on the Statute) that became the “apple of discord” between itself and the Committee of Ministers, after PACE had refused, in 2014, to ratify the powers of the Russian delegation as a result of the illegal annexation of Crimea. Russia’s response was to suspend the payments of its membership fee to the CoE budget, which put the CoE in a serious financial crisis and, in a sense, even jeopardized its own functioning. With a view to finding a solution to the return of the Russian parliamentary delegation to participate in the PACE operations, the two statutory bodies, the Parliamentary Assembly and the Council of Ministers, drafted and adopted, in compliance with their internal rules and procedures, a “Complementary joint 4 For further details Ibid 168 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW procedure between the Committee of Ministers and the Parliamentary Assembly in response to a serious violation by a member State of its statutory obligations”. The main purpose of this additional procedure was to restore, through a constructive dialogue and cooperation, abidance on the part of the Member States concerned by the CoE obligations and principles, while avoiding the imposition of sanctions.5 It should be noted here, however, that there have been problems with both the “regular” PACE monitoring, as well as with the post-monitoring dialogue. Since the establishment of the Monitoring Committee in 1997, this procedure has undergone major changes; however, the practice of its implementation indicates that constant rethinking is needed in order to increase its effectiveness. In addition, the COVID-19 pandemic has raised yet another issue related to the adaptation of the Monitoring Committee’s methods to the circumstances caused by the disease, as well as to the relevant measures taken to get the pandemic under control.6 Any developments resulting from the effectiveness of this monitoring are regularly tracked down by the CoE Secretary General in his annual reports. As stated in the Secretary-General’s 2019 annual report, some further reforms need to be effected in order to make the CoE monitoring system more effective. This applies to both the political and the conventional monitoring. Better coordination is needed between the two, starting with PACE and the CoM. The thesis that PACE’s monitoring and post-monitoring dialogue is ineffective and needs to be improved accordingly is shared by most Member States, especially these in Central and Eastern Europe, which are the ones most often monitored. It is a fact that the procedures are applied to a limited number of countries and take too long, without any sufficient clarity of the evaluation criteria. The Rapporteurs are usually very busy with their commitments and find it relatively difficult to set some time aside for on-site visits, which complicates further the entire process. This is also bound to lead to the accumulation of monitoring fatigue, thus calling the beneficial effect of the monitoring into question7. The monitored countries maintain the position that it is not always clear what is expected of them exactly in order to make any progress in either the monitoring procedure or in the post-monitoring dialogue; that the scope of the reports exceeds significantly the initial commitments, and that the efforts of the national authorities in this process are not appreciated appropriately. A number of countries have been in favor of a clear definition of the areas, which have to be monitored, as well as of applying the latter to all Member States, in accordance with the principle of equal treatment and of the elimination of the double standards and the dividing lines between the CoE countries. Therefore, a positive assessment was made of the possible introduction by PACE of a 5 See: Doc. 15024 / 06 January 2020: Complementary joint procedure between the Committee of Ministers and the Parliamentary Assembly in response to a serious violation by a member State of its statutory obligations, Report 1 Committee on Political Affairs and Democracy, Rapporteur: Mr. Frank Schwabe, Germany, Socialists, Democrats and Greens Group. 6 See: Resolution 2357 (2021): Progress of the Assembly’s monitoring procedure (January-December), Committee on the honoring of obligations and commitments by Member States of the Council of Europe, Rapporteur Mr. Michael Aastrup Jensen, Liberal group, Denmark, Parliamentary Assembly. 7 Ibid 169 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW “common” monitoring procedure for all Member States by establishing a periodic review by individual countries of the implementation of their statutory obligations. This embraces the 33 countries that are not covered either by the stricto sensu monitoring or by the post-monitoring dialogue (PACE Resolution 2018 (2014)). In addition, the Monitoring Committee was invited to launch a thematic, cross- country monitoring to be conducted by groups of countries, thus complementing the country by country monitoring procedure. The ultimate goal of this reform is to ensure equal treatment of the Member States by means of a periodic monitoring of all, on an equal basis, and on an equal footing.8 The post-monitoring dialogue is also subject to reform. Its timeframe should be determined by much stricter rules; and a specific negative report should indicate the deadlines for implementation, set previously in cooperation with the country with which the dialogue is being conducted. In this sense, some amendments have been made to the mandate of the PACE Monitoring Committee, which stipulates an obligation for the post-monitoring dialogue reports to include a draft resolution stating either that the dialogue should be concluded, or setting specific deadlines for the implementation of specific commitments. In the event that, according to PACE, these deadlines are not met by a State in a post-monitoring dialogue, a return to a full monitoring procedure is envisaged. 2.2. Conventional monitoring The conventional monitoring functions are performed by specialized institutions or control bodies, which are established by virtue of specific conventions within the CoE system. This group also includes the ones that are created on the basis of CoM resolutions. Among the specialized institutions and control bodies we should specify the following:  The Commissioner for Human Rights: this is a non-judicial, independent and impartial institution, with a mandate to promote the effective respect for human rights and to assist the individual states in implementing the norms and the standards in this area;  The European Commission for Democracy through Law, known to the general public as the Venice Commission. It has advisory functions and provides legal advice on matters of interest to the Member States, especially in the field of constitutional law. The aim here is to ensure that the national institutions comply with the European norms in relation to democracy, the human rights, and the rule of law;  The European Committee for the Prevention of Torture and Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Punishment (CPT). By means of on-site visits, the CPT monitors the condition and the circumstances in the prisons and in some other places of detention. It assesses the treatment of the persons deprived of their liberty. It draws up reports and recommendations to the relevant Member States concerned; 8 See: Doc. 15031, 13 January 2020, The progress of the Assembly’s monitoring procedure (January- December 2019), Report, Committee on the honoring of obligations and commitments by Member States of the Council of Europe, Rapporteur Sir Roger Gale, United Kingdom, European Conservatives Group and Democratic Alliance. 170 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW  The European Committee of Social Rights, which monitors the implementation of the Social Charter in two complementary manners, namely, on the basis of collective complaints on the part of the social partners, as well as on the basis of national reports drawn up by the Social Charter countries;  The Advisory Committee on the Framework Convention for the Protection of National Minorities (FCNM). It is composed of independent national experts assigned with the task of assessing the implementation of the Framework Convention by the relevant Framework Convention Parties and, also, of forming opinions designed for the CoM;  The Committee of Experts of the European Charter for Regional or Minority Languages, which assesses the honoring of obligations by the Parties to the Charter and may make recommendations for the improvement of the legislation and of the relevant policies and practices;  The European Commission against Racism and Intolerance (ECRI) composed of independent members. The efforts of this commission are aimed at counteracting all manifestations of racism, racial discrimination, xenophobia, antisemitism or intolerance. The Commission prepares reports based on the on- site visits, along with recommendations to the relevant governments;  The Group of Experts on Action against Trafficking in Human Beings (GRETA) is the expert technical element of the monitoring mechanism under the Convention on Action against Trafficking in Human Beings. The second element is political in nature and involves the Committee of the Parties to the Convention. The two elements operate closely together, i.e. GRETA prepares the evaluation reports on the measures taken to combat trafficking in human beings and the Committee of the Parties makes recommendations to ensure the implementation of GRETA’s conclusions;  The Group of States against Corruption (GRECO). The aim of this group is to increase the capacity of states in their fight against corruption by complying with the CoE’s anti-corruption rules, in particular the CoE’s Criminal Law Convention on Corruption and the CoE’s Civil Law Convention on Corruption. GRECO applies the method of mutual evaluation and mutual pressure. Monitoring is carried out through a “horizontal” evaluation procedure. The resulting recommendations are aimed at encouraging legislative and institutional reforms, along with the relevant practices;  The Committee of Experts on the Evaluation of Anti-Money Laundering Measures and the Financing of Terrorism (MONEYVAL). This committee assesses the implementation of the basic international norms regulating the fight against money laundering and terrorism financing and formulates recommendations aimed at the attention of the national authorities for the purpose of securing a more efficient application of the said norms. All MONEYVAL reports are made public automatically;  The Group of Experts on Action against Violence against Women and Domestic Violence (GREVIO). This is the monitoring body under the Convention on preventing and combating violence against women and domestic violence (the so-called Istanbul Convention). GREVIO is composed of independent experts. 171 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW It draws up evaluation reports on the measures taken by the Parties to the Convention to implement this convention effectively;  The Committee of the Parties to the Convention on the Protection of Children against Sexual Exploitation and Sexual Abuse (the Lanzarote Committee). It assesses the protection of children against sexual violence in the countries, Parties to the Convention. In exceptional cases, it may require the urgent submission of a special report;  The Congress of Local and Regional Authorities. It is responsible for assessing the implementation, by the Member States, of their obligations under the European Charter of Local Self-Government. The conventional monitoring is carried out through a couple of working methods, which are generally accepted in relation to the system of CoE conventions. These methods are also adopted in the CoM resolutions as follows: - regular visits paid by the relevant experts to each one of the States that has to be monitored. The visits are made within certain cycles; - ad hoc on-the-spot inspections where some exceptional circumstances so require. In principle, such a possibility is contained in the text of the relevant convention or in the procedural rules of the monitoring body; - an assessment made on the basis of the questionnaires prepared by the experts and answered by the national authorities; - reports drawn-up by the countries to be monitored, assessing their own progress. The expert assessment of the monitoring body is usually made in the form of a report containing the findings, the opinions and the recommendations concerning the implementation of the obligations of the relevant State. Every report shall be addressed to the national authorities. The evaluation itself has both a political and a practical effect. Political, because the reports, i.e. the progress made in honoring of the obligations is considered by the CoM, which is in effect the CoE decision-making body; and practical, because the recommendations have to be implemented through the necessary measures on a practical level. The observations indicate that, in the field of conventional monitoring, there has been a clear tendency of gradually expanding the range of issues subject to verification and evaluation. This approach is based on the doctrine of living instruments, which has its origins in the case law of the European Court of Human Rights in Strasbourg. The understanding is that the conventions are living instruments and should be interpreted by the monitoring bodies in the light of modern requirements. As a result, they acquire the quality of being a source of an ever-widening range of obligations for the States that goes beyond the range of obligations they have agreed to assume when acceding to the relevant convention. It should be noted, however, that not all the Member States are inclined to embrace this approach without any reaction, as it puts them in a defensive position regardless of the fact that the CoE normative acquis and the mechanisms implementing the said acquis are indisputable standards in the field of democracy, the human rights, and the rule of law. 172 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW 3. The CoE monitoring mechanisms and Bulgaria 3.1. Conventional monitoring Bulgaria has been cooperating fairly well with the CoE conventional monitoring mechanisms, including the ones established by CoM statutory resolutions. The Bulgarian authorities are prepared to assist in the organization of the on-site expert visits and in the implementation of their full work programs. Following the submission of the reports, the governments’ comments shall be submitted on time and most of the recommendations shall be implemented. We need to emphasize here the constant working dialogue of the Bulgarian authorities with the Venice Commission in the field of the judicial reform, including the status of the Prosecutor General. Bulgaria regularly requests from the Venice Commission consultative opinions on all issues related to the structure and the functioning of the judiciary. The proposals for any legislative or constitutional amendments are also coordinated with the Venice Commission and the Commission’s recommendations are discussed by the competent authorities with all the due attention. Over the past two years, the pandemic situation in Europe has to a certain extent disrupted the work schedule of the visits under the monitoring mechanisms but, nevertheless, in 2020 and 2021, GRETA, CPT, ECRI, and MONEYVAL experts paid evaluation visits to the country. Even in its first evaluation report and the respective recommendations, GRETA really encouraged the Bulgarian authorities to strengthen prevention through the social and economic measures designed to protect the vulnerable groups against human trafficking. Bulgaria has adopted a number of political documents and action plans aimed at preventing any human trafficking, for example in the field of gender equality, the prevention of violence against children, and the Roma integration. The third monitoring report was published in May, 2021. In this report, GRETA appealed to the authorities to improve the access of the victims of trafficking to justice and compensation, as well as to ensure the sustainability of all trafficking combating measures by providing the necessary funding. On November 4, 2021, the CPT published the so-called public statement regarding Bulgaria. This is a critical publication concerning the persistent lack of implementation of the CPT’s long-term recommendations as regards the current situation, i.e. the ill-treatment of persons with psychiatric needs accommodated in the social institutions and the psychiatric hospitals. The MONEYVAL experts made a monitoring visit to the country between September 6 and September 17, 2021. The aim of this visit was to assess the technical standards adhered to in Bulgaria, along with the effective implementation of the international standards in the field of combating money laundering and terrorist financing in compliance with the 2013 Methodology drawn up by the Financial Action Task Force. The report will be ready in the spring of 2022. In addition, the report of the European Commission against Racism and Intolerance (ECRI) is expected to be ready in the spring of 2022. As far as the communication of the Bulgarian authorities with the conventional 173 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW monitoring mechanisms is concerned, it seems appropriate to boost the role of the National Coordination Mechanism on Human Rights established by Decision 796/19.12.2013 of the Council of Ministers in response to the need to take concrete measures to improve the coordination between the state bodies having competence in the field of human rights, as well as between the said bodies and the independent institutions dealing with various aspects of human rights. The first priority of the Mechanism involves the distribution, among its own members, of the fulfillment of obligations related to the permanent dialogue and the periodic national reports intended for the international control and monitoring human rights mechanisms. The observations of previous practice, however, show that the Mechanism’s potential in this regard has not been used fully yet. 3.2. Statutory political monitoring Bulgaria has been a member of the Council of Europe since May 7, 1992. It was also subject to full monitoring until 2000 in terms of compliance with the principles of the organization, namely democracy, human rights and the rule of law. In the year 2000, PACE adopted Resolution 1211, which eased this procedure and changed it into a post-monitoring dialogue, which continues until present day. Bulgaria is the only EU Member State under a CoE political monitoring procedure. The initial areas of observation were: the judiciary and the independence of the judiciary system; the administrative reform; the media independence; the fight against corruption and police violence; the situation of minorities (mostly Roma population); freedom of thought and protection of the religious minorities; the operations of the Ombudsman’s Office; human trafficking. The reports on Bulgaria are drawn up by members of the PACE Monitoring Commission following on-site visits.9 3.2.1. Report by Serhiy Holovaty (Ukraine), Chairman of the Monitoring Committee, following an on-site visit made in November 2008 Based on this report, PACE adopted Resolution 1730 (2010), which recognized the progress made by Bulgaria in launching the judiciary reform, the introduction of the Ombudsman’s institution, the amendments to the Constitution, the Criminal Code, and the Code of Civil Procedure. It was recommended that the post-monitoring dialogue be continued until full compliance with the European standards is achieved. 3.2.2. Report by Luca Volontè (Italy), European People’s Party, following visits paid in December 2011 and September 2012. This report was considered on January 22, 2013. The draft resolution contained a proposal to end the post-monitoring dialogue, however, it did not receive sufficient support in the vote, in fact, two votes were not enough. With the adopted Resolution 1915 (2013) PACE decided to continue the post-monitoring dialogue in three main areas: reform of the judiciary and the fight against corruption; freedom of the media and transparency in relation to the media owners; the election legislation. 3.2.3. Report by Frano Matusic (Croatia), European People’s Party, following visits made in February 2014 and June 2015. 9 See: Time-sequence of reports, Reference prepared by the Human Rights Directorate of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, May 29, 2019. 174 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW The range of the issues monitored covered: the reform of the judiciary, freedom and transparency of the media, the reform of the Electoral Code, the fight against corruption, the rights of minorities and, in particular, the integration of the Roma. During this visit, the representatives of all government institutions expressed their position for an end to be put to the post-monitoring procedure, as they believed that it had already fulfilled its purpose. 3.2.4. Report by Frank Schwabe (Germany), Socialists, and Zsolt Németh (Hungary), European People’s Party, following visits made in June 2016 and October 2018. This report acknowledged the progress made by Bulgaria in the monitored areas and, in particular, the introduction of legislative changes for the purposes of the judicial reform and the fight against corruption. Some unresolved issues, however, were identified, which, according to the report, were causing some concern and called into question the sustainability and the irreversibility of the reforms. These included: functioning of the judiciary, the legal framework and, in particular, the Code of Criminal Procedure and the Criminal Code; the high-level corruption and organized crime; the freedom of the media; the Electoral Code; the human rights and, in particular, the implementation of European Court of Human Rights’ judgments; the treatment of detainees, minorities (with a focus on the Roma), refugees and asylum seekers; the rights of women. For this reason, the Monitoring Commission proposed to the PACE to continue the post-monitoring dialogue until June 2020, when an assessment of Bulgaria’s progress in these areas had to be made. This report was the subject of a debate in the plenary hall on June 27, 2019, and a precedent arose during the discussion. One of the rapporteurs, F. Schwabe, supported the proposal to continue the post-monitoring dialogue, while the other one – Z. Németh, came up with a proposal that this procedure had to be terminated. Unfortunately, again, two votes were missing for the adoption of Z. Németh’s proposal as a result of which the post-monitoring dialogue with Bulgaria continues until present day. Resolution 2296 (2019) was adopted. As a result, Z. Németh resigned as Rapporteur for Bulgaria. He was replaced by Aleksander Pociej, Chairman of the PACE Group of the European People’s Party. The pandemic situation in Europe has changed the PACE working methods and has also thwarted the decision to consider the post-monitoring dialogue with Bulgaria during the June PACE session scheduled for 2020. There have been no developments in relation to this topic, as the two Rapporteurs have not yet visited Bulgaria for an evaluation visit. 3.2.5. Report by Michael Aastrup Jensen: Progress made by the PACE monitoring procedure Thus far, there has been no concrete action in relation to the post-monitoring dialogue with Bulgaria. However, this country has been included in the group of States under constant PACE monitoring, where the results are presented in the annual reports of the Monitoring Commission.10 M. A. Jensen’s report has confirmed the position of the Monitoring Committee 10 See: Resolution 2357 (2021) Progress of the Assembly’s monitoring procedure (January-December), Committee on the honoring of obligations and commitments by Member States of the Council of Europe, Rapporteur Mr. Michael Aastrup Jensen, Liberal group, Denmark, Parliamentary Assembly 175 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW that Bulgaria needs “to step up its efforts in addressing outstanding concerns identified in Resolution 2296 (2019) “Post-monitoring dialogue with Bulgaria” including high level corruption, media freedom, human rights of minorities, hate speech and violence against women and to make full use of the Council of Europe’s legal expertise in the process of the adoption of a new Constitution”. 3.3. Usefulness of the post-monitoring dialogue with Bulgaria Undoubtedly, the PACE post-monitoring procedure is useful for meeting the commitments of the respective CoE Member States in accordance with the content and the level of the organization’s standards in the field of democracy, human rights, and the rule of law. In the case of Bulgaria, however, there are some aspects of the post-monitoring dialogue that require further attention: Duration and time frames. The PACE post-monitoring dialogue with Bulgaria has been going on for 21 years now and it is still not possible to talk about any time frames. This fully overlaps with the conclusion made in the 2019 Secretary General’s Report that this procedure applies to a limited number of countries over a long period and, therefore, brings forth monitoring “fatigue” in the countries concerned. This is supposed to mean that a post-monitoring dialogue stretched over time becomes ineffective and loses its usefulness11;  Areas under surveillance. In the case of Bulgaria, there has been a tendency of gradually expanding the range of the areas observed. This becomes clear in the report by F. Schwabe and Z. Németh, where the treatment of detainees, minorities, refugees and asylum seekers, along with the rights of women have been added to the initially identified areas of concern. In reality, the application of such an “ever broadening” approach tends to turn the post-monitoring into sheer monitoring, thus melting away any prospects for completion. This approach has been demotivating for Bulgaria, which has been making futile efforts to make any progress in the areas under surveillance, as the number of these areas has been constantly increasing;  Fact assessment criteria. The latest post-monitoring report on Bulgaria, that of F. Schwabe and Z. Németh, contains a compilation of relatively accurately presented conclusions made by various CoE monitoring mechanisms, such as the CoE Commissioner for Human Rights, the European Commission for Democracy through Law (the Venice Commission), the Group of States against Corruption (GRECO), the Group of Experts on Action against Trafficking in Human Beings (GRETA), the European Committee for the Prevention of Torture (the CPT), the Advisory Committee on the Framework Convention for the Protection of National Minorities, the European Commission against Racism and Intolerance (ECRI), etc. The general impression is that the report is based mainly on the assessments of these monitoring bodies, while the criteria for analysis and self- assessment remain unclear. This becomes especially evident in the interpretation of some sensitive topics in relation to which the Bulgarian constitutional and legal order has come up with a decision. Some specific examples include the 11 See: Report by the Secretary General for the Ministerial Session in Helsinki, 16-17 Мay 2019, April 2019, Ready for future challenges reinforcing the Council of Europe 176 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW minority rights issue and the ratification of the CoE Convention on preventing and combating violence against women and domestic violence (the so-called Istanbul Convention);  Evaluation criteria in relation to completion of the post-monitoring dialogue. Bulgaria’s experience indicates that these criteria and their application also need some more objectivity and impartiality. They are the ones that determine whether any post-monitoring is necessary and useful to the country concerned and should not be influenced by subjective reasons. The precedent with Bulgaria is very indicative when in the same report one of the Rapporteurs maintains the position of terminating the procedure and the other one, on the contrary, of continuing it. Therefore, clear criteria are needed to give clear indications of the end of the post-monitoring dialogue and, also, to motivate the relevant country. The above-stated considerations in connection with the post-monitoring dialogue with Bulgaria are important for the usefulness of this dialogue and for achieving its objectives. They are, in effect, part and parcel of the discussion in the Council of Europe about the need to reform the organization’s monitoring mechanisms in order to increase their effectiveness. In particular, the discussions on the usefulness of the post-monitoring dialogue are an element of the broader PACE debate on the development of the monitoring procedure in general, which aims to include all the CoE Member States in this monitoring procedure. 3.4. The position of Bulgaria Bulgaria insists that its post-monitoring dialogue with PACE should be ended due to the fact that the country has achieved a high level of CoE standards in the field of democracy, human rights and the rule of law as a result of which the post-monitoring dialogue has achieved its purpose and its usefulness has already been exhausted. In addition, Bulgaria agrees with the possibility of monitoring and evaluating the implementation of its statutory obligations under the new procedure for general review-monitoring of all the CoE Member States. Over the years, this position has been expressed by a number of Bulgarian representatives on the highest possible level and in various formats. Looking to the future, it should be emphasized that the Bulgarian authorities should continue to take active steps, in cooperation with the CoE, in order to make progress in the areas of monitoring and evaluation, such as the reform of the judiciary, the legal framework, the fight against high-level corruption and organized crime, the media freedom, the electoral law, and human rights. These authorities should also object to the use of a broader approach to the currently supervised issues. It is also necessary for Bulgarian foreign policy and Bulgarian diplomacy to set as their priority Bulgaria’s interaction with the CoE monitoring mechanisms, and within the framework of these mechanisms, as well as the completion of Bulgaria’s post-monitoring dialogue with PACE. However, a national consensus and joint efforts are needed in relation to these issues. In particular, the conclusion of the post-monitoring dialogue with PACE requires 177 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 INTERNATIONAL LAW the development of a national action plan and close coordination between all state bodies that are competent on the subject – in the first place, the National Assembly and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. Bulgaria’s interaction with the CoE monitoring mechanisms is an issue of particular importance to the country’s national interests. The evaluation capacity of these mechanisms enjoys wide publicity and affects directly the image of the country domestically and internationally, as well as its authority in the international relations. It is also important to note here that the European Union also sees the Council of Europe as a referent point in the process of making its own decisions. The comprehensive and legally binding permanent mechanism, which is in place in the European Union for monitoring the rule of law in the EU Member States, refers to CoE standards, in particular the findings and the assessments of the CoE monitoring system. It is well known that close cooperation has been established between the CoE and the EU, so it could be assumed that, in the future, the CoE is going to play an even greater role in ensuring uniform norms and standards in Europe in the protection of democracy, of human rights and of the supremacy of the law. Ambassador Katya Todorova, D.Sc. (Law), is a diplomat with extensive professional experience. She has held a number of expert and managerial positions at the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, including the positions of Director of the International Law Directorate and of the Human Rights Directorate. She has also been long-term participant in the work of the Committee on Legal Questions (Sixth Committee) of the UN General Assembly. In the period February 2011 – February 2014, she was Chief Inspector of the Bulgarian Ministry of Foreign Affairs. She was appointed Deputy Minister of Foreign Affairs in three Bulgarian governments, namely: Simeon Saxe-Coburg-Gotha’s (2001-2005), Prof. Georgi Bliznashki’s (2014), and Boyko Borissov’s (2014- 2015). In the period 2005-2010, Ms. Todorova was Ambassador of the Republic of Bulgaria to the Kingdom of Morocco. She was awarded a state award by King Mohammed VI for her contribution to the development of the bilateral relations between the two countries. From September 2015 to November 2020, Ms. Todorova was Permanent Representative of the Republic of Bulgaria to the Council of Europe. During the Bulgarian Presidency of the CoE Committee of Ministers (November 2015 – May 2016), she served as Chairman of the Committee of the Member States’ Permanent Representatives. 178 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION The Visegrad Group – role in shaping the EU policies and opportunities for cooperation with Bulgaria Alexander Seferinov Introduction On February 15, 2021, we marked the 30th anniversary of the establishment of the Visegrad Group. Back in 1991, in their quest for European integration, the presidents of Poland and the then Czechoslovakia, together with the Hungarian Prime Minister, signed at the Visegrad Castle in Hungary a political declaration marking the beginning of cooperation and rapprochement between the peoples of the three countries (which a little later became four in number). There have been several stages in the transformation of this European region from four Central European countries into a key region in the decision-making process of the European Union (EU), with a great potential in terms of the EU future economic development. The initial objective of cooperation was aimed at the full restoration of state independence, at democracy and freedom, at the elimination of the totalitarian system’s manifestations, at building parliamentary democracy, respect for human rights and the fundamental freedoms, at introducing a market economy, and at full integration into the European political and economic community, including into the EU security and justice systems. The culmination that followed was also symbolic in nature as it involved the accession of the four countries first – to the North Atlantic Alliance (NATO), and then – to the European Union. Today, the four Visegrad countries continue to be linked together on the basis of friendship and good neighborly cooperation, of common understanding of the similar social and economic interests and challenges, of regional solidarity, and of the achievement of the common goals of the European and the foreign policy1. The objective of this expert report is to provide a brief historical retrospective of the establishment of the Visegrad Group, with a focus on the context of cooperation between the four Central European countries, as well as on the specific areas in which this cooperation has been essential in shaping particular EU policies. Last but not least, the cooperation of these four countries with Bulgaria is also going to be considered here, along with the future opportunities for its further development with a view of addressing the common EU challenges. History and establishment of the Visegrad Group The Visegrad Group (the VG) or the Visegrad Four (the V4) was set up on February 15, 1991, by the former president of Czechoslovakia – Václav Havel, the Polish president – Lech Walesa, and the Hungarian Prime Minister – József Antall. This group was originally referred to as the “troika”, while two years later following the break-up of Czechoslovakia, it became known as the Visegrad 1 30 years of Visegrad cooperation, a congratulatory message on the part of the Polish Presidency, at https://www.gov.pl/web/bulgaria-bg/rocznica-wspolpracy-wyszehradzkiej 179 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION Four. The emergence of this group had been motivated by the social reforms taking place in these countries after 1989, by the desire to say “goodbye” to the communist past, and by the participation of the said countries in the European integration process. In the initial period (1991-1993), this group had an important part in launching negotiations for EU and NATO membership in the four countries. In the years that followed, however, this cooperation failed in intensity in favor of the four countries’ individual efforts to join the Euro-Atlantic structures. The V4 activity was revitalized in 1998 as the group started holding regular meetings of the negotiating teams, as well as summits and some other formats aimed at protecting the common interests and strengthening the role of the Visegrad Group in Europe. After 1998, all the four countries joined NATO progressively (the Czech Republic, Poland and Hungary in 1999, and Slovakia in 2004) and then the European Union (all four together, as part of the EU 2004 major enlargement). Following the accession of the four countries to NATO and the EU, the interaction within the VG focused on: strengthening regional cooperation, the consultations and the coordinated action on topics important for the countries and the region of Central and Eastern Europe (the CEE region); upholding common or similar positions within the EU and enhancing the CEE role in Europe, the Euro-Atlantic structures and the international organizations; and improving their cooperation with the neighboring countries. The Visegrad Group is not institutionalized. Its only institutional element is the Visegrad Fund with its headquarters in Bratislava, which was established in 2000 upon the initiative of Slovakia. The main objective of this fund is to encourage regional cooperation within the V4, between the V4 members, and with other countries – mainly from the Western Balkans and the Eastern Partnership, as well as cooperation between the citizens, the NGOs, the public and the educational institutions and organizations by implementing varied projects and awarding grants. For its 20 years of existence, this fund has implemented some 6000 projects in the four countries and throughout the Western Balkans and the Eastern Partnership, worth a total of 100 million €2. Priority cooperation areas and contribution to the formulation of the EU policies An interesting element of the above-mentioned cooperation is that the Visegrad Group is a discussion format on consensus topics for which reason its agenda excludes any issues on which the four countries have different opinions. In the latter case, each one of them begins looking for partners for the achievement of its own goals and this flexibility is assessed as being a strong quality3. What are, in fact, the main differences between the four VG countries? The European observers often refer to the Visegrad Four as a “2+2” format, emphasizing in this way their different attitudes towards European integration or the so-called “differentiated integration”. On one hand, the Czech Republic and Slovakia are relatively pro-European while, on the other hand, Poland and 2 Information on the Visegrad Group compiled by the Bilateral European Cooperation Directorate of the Bulgarian Ministry of Foreign Affairs, June 2021. 3 Ibid 180 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION Hungary are much more Eurosceptic. Out of the four countries, only Slovakia is a Eurozone member4. Among the significant differences between the four countries has been their attitude towards some other European partner-countries or allies. Germany is seen by the VG as its most important partner-country within the EU but each of the four countries is divided over the nature of such relations. The Czech Republic and Slovakia consider these relations positive, while Poland and Hungary consider them neutral. In addition, the Czech Republic considers Austria a close ally, Poland is oriented towards Great Britain, and Slovakia is oriented towards France. For its part, Hungary is the only VG country maintaining active relations with Russia, which are expressed by the close contacts between the Hungarian Prime Minister – Viktor Orban, and the Russian President – Vladimir Putin. Another difference is the attitude of the four countries towards the scenario concerning the future development of Europe at different speeds. All four countries consider this scenario to be the most likely one but, with the exception of Slovakia, the other three consider it unfavorable. Despite their differences and the fact that they belong to different political groups within the EU, a specific topic has united the VG in recent years, i.e. the migration and refugee issue. All four of them reject totally the fixed refugee distribution quotas in the EU and this position of theirs has largely resulted in the EU inability to make any progress in reforming its own legislation in this area. In this context, the individual positions of the four countries deserve due attention. Hungary does not want to become an immigrant country – a position that has been repeatedly emphasized by Prime Minister Viktor Orban, who has set up a dividing fence along the border with Serbia and Croatia. In 2015, more than 170 000 people applied for asylum in Hungary, and in 2017 the figure was a little under 3400. Viktor Orban has been one of the harshest critics of former Chancellor Angela Merkel’s refugee policy. The former Czech Prime Minister – Andrej Babiš, has also been a staunch opponent of the EU solidarity with the refugee policy. In 2018, he declared that his country was not going to accept “any illegal migrants”. Just like Hungary and the Czech Republic, Poland has refused flatly to accept the refugee distribution quota – an issue that has still been causing a lot of tension. Last but not least, Slovakia is the only V4 country that has agreed to accept small contingents of refugees and, unlike the other three countries, it has managed to avoid criminal proceedings by the European Union. However, in 2018, Slovakia voted jointly with Hungary, Poland, and the Czech Republic to reject the UN Migration Pact. The then Slovak Prime Minister – Peter Pellegrini, stated that “Slovakia does not agree that there is no difference between legal and illegal migration; this country believes that economic migration is illegal and damaging and poses a risk to security“5. 4 Harper, Jo, Visegrad Group: A new economic heart of Europe? 05.07.2019, https://www.dw.com/en/ visegrad-group-a-new-economic-heart-of-europe/a-49483505 5 Klein, Rahel, This is how the Visegrad Group Works, 07.02.2019, https://www.dw.com/en/this-is-how-the- visegrad-group-works/a-47402724 181 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION In fact, what is the economic potential of the four Visegrad countries? If the Visegrad Four were a single nation state, this state would have been the fifth largest economy in Europe and the 12th largest in the world. Its total population of 64 million people would have placed it 22nd in the world and 4th in Europe, respectively, with the corresponding distribution between the four countries: 38 million in Poland, 11 million in the Czech Republic, nearly 10 million in Hungary, and about 5.5 million in Slovakia. The 2019 forecasts had been that the economies of Europe’s 27 member states were going to increase by only 1.4 % and the economies of the Eurozone’s 19 member states – by 1.2 %. Meanwhile, the economies of the Central European countries are among the fastest growing in the EU. With an economy of 525 billion dollars (475 billion €) and a steady growth of an average of 4.2 % per year between the years 1992-2019, Poland has been successfully catching up with Western Europe. One of the most important features of the Polish economy is the large domestic consumer market, which accounts for 61 % of its gross domestic product (GDP), thus exceeding the EU average. In terms of structural investment, Poland used to be the largest beneficiary of EU funds in the 2007-2013 and the 2014-2020 periods, with 102 and 106 billion € respectively. Despite this boom, Poland has one of the largest fiscal deficits in Eastern Europe. Labor shortages are also expected and are already being felt by more than half of the Polish companies. There are also some negative demographic trends in Poland, including the relocation of workers to the richer EU countries accompanied by one of the lowest birth rates in Europe. Ranked by the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) as the fastest growing economy in the world, Slovakia ranks just behind its Polish neighbor, with a GDP growth forecast of 3.8 % in 2019 and 3.4 % in 2020; and with 4.1 % growth in 2018. The Slovakian automotive industry accounts for 44 % of its industrial production, as well as for 40 % of this country’s exports. With a population of 10.6 million people, the results of the Czech economy are the most disappointing within the VG, with growth of only 2.6 % in 2019 and 2.4 % in 2020. The Czech Republic has been an EU member since 1 May 2004, and is also part of the Schengen area. However, it has so far opposed the adoption of the Euro. Highly dependent on its car production and exports to the Eurozone, the Czech economy recovered from a long crisis in 2013 and now boasts the lowest unemployment rate in the EU at just 2.2 %. The former Managing Director of the International Monetary Fund (IMF) – Christine Lagarde, often gives the Czech Republic as an example when warning of the dangers of the US tariffs in relation to the European goods, despite the very low exports of automotive components from the Czech Republic to the United States. According to a European Commission forecast, today Hungary ranks 5th as the fastest growing economy in the EU, with a 3.7 % growth in 2019. With an inflation rate of 3.2 %, Hungary has recorded the second fastest growing prices after Romania (3.6 %) and more than twice the EU average. The ongoing global trade war between the United States and China remains of particular concern to Hungary, which relies heavily on foreign trade. Generally speaking and despite the existing problems, the Visegrad countries 182 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION continue to have a key role with respect to the German exporters and, according to the Centre for European Reform, the V4 collectively managed to become Germany’s most important trading partner in 2017, ahead of China and the US, due to their integration into the European supply chains6. The coordination of the VG activities is formally carried out based on a rotating presidency of each Member State for a period of 1 year. Regular meetings are held between the prime ministers, the foreign ministers and the other line ministers of the four countries, as well as in wider formats with some other European countries, with the ultimate objective of setting up a strong lobby for the Central and Eastern Europe region in the EU and NATO and of striving to equalize the weight of this region with that of Western Europe. Since the EU accession negotiations, the VG countries have been involved in the debate on the future of Europe. During the Hungarian Presidency of the Visegrad Group (2017-2018), a declaration of the Heads of Government on the future of Europe was adopted. In this declaration, they reiterated the views that they had previously expressed in similar declarations before the summits in Bratislava and Rome, and reaffirmed their support for the Declaration and the Bratislava Roadmap (2016), as well as for the Rome Declaration (2017). To a large extent, these positions continue to be valid in relation to the Conference on the Future of Europe, with some additions related to the current challenges and developments within the EU. The VG countries have also been among the leading ones in the area of putting an end to the double quality of goods in the EU, starting with the quality of food. They have also joined efforts to put an end to the practice of offering goods with the same designation, but different composition, for the countries of Western and Eastern Europe. The dominant topic during the VG Czech Presidency (2015-2016) involved the efforts to deal with the refugee crisis. Their strong opposition to the refugee distribution mandatory quotas managed to put these countries in the focus of public attention. While the success of the Czech Presidency has been mostly due to the countries’ strong common position on building the Energy Union, on the energy security issues, on the deepening of the single market, etc. In the period July 1, 2017 – July 1, 2018, the rotating VG Presidency was held by Hungary while the key issues on the agenda included: the withdrawal of the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland from the EU; the debate on the future of Europe, including the social dimension with a significant impact on the European competitiveness; the debate on the EU Multiannual Financial Framework (MFF) after the year 2020; the cohesion policy and the common agricultural policy (CAP). At a Ministers of Foreign Affairs’ meeting (held on December 4, 2017) in a “V4+” format (including Bulgaria, Romania, Greece, Croatia, and Slovenia), some messages were sent in relation to the full recovery of Schengen (including support for membership of Bulgaria and Romania); diversification of the energy sources in the CEE, and an European energy policy 6 Harper, Jo, Visegrad Group: A new economic heart of Europe? 05.07.2019, https://www.dw.com/en/ visegrad-group-a-new-economic-heart-of-europe/a-49483505 183 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION without double standards; support for the candidate countries and accelerated enlargement of the EU; and support (excluding Serbia) for the priorities of the Bulgarian Presidency of the Council of the EU on the integration of the Western Balkans (the WB). At a meeting of the V4 Prime Ministers (held on January 26, 2018), a joint declaration was adopted emphasizing the importance of the following issues: deepening of European integration in some areas, such as the economic and the monetary union; restoring the proper functioning of Schengen; protection and development of the Single Market based on the four fundamental freedoms, including the free movement of workers and services; a commitment to a further EU enlargement to the Western Balkans and assisting the Eastern Partnership countries on their path to the European standards; a strong EU by respecting the identities and the individual characteristics of its Member States and their equal treatment by the EU institutions by leaving them to carry out their tasks strictly, on the basis of their competences defined by the Treaties; leaving the EU Member States to further carry out the internal reforms within their powers; strengthening the EU internal and global competitiveness; adequate funding of the existing cohesion policies, the CAP in particular. The V4 position on the migration policy was also reflected, including: protection of the external borders; separation of the asylum seekers from the economic migrants; cooperation between the countries of origin and the transit countries; sustainable consensus and a comprehensive approach to the migration and asylum policy. On July 1, 2018, Slovakia took over the VG Presidency, with some of the following key priorities in mind: strengthening the European unity on the basis of the V4 strong positions on the following issues: the Multiannual Financial Framework; Security; the Cohesion Policy; the CAP; Brexit; intensive cooperation in a “V4+ format”; support for the EU enlargement; finding a lasting solution to migration; and stimulating investment7. On July 1, 2020, Poland took over the VG Presidency under the motto “Back on track”, which was aimed at restoring the normal functioning of public life within the Visegrad region and compensating for the damages caused by the COVID-19 pandemic. The major objectives of this particular presidency boiled down to the following: a strong VG; return to normality; and further development of the contacts between people8. In the context of the COVID-19 pandemic, the European Commission turned out to be entirely helpless in the fight against this particular virus as it had to face the dictates of the major pharmaceutical companies. Having once received huge funding in order to speed up their work on the vaccines, along with huge orders from the European community countries, these corporations began all of a sudden to treat the relevant contracts as declarative rather than as mandatory. In this way, the EU Member States found themselves virtually 7 Information on the Visegrad Group compiled by the Bilateral European Cooperation Directorate of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, June 2021. 8 The priorities of the Polish VG Presidency, 01.07.2020, https://www.gov.pl/web/bulgaria-bg/prioritetite- na-polskoto-predsedatelstvo-v4 184 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION alone in this fight9. On October 9, 2020, a virtual center for monitoring and tracking the spread of the COVID-19 pandemic was set up, funded by the Visegrad Group countries. Its aim was to improve the exchange of information and experience and to facilitate control over the spread of the infection10. In addition, the VG countries are said to have called for a much wider and faster access to the COVID-19 vaccines within the EU. Currently, Hungary has been the only EU member state to widely use the Russian Sputnik-V vaccine, in Slovakia, the vaccine has been approved but given to a very limited population, and Czech authorities are still considering such a move.11. The VG countries have also been having an important role in relation to the new European Union Multiannual Financial Framework (MFF) (2021-2027).
