In November 2020, Poland and Hungary blocked the adoption of the MFF and of the EU Economic Recovery Plan after COVID-19; they also blocked the adoption of the “own resources system” on the assertion that they do not approve of the new conditions binding the access to the EU funds with the rule of law. The total EU budget for the coming years amounts to some 1.1 trillion €, and the Recovery Plan – to an additional 750 billion €. Poland and Hungary remain the two EU member states that have been most criticized in relation to the independence of the judiciary and the rule of law in their countries. Unfortunately, the European institutions have been including lately Bulgaria in the same “batch” with similar criticism12. Eventually, this package covering seven cost areas was adopted on 16 December 2020. With regards to the cohesion policy area, i.e. one of the EU emblematic policies, the trend has not changed as the Visegrad Group countries continue to be among the leaders in terms of receiving the highest amounts of the EU funds1314. All the Visegrad Group countries have declared their unconditional support for the European and Euro-Atlantic perspective of the Western Balkan countries. In this context, they rendered support to Bulgaria during the country’s first Presidency of the Council of the European Union in 2018, to put the issue of the European future of the Western Balkan countries back onto the EU Agenda. At the same time, quite surprisingly, the Czech Republic and Slovakia blocked the adoption of the EU Council conclusions on the expected forthcoming enlargement, which contain certain texts on the importance of the agreements concluded between 9 Central Europe in 2021: The 30th Anniversary of the Visegrad Group. This was done for the purpose of formulating some preventive measures and defense in case of escalation of the tensions along their Eastern borders.17. Last but not least, the VG countries have also voiced some reservations as regards the European Green Deal and the proposal for a climate-neutral Europe until 2050, bearing in mind the fact that they do not rank among the champions in this respect. The Czech energy and heating systems are still dependent on coal. The Czech government remains committed to nuclear energy, as is the case of Hungary, too. However, the lack of support for the EU climate objectives may hamper Poland’s access to the relevant finance and, therefore, make it more difficult for this country to achieve green transformation; as the Polish economy has remained the most dependent one within the EU in this respect, while 36 of the 50 most polluted cities in the EU are to be also found in Poland. On the other hand, Slovakia was the first V4 country to openly support the objective of carbon neutrality. In addition, it was also among the first EU countries to publish a framework document, which can serve as a roadmap for the national recovery plan 18. To sum it up, the Visegrad countries share common positions on: cooperation in the field of home affairs; the EU Pact on Migration and Asylum; military cooperation within the framework of the EU Common Foreign and Security Policy; energy security and diversification of the energy sources; the question of the future of Europe, enlargement, the common agricultural policy, the cohesion policy, health, etc. On all these issues, the four countries have been seeking to formulate key policies within the EU. 15 Dimitrov, Deyan. They share the strong common position that a multi- speed Europe is unacceptable. Within the EU, Bulgaria and the VG have joined efforts on a number of issues related to convergence and the equal treatment of the Member States, to the protection of the central role of the cohesion policy in the MFF, to migration, the enlargement, energy and climate, etc. The Visegrad countries and Bulgaria have also been having similar positions on the climate policy. Following the European Commission’s communication published on September 16, 2020, they expressed their concerns about the unacceptably high increase in the 2030 target (from 40 % to 55 % carbon emissions reduction) and stressed the need for the creation of the so-called facilitating framework that could maintain the competitiveness of the European industry and ensure a fair and smooth economic and social transition19. Bulgaria participates in the “V4+” meetings at varied levels. In 2014, among some other common documents between the VG countries, Bulgaria and Romania signed a document on a ministerial level in order to coordinate the positions of the six countries in the regional dimension of the cohesion policy. Bulgaria, Poland, Hungary, Lithuania, Latvia, Malta, Romania, Cyprus and Estonia are in the group of the so-called “like-minded countries”, which regularly receive démarches in the process of discussing and adopting the Mobility I Package. In response to a letter from the Bulgarian Minister of Transport, the Polish party supported the coordination efforts of the country along the lines of preparing appeals to the European Court of Justice in order to repeal some of the provisions in this package. The Visegrad Group countries and Bulgaria have a great potential for the development of connectivity and digitalization within the Three Seas Initiative, whose Sixth Summit and Business Forum were hosted by Bulgaria on July 8 and 9, 2021. In the context of the indicated priority areas of interaction between Bulgaria and the Visegrad Four, the following recommendations could be made: • Given that Bulgaria is an external border of the EU, the cooperation between defense and home affairs, as well as regarding the protection of external borders and Frontex, deserve further attention and the cooperation itself could be further deepened. • The parties could also deepen their cooperation in the field of health care and their own national recovery plans in order to better address the negative effects of the COVID-19 pandemic and work together to distribute the vaccines more evenly across the EU and the Western Balkan countries. 19 Information on the Visegrad Group compiled by the Bilateral European Cooperation Directorate of the Bulgarian Ministry of foreign Affairs, June 2021. 187 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION • As cooperation deepens, there is a potential for some countries, such as Bulgaria and Romania, to seek additional opportunities in defense of their own interests, as well as to pursue common policies in the EU together with the V4 countries. • Given the fact that Bulgaria and some of the Visegrad Group countries’ economies remain heavily dependent on coal and on some of the other carbon energy sources, they could coordinate their efforts towards diversification and finding an alternative; as well as towards renewable energy sources where they could even come up with some general conditions in the process of negotiating the future energy projects. • Additional attention could be paid to the defense sector in order to deepen cooperation, given the geographical location of the countries on NATO’s Eastern flank. • Given the similar positions of Bulgaria and the VG countries regarding the European perspective of the Western Balkans, it would be appropriate for Bulgaria to work actively in order to further clarify and seek support for our own positions in relation to the case of the Republic of Northern Macedonia. • Bulgaria could seek more actively the support of the Visegrad Group countries with a view of implementing our main priorities, including the Schengen membership, the Eurozone, and the OECD. Conclusion 30 years since its inception, the Visegrad Group has become an important factor within the EU – a factor that has become indispensable in the formation of the key EU policies. However, a factor whose voice is heard clearly is in a position to also impose things and even sometimes to go so far as to come into direct clash and confrontation with the common European values b y aiming to oppose some leading EU countries, such as France and Germany. Having gone through a great number of historical vicissitudes and varied stages along their road to European and Euro- Atlantic integration, Poland, the Czech Republic, Slovakia and Hungary have become today important economic factors, with a potential to even compete in the coming years with the leading European economies, while minimizing at the same time the damages inflicted by the COVID-19 pandemic. In the context of the overall VG policy, expressing zealously and defending its own voice and positions in the EU, Bulgaria, having an awful lot of similar interests with the four Central European countries, could actively seek to deepen cooperation with them in order to help boost the region’s weight in the process of formulating and implementing the key EU policies. History of development of the Three Seas Initiative The historical development of the Central and Eastern European countries after World War II unfolds under varied political, economic and social conditions within the framework of these countries’ diverse national, cultural and religious traditions. Irrespective of that, the countries of this region have a common element in their past – the Iron Curtain, which divided the European continent in the middle of the 20th century, thus hindering, for half a century, the natural progress, the economic growth and the unity of the European peoples and severing the link between the Central and the Eastern parts of the European continent. Currently, while the Western European countries remain more closely connected via highways, railway lines, power lines and pipelines, the Central and Eastern European countries remain still relatively isolated from one another due to the lack of modern connecting infrastructure and the missing investment to build such infrastructure. This problem is felt most acutely along the North-South axis of the Central and Eastern European region. Integration within the European Union (the EU) has laid the foundations for the stable development of the European continent, but the countries of Central and Eastern Europe are still lagging significantly behind their Western and economic partners in the field of economy and infrastructure. Despite the common EU policies, these development disparities are still to be addressed. The gap between Western and Eastern Europe in terms of investment in infrastructure, energy, and digitalization is estimated to have reached 1.5 trillion €. If this deficit is reduced, the development of this region will receive a huge boost and the whole European continent will become more united and much stronger. In 2004, the issue of inequalities in Europe’s development was highlighted in a US Atlantic Council report entitled “Completing Europe”. The idea of overcoming this deficit was the major driving force that inspired the then presidents of Croatia and Poland – Kolinda Grabar-Kitarovic and Andrzej Duda, to come up with a new regional initiative – The Three Seas Initiative, involving the countries between the Baltic, the Black and the Adriatic seas with a view of attracting the investors’ interest and speeding up the development of this region. This idea was endorsed in 2015 as a political and economic project on a heads of state level. Among the major objectives of this Initiative was to enhance the economic convergence between the countries of this region and the Western European countries via the development of transport, the digital and energy infrastructure and connectivity. The project relies on the active cooperation with the European Commission, Germany and the United States, which are considered the strategic partners of this Initiative. 189 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION 2. The Three Seas Initiative. Format The Three Seas Initiative (the 3SI/TSI Initiative), also known as the Baltic, Adriatic and Black Sea Initiative (the BABS), Trimarium in Latin or simply – The Three Seas, is a group of twelve EU Member States, including: Austria, Bulgaria, Croatia, the Czech Republic, Estonia, Hungary, Latvia, Lithuania, Poland, Romania, Slovakia, and Slovenia.1 The territorial scope of the Three Seas participating countries covers almost one third of the EU area, with a population of 111 million European citizens. At the same time, the average economic growth rate of the twelve countries in the period 2015-2019 was 3.5 %, while that of the EU was 2.1 % 3. Objectives The Three Seas Initiative countries share a number of common objectives, in brief: economic growth, security, and building a stronger and more cohesive Europe, namely:  Economic growth: The main objective of the 3SI Initiative is to stimulate economic growth and prosperity in the region. The increased dynamism in trade and in the exchange of services between the 3SI countries and their neighbors is also going to create a better environment for growth and will be in support of Europe’s global competitiveness.  Attracting much better the interest of the investors: Among the EU Member States, the 3SI countries have had, in recent years, higher than the average economic growth rates and, for this reason, they have been offering the investors higher rates of return. Through joint action and the implementation of common policies, these countries are going to make this region much more attractive on the world financial market.  Energy security: Energy is not just an economic issue. It is important for the strategic development of the region and for the region’s security. Setting up a well-connected and efficiently functioning energy market, with a possibility for the participants to choose their suppliers, is going to stimulate free competition and ensure that the region is better supplied with high energy security.  Geopolitics: The stable economic development of Europe and in the Three Seas region respectively, is going to contribute to a more effective protection of the participating countries’ interests and will, at the same time, enable these countries to respond to the common challenges.  Smart connectivity: When it comes to new investments, the goal is to make the most of the region’s experience and potential for the implementation of digital solutions. This, however, goes far beyond the development of digital 1 All twelve 3SI countries are EU Member States and (with the exception of Austria) also NATO countries and are located along the Eastern flank of the Alliance. Six out of these member countries (Austria, Croatia, the Czech Republic, Hungary, Slovakia and Slovenia) also belong to the CEDC (the Central European Defense Cooperation) set up in 2010 for the purpose of further developing and strengthening the defense cooperation among the Member States. Four out of these member countries (Hungary, Poland, the Czech Republic and Slovakia) have also formed a cultural and political union, known since 1991 as the Visegrad Group or the Visegrad Four. All 3SI countries (with the exception of Austria) used to be communist countries under the influence of the Soviet Union and were part of the former socialist group known as the Eastern Block. Seven out of these member countries (Hungary, Romania, the Czech Republic, Slovakia, Latvia, Estonia and Lithuania) were part of Poland’s Intermarium Plan by Jozef Pilsudski, who, after the end of World War I, offered a federation to the Baltic countries. 190 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION infrastructure alone and involves the formulation of intelligent and modern data exchange solutions, as well as a more efficient use of the information.  Helping achieve the climate goals: If the 3SI countries adopt a unified approach in line with the objectives of the EU Green Deal for the development of a new modern infrastructure, this is going to contribute to the reduction of carbon emissions and the achievement of carbon neutrality. Launched as a platform for the exchange of ideas between its member states, the 3SI has further evolved to include today an annual business forum and an investment fund; while the executive branch of the twelve countries has been increasingly involved in the Initiative. 4. Three Seas Initiative Investment Fund In order to realize the significant potential of the Three Seas countries and to support the implementation of their projects in the field of transport and energy and in the digital and information technologies, these countries will have to join efforts in order to overcome the lack of investment. It is for this purpose that the Three Seas Summits and accompanying international business forums are held once every year and a Three Seas Initiative Investment Fund (3SIIF) has been established as an economic dimension of the Initiative and a financial mechanism for the implementation of projects in this region. The Luxembourg-based 3SI Investment Fund, established in June 2019, during the Fourth 3SI Summit in Ljubljana, Slovenia, is a key tool for the implementation of the 3SI infrastructure projects. This Fund is going to finance the projects proposed and approved by the 3SI participating countries. To date, nine 3SI Member States have announced their contributions to the Investment Fund. Poland has made the largest financial commitment by contributing 750 million € to the fund. The rest of the countries participate in the Investment Fund with a minimum contribution of 20 million €, while the contribution of Slovenia thus far has been 23 million €. The Czech Republic, Slovakia, and Austria have not yet contributed to the 3SI Investment Fund. In November 2020, the US Congress reaffirmed unanimously its bipartisan support to the Three Seas Initiative, which seems to be undoubtedly a priority for the new US administration. The US support to the 3SI was also confirmed in the Joint Statement of the Bulgarian former Prime Minister Boyko Borissov, and the US former President Donald Trump dated November 25, 2019. The objective of the 3SI Investment Fund, which operates entirely on a commercial basis, is to support the implementation of infrastructure projects in Central and Eastern Europe (the CEE). The Fund had been designed to invest in the energy, the transport and the digital infrastructure of the EU CEE Member States bordering on the Baltic, the Adriatic and the Black Sea, where the need for long-term investment in the national infrastructure had been among the most pressing ones within the EU. The projects subject to financing have to receive the respective capital investment through the sale of some, or of all their shares. These projects have to be viable in order to generate a long-term return on investment, i.e. it has to have a high financial return (between 13 and 15 %), and 191 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION the amount of investment can range between 50 and 250 million €. To ensure transparency, however, the selection and the approval of investment has to be carried out by the investment adviser of the regional initiative – the international fund of independent investment management specialists called the Amber Infrastructure Group, and the Fund Manager. It is important to note here that the Investment Fund does not provide any loans or grants. As through its financing, it acquires partially or entirely specific infrastructure, the proceeds of which go directly to the Investment Fund. According to the analysis of Kai-Olaf Lang2 entitled “The Three Seas Initiative – Economic Cooperation in a Geostrategic Context“, published by the Science and Politics Foundation (Stiftung Wissenschaft und Politik)3, there have been two major conclusions drawn from the Fifth 3SI Summit held in 2020 in Tallinn, as follows: 1) an increase in the 3SI Investment Fund, in which 9 of the 12 3SI countries participate, to almost 1 billion €. 2) a continuing and enhanced US commitment in political and financial terms. The United States have announced that they were going to provide $ 300 million via the International Development Finance Corporation. For the time being, however, meeting of this commitment is being delayed by the new US administration, and it is even been rumored that some of these funds are going to be granted as loans. A little earlier, in February 2021, Washington had officially declared that it was ready to invest in this Fund up to $ 1 billion, provided that these American funds account for 30 % of the contributions of the participating 3SI countries. While on its part, the US EXIM-Bank declared its preparedness to financially support the 3SI projects in addition to funds coming from the International Development Finance Corporation. 5. The 3SI summits Each and every year, the 3SI brings together the heads of state of its 12 participating countries, i.e. Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Poland, the Czech Republic, Slovakia, Hungary, Slovenia, Austria, Croatia, Romania, and Bulgaria. To date, six 3SI summits have been held with the participation of the Member States’ presidents and representatives of the United States and the European Union, most of which have been accompanied by a Business Forum. The 6th 3SI summit was held in Sofia on July 8 and 9, 2021, after Bulgaria had been “handed over” the hosting of the next 3SI summit by Estonia.  The first 3SI Summit, held in 2016 in Dubrovnik The first 3SI summit in Dubrovnik (Croatia) took place on August 25-26, 2016. It was chaired by the then Croatian President – Kolinda Grabar-Kitarovic. The participating countries reached an agreement that the economic cooperation 2 A political scientist with the prestigious German Science and Politics Foundation. 3 Stiftung Wissenschaft und Politik is an independent scientific institution providing consultations to the political leaders (the German Bundestag and the German Federal Government) on foreign policy, security policy, international and European policy. The SWP is one of the largest think tanks in Europe. 192 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION should boost the economic activity, first and foremost, in the energy and the infrastructure sectors. The two-day event ended with a declaration of cooperation on the economic issues, focusing on energy, transport, and the communication infrastructure.  The second 3SI Summit, held in 2017 in Warsaw The second summit of the Three Seas Initiative was held on July 6-7, 2017, in Warsaw (Poland) and was chaired by the Polish President, Andrzej Duda. The presence and the speech made by the US President – Donald Trump, were described as indicative, as this had been first visit to Europe of the then- elected president, breaking the tradition of visiting first Berlin, London or Paris at the beginning of the presidential term. The participating countries agreed unanimously to establish a Three Seas Business Forum.  The third 3SI Summit, held in 2018 in Bucharest The third 3SI summit was held on 17-18 September, 2018, in Bucharest (Romania). The participants approved a list of priority connectivity projects in three key areas including transport, energy, and digital information. The European Commission President – Jean-Claude Juncker, the German Foreign Minister – Heiko Maas, and the US Secretary for Energy – Rick Perry, attended the meeting. Within the framework of this meeting, the first meeting of the Business Forum also took place. Further, a network of the 3SI Chambers of Commerce was set up and a letter of intent was signed for the establishment of the Three Seas Investment Fund.  The fourth 3SI Summit, held in 2019 in Ljubljana The fourth summit was held on June 5-6, 2019 in Ljubljana (Slovenia) and was attended by the President of the European Commission – Jean-Claude Juncker, the German President – Frank-Walter Steinmeier, and the US Secretary of Energy – Rick Perry. President Rumen Radev, who appealed to those present to get out of politics faster in order to concentrate on the economy and the particular decisions, represented Bulgaria at this summit. “We would like to have clear principles for the formation of the Investment Fund capital, as well as clear criteria for defining the priority projects, which should meet our national interest” were the words of the Bulgarian president.  The fifth 3SI Summit, held in 2020 in Tallinn In October 2020, the Estonian President, Kersti Kaljulaid, hosted the 3SI summit in Tallinn (Estonia). As this meeting was held during the COVID-19 pandemic, most of the 3SI countries’ heads of state participated remotely. At a symbolic ceremony in Tallinn, the Estonian President handed over the hosting of the next 3SI summit to the Bulgarian President Rumen Radev who received it by saying: “Bulgaria is not going to focus on the challenges of the present alone. Our ambition is to focus on the challenges of the future and to find common solutions to these challenges. We are going to further develop the capacity of the 3SI 193 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION Initiative and turn it into a real tool for cooperation and a speeded-up development of this region by improving the energy, the transport and the digital connectivity.” After the end of the summit in Tallinn, the Bulgarian President added that as a 3SI host country Bulgaria is going to further promote the political dialogue between the participating countries to the highest possible level. Jointly with some other countries, Bulgaria will continue to develop the relevant mechanisms with a view of improving the Three Seas decision-making process. Our biggest challenges involve the definition of project winning criteria and the introduction of clear mechanisms for granting 3SI funding. President Radev also called for a faster transition from political speaking to genuine action. According to him, the 3SI countries could achieve an even distribution of the 3SI resources if they manage to introduce sustainable links throughout the region between the universities, the research institutes and the research units consisting of young people. Therefore, one of our priorities as a host country is going to involve the development of the educational and scientific connectivity. The ultimate objective being to come across intelligent future solutions in the field of transport, energy, and science on the basis of joint projects developed by the research teams and the young people from the 3SI region.  The sixth 3SI Summit, held in 2021 in Sofia Playing host to the 3SI 2021 Summit, the ultimate objective of the country was to contribute to the upgrading of the previous achievements and to their further consolidation. Working for the full realization of the potential of the Three Seas Initiative, the process of preparation in the country began with two important government decisions as follows: Council of Ministers Decision (CMD) No 613 of September 9, 2020, endorsed the participation of the Bulgarian Development Bank (the BDB) in the 3SI Investment Fund with a contribution of 20 million €; and Council of Ministers Decision No 629 of September 3, 2020 (amended and supplemented by CMD No 429 of May 27, 2021), endorsed the appointment of a 3SI Bulgarian National Coordinator. By December 2020, the Bulgarian Development Bank had accomplished the procedure of joining the 3SI Investment Fund, which had made the BDB an official 3SI Class A shareholder with a cash contribution of 20 million €. The Bulgarian preparations for holding the 6th 3SI Summit in Sofia involved a wide range of activities, workshops and some others, including the economic aspects of the accompanying Business Forum. A Council of Ministers Decision No 629 of September 3, 2020, endorsed setting up of an Interdepartmental Working Group for Bulgaria’s preparations for hosting the 6th 3SI Summit. Meetings of the group composed of the so-called “Sherpas” from the individual participating countries’ presidents’ administrations were held, which discussed the major objectives of the forthcoming summit, a working calendar of the meetings was compiled, there were discussions on the possible establishment of a 3SI Secretariat, along with discussions on the draft Joint Final Declaration and the 3SI Communication Strategy proposed by Bulgaria. There was also a video conference of the 3SI participating countries’ National Coordinators, as well as a couple of virtual conferences organized by 194 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION the Bulgarian Development Bank on the funding opportunities from the Three Seas Investment Fund; and others. The topic of the Sofia Summit was “The Three Seas Initiative: A path to stronger, united and competitive Europe”. In his capacity of playing host to this forum, the President of the Republic of Bulgaria – Rumen Radev, welcomed in Sofia the Heads of State and heads of delegations from the 12 participating countries, along with the representatives of the 3SI partner countries, namely the USA, the Federal Republic of Germany, and the European Commission; the presidents of Germany, Estonia, Latvia, Poland, and Slovenia; the Prime Minister of Croatia; the Deputy Prime Ministers of the Czech Republic and Slovakia; the Deputy Minister of Foreign Affairs of Austria. The guests of the Sofia 3SI Summit included the Managing Director of the International Monetary Fund – Kristalina Georgieva, and the Greek President – Katerina Sakellaropoulou. There were also two video addresses made by the US President – Joseph Biden, and the Lithuanian President – Gitanas Nauseda. On July 8, the Business Forum, which was held in parallel with the Sofia 3SI Summit, opened with a presidential panel on “The Three Seas Initiative: Road to smart development and comprehensive economic growth”. Adhering to the continuity of the 3SI Summits, and the results achieved that far, the Bulgarian party concentrated on the following main areas:  a significant improvement of the energy, transport and digital connectivity between the 3SI countries located along this region’s North-South axis, owing to: the introduction of modern infrastructure cross-border connections; ensuring security and diversification of the energy supplies; fast transport communications, and horizontal digitalization;  the compatibility and complementarity of the projects with the processes of convergence and cohesion within the EU and their harmonization with the EU sectoral policies;  the introduction of an attractive investment and business climate based on the principles of the free market, transparency and competitiveness, with a view to attracting to this region fresh strategic investments and private capital;  the provision of favorable conditions for the economic development of the adjacent Black Sea and Danube regions;  enhancement of the direct business contacts between the 3SI countries in the field of innovation, new technologies and applied research. The Sofia 3SI Summit ended with signing of a joint declaration. This declaration was an expression of the common political will for the rapid development of the countries in the region by improving the transport, energy and infrastructure connectivity. The principle of promoting public-private partnerships and new innovative development models has been maintained. The declaration was meant to open up new opportunities for cooperation, both with the EU and with some other regional and global partners, with whom we share common values. It was for the first time, that the need to develop R&D in the field of hydrogen and make the transition to a climate-neutral energy sector was mentioned in this 195 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION declaration. Further on, the need to further strengthen the 3SI involvement in the EU policies and transatlantic relations had been recognized, thus facilitating the implementation of the 3SI projects. The idea to develop a duo format had been born, which includes two consecutive programs for the host countries to work on the 3SI common goals. As with the cooperation and the dialogue between the parties making progress, this format could be imposed. 6. The 3SI between economics and geopolitics The geopolitical significance of the 3SI is indisputable, as it outlines, on one hand, a region with great opportunities for development and a real chance to catch up economically with the Western European countries; on the other hand, it is an area where a great number of interests collide. According to an analysis published by the Science and Politics Foundation of Mr. Kai-Olaf Lang, i.e. “The Three Seas Initiative – Economic Cooperation in a Geostrategic Context“4, we could differentiate here between the following three individual approaches:  The Polish party believes that the 3SI economic aspects and geopolitical dimensions could lead to the reduction of the region’s vulnerability to Russia and the restraint of a possible economic and political supremacy on the part of Germany. The prominent US presence in the Three Seas Initiative, which is also seen as a counterweight to the German influence, has made a decisive contribution to the achievement of these objectives.  For the Baltic States and Romania, which are critical of Russia and have a strong transatlantic orientation, one of the most attractive aspects of the 3SI is the involvement of the integration of the United States and the reduction of the dependence on Russia. These countries reject categorically the restriction of Germany, and, in general, any anti-German subtext, while insisting on the closest possible connection of the 3SI with the European Union.  The latter also applies to all other 3SI partners. In addition, there are also some geopolitical or geoeconomic considerations. Some governments (e.g. the one in Hungary) or some other important political actors (e.g. the Czech and the Croatian presidents, along with some important political parties in the Czech Republic) want to continue their cooperation with Russia or China. In the author’s opinion, the 3SI, in this context, is a framework for economic and infrastructural cooperation with a pragmatic focus, in which some influential participants project geopolitical aspirations. For example, Poland has successfully managed to bring Washington’s involvement to the fore, in line with the American interests, but has failed to block the other 3SI countries’ desire to attract Germany as a partner. The analysis also addresses the different roles played by the United States and Germany as 3SI partners. Some 3SI participating countries rely on a significant US involvement, while, on the other hand, the motives of Washington to engage in Eastern, Central, and Southeastern Europe have a clear geostrategic focus, meaning the role of this region in the global competition with Russia and China. The US main interest in the Three Seas Initiative continues to be focused on the 4 This analysis has been used in this particular part of the current presentation. 196 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION projects in the field of energy security. Therefore, any consistent support to the US is not only a sign of this platform’s geostrategic focus, but also attaches to it a transatlantic dimension. On its part, Germany has been sending signals about its preparedness to participate in the 3SI – from both the position of an active partner to contribute at the political level (by taking part in the 3SI Summits), as well as at the project level. Strengthening the sustainability and deepening the connectivity in and with this region is in Berlin’s interest and is going to help Germany to achieve its own climate and investment policy interests. Through cooperation in these areas, Germany is going to emphasize its already strong economic and political presence in Europe, to strengthen the “Europeanization” of the Three Seas Initiative, and to position itself as a geoeconomic player in this region along with the United States, China, and Russia. Russia’s infrastructural footprint in this region is a legacy of the bipolar world order; however, it is limited mainly to the energy sector. By their efforts at diversification via the construction of new pipelines and liquefied natural gas (LNG) facilities, the 3SI countries have been seeking to reduce their dependence on Russia or are already in the process of doing so. As a transit country, Bulgaria has been deriving some benefits from the Balkan Stream Gas Pipeline, which since December 2020 has been connected to the Turkish Stream Gas Pipeline; so, it has been transporting Russian gas to Serbia and is, at the same time, a consumer of the Trans-Adriatic Pipeline (TAP), which was also opened in late 2020. No matter how different the diversifying strategies of 3SI countries are, along with their cooperation with Russia, these countries are united by a common desire for greater flexibility and security of the natural gas supply through a better network, including the economic benefits. Potentially, China is yet another important infrastructural and political player in this region through its Belt and Road Initiative and its economic activities in Eastern Europe. The focus of the “16+1” Initiative has been on the investments in infrastructure. However, the Chinese investments have been mainly in the Western Balkan countries, with the exception of a couple of larger projects in Greece, Hungary and Croatia. The main topic of debate within the 3SI has been the participation of Chinese companies in the development of the 5G technology, due to the pressure exerted by the United States, as well as to the growing awareness of the security implications of the participation of Chinese investors. 7. The Three Seas Initiative. Prospects The implementation of the priority projects for regional connectivity in the field of transport, energy, and the digital technologies could contribute to the economic development of this region and are a key criterion for the success of the 3SI. The 3SI has provided a basis for strategically important infrastructure projects, two of which have so far been central in this direction:  The North-South Motorway Project. This project is going to connect Lithuania, Poland, Slovakia, Hungary, Romania, Bulgaria, and Greece. The proposed route is going to connect Klaipeda – Lithuania’s important port on the Baltic Sea, with 197 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION Thessaloniki – one of the most important trade centers in Southeast Europe. It is also going to help boost the trade with Poland by providing Eastern Poland with a north-south route to connect with Central Europe.  Liquefied natural gas (LNG) projects. Poland and Croatia already have liquefied natural gas terminals. The construction of a common LNG pipeline is also an element of this project. Out of the 77 priority projects in 2020, only three have been completed, seven have been registered, and 14 have been making great progress. On the other hand, only 12 % of the required funding, totaling more than € 85 billion, has been provided (according to the 3SI Status Report). Although the 3SI has not yet gained momentum, it is possible that all the 3SI countries could receive partial added value on the basis of these projects. Although the current discussions have outlined two strategic lines – for closer engagement with the United States (Croatia, Poland and Romania) and for non- departure from the European framework and the EU policies (Slovakia, the Czech Republic, Slovenia, Austria, and Hungary), the hope of the 3SI participating countries has been that this initiative is not going to contribute to a new division in Europe, but that it would rather contribute to the cohesion of the EU and to the further strengthening of the transatlantic ties. There also has been an idea of a n inclusive approach in support of a united Europe and strong transatlantic cooperation with a view of implementing the strategic investment projects in this region. There has also been a desire to actively involve the European Commission in the 3SI, to enhance the EU cohesion, to avoid the risk of duplication, and to link the 3SI to the already existing EU mechanisms and macro-regional strategies. The Kai-Olaf Lang‘s analysis identifies a couple of possible scenarios for the 3SI in the medium and the long run, as follows:  The accession of Germany is unlikely due to resistance on the part of Poland. An accession of Germany would turn the 3SI into a kind of enlarged Central European format with a German predominance. Germany’s economic strength is almost twice that of the 12 3SI participating countries.  An accession of some Eastern European countries outside the EU, such as Ukraine, is going to make the 3SI move closer to the idea of an Intermarium, inspired by some geostrategic considerations in Poland between the two World Wars. In addition, such a development is going to be met with reservations by all other participants.  A format similar to that of “16+1”, with the US instead of China, is unlikely in the case of Joe Biden’s administration, which does not aim at any division within the EU.  In theory, it would be possible to intensify the 3SI Europeanization and transform this initiative into an EU macro-strategy (similar to the Baltic Sea Strategy or the strategy for the Danube Region). Such a scenario is bound to fail, as the 3SI is based on countries, some of which are reluctant to transfer their sovereignty to the EU. Nevertheless, the EU complementarity approach will remain the dominant factor for the 3SI.  It cannot be ruled out, however, that the 3SI is going to develop as a kind of 198 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION a “Baltic Maritime Infrastructure Council” in the long run; it will continue to exist, but without being politically attractive and without any significant results. The topic involving the 3SI relations with the neighboring regions is also in perspective, and, in particular, the issue of a possible expansion of the Initiative. The Bulgarian president has already called for a possible accession of Greece and Cyprus to the 3SI. In early 2021, the presidents of Ukraine and Moldova also expressed some interest. However, in the nearest future, the main focus will be on the project-oriented cooperation, rather than on a full participation of some new countries in the Initiative. It is also considered that a possible accession of some non-EU countries of the Western Balkans, and especially of the Eastern Neighborhood area, is going to provoke strong reservations. Like any other 3SI country, Bulgaria has the opportunity through an investment of only 20 million € to implement projects having at times much higher returns. We have also had the first investment of the 3SI Fund in Bulgaria – in the solar park near the village of Karadjalovo, in the vicinity of Parvomay, with an area of 1 000 decares. The other projects presented to the 3SI for implementation by Bulgaria involve: the expansion of the gas storage site in the village of Chiren; restoration of the design parameters of the Ruse-Varna railway line; the construction of a tunnel under the Petrohan Pass, and construction of the Black Sea Highway (Burgas-Varna). Expanding the geographical scope of the 3SI is also important and will be another priority for Bulgaria. Greece and Cyprus are considered to be natural partners of the 3SI, as the Greek party participated in the Sofia Summit, held on July 8-9, as a guest and was represented there by the Greek President – Katerina Sakellaropoulou. In the early 2021, the presidents of Ukraine and Moldova indicated some interest in joining the Three Seas Initiative, but now the main focus is on cooperation and project implementation. It is also considered that the accession of any non-EU countries from the Western Balkans will not be welcomed by the participating countries. The new priority area “Science, Education and Innovations”, launched by Bulgaria, could also help attaining the 3SI objectives. The success of the Three Seas Initiative continues to depend on the strong commitment of some strategic 3SI partners, such as the United States and Germany, in synergy with the European Commission. While the main focus remains on consolidating this Initiative in its current format, Bulgaria supports a flexible and pragmatic approach to opening the 3SI on an ad hoc basis for selected value-added projects and by involving strategic partners and like-minded people outside its current geographical scope. An example of such flexibility would be the completion of the North-South Corridor by connecting it to the Greek ports in the Aegean Sea through modern infrastructure. A similar idea would be to connect the North-South connection with the Trans-European Corridor № 8, which links the Bulgarian Black Sea ports with the Albanian Adriatic coast. The potential of the 3SI can be useful for the stabilization process in the Western Balkans and the integration of this region into the rest of Europe. The opportunity for Bulgaria and Romania to combine their scientific and 199 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 REGIONAL COOPERATION educational potential in search of sustainable technologies and solutions in the field of transport and energy was one of the topics of conversation between the two presidents within the framework of this Initiative. Bulgaria and Romania should work to achieve much greater connectivity between the two countries, including through the construction of new bridges across the Danube, has been the opinion of the Bulgarian president. Deepening of the partnership with the European Commission in this direction is important for the overall development of the Danube River as a major transport corridor in Eastern Europe. President Johannis also stressed the need to improve the navigation of shipping in the Bulgarian-Romanian section of the Danube. The following years are particularly important for the 3SI, as this initiative could play a special role in contributing to a new phase in the transatlantic relations and in helping our region’s post-pandemic economic recovery and growth through improved connectivity, as well as by synergies and coordination between the available financial instruments and programs. Economic stability and development is the goal that this country has been striving for. The challenges for Bulgaria are many but, in general, we can highlight the need for effective absorption of the 3SI Fund’s financial resources, as well as the continuation of our close relations with Brussels. By realizing the 3SI potential, Bulgaria and the countries in this region have the opportunity to become a bridge between the East and the West, rather than a barrier. Conclusion The 3SI is an initiative with some particular positive potential. And although in its current form it could still be called a “young project”, it is perceived as a platform for regional cooperation based on the common values, promoting economic growth, overcoming the gaps and the deficiencies in the infrastructure and cohesion within EU, with a focus on Central and Eastern Europe. The 3SI has also a beneficial impact through its strong transatlantic dimension and consistent support from the United States on security and resilience in the wider Euro- Atlantic area. The implementation of the cross-border infrastructure projects proposed by the participating countries is fully in line with the EU policies for convergence, cohesion and equalization of the living standards between the EU Member States. Since its inception, every year, the history of the Three Seas Initiative has been writing a new chapter, with a view of realizing the vision of the common future of this region. The cooperation between the countries of the Three Seas Initiative and their partners is designed to lead to a stronger, more united and more competitive Europe on the international stage. Martin Krastev has attended the XVIth “Stoyan Petrov-Chomakov” Diplomacy Course for Trainee Attachés organized by the Diplomatic Institute at the Bulgarian Ministry of Foreign Affairs in 2021. He works at the Middle East and Africa Directorate of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. The consultant of this report is Ambassador Ivan Petrov. 200 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 BILATERAL COOPERATION 20th anniversary of the Council of Education at the Embassy of Spain in Bulgaria: educational activities and teaching of Spanish Angel Santamaria Barnola 1. Summary The year 2022 marks the 20th anniversary since the establishment of the Council of Education at the Embassy of Spain in Sofia. During the last quarter of the last century the intensity and warmth of the relations between Bulgaria and Spain were manifested in the fields of politics, strategy, business and economy, as well as in culture and education. Spanish, being a European and universal language, played a very important role as a tool in the extensive ties that connect the Bulgarians to the Spanish. In this article we are going to show how the relations in education were established, focusing on the development of studying Spanish and its transformation from a marginal and exclusive subject to one of the languages most wanted by the society and the educational community in Bulgaria in the 21st century. 2. Background of teaching Spanish in Bulgaria The first references to Spain in Bulgarian texts were made by Joan Exarch, one of the most remarkable figures at the Preslav literary school – the rich cultural, literal and artistic period that flourished between the 9th and the 10th century, at the time of the First Bulgarian Empire. During that prosperous period, the Exarch wrote his most important work Hexameron (Шестоднев). In it he introduced – apart from the story of the creation of God from Genesis – everything which he considered as a summary of the activities happening in the empire, the society, the court, the fashion and the knowledge of the Slavs in medicine, astronomy and historiography. It was in his work where Spain was mentioned for a first time in any text in Bulgarian. For its part, the demonym Bulgarian was first mentioned in the Spanish literature in the First General Chronicle by king Alfonso X el Sabio (the Wise), in the 13th century. It did not happen by chance, because as Jaime de Salazar says, the king of Toledo and Seville “represented a genetic conglomerate of European lineage, geographically from the Atlantic till the Black Sea and from the North Sea to the Mediterranean Sea. (….) for his mother had bound him with others from far away, like the German emperor, the emperor of Byzantium, the kings of Bohemia, Poland and Denmark and Serbia, the tsar of Bulgaria and the great prince of Kiev.” But it was from and within the medieval diaspora that Spanish could be heard in the Balkans. The first persecutions of Jews happened during the rule of the Capets in France in the 11th century. Since the 12th century the Jews were 201 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 BILATERAL COOPERATION successively expelled from German territories, from the French kingdom, from England. At the end of the 15th century two decrees, written by the Catholic kings of Spain and by Manuel I of Portugal, led to the leave of thousands of Jews from the Iberian Peninsula. While the Portuguese Jews were heading to the Netherlands, Morocco and the New World, the residents of the kingdoms of Castile and Aragon, without rejecting mentioned destinations, in their major part were going to the west coast of the Mediterranean Sea, settling in different parts of the Ottoman Empire and mainly in the present day Bulgaria. And because those Sephardim, coming from Sefarad (which means Spain in the modern Hebrew) rejoiced a superior sociocultural and economic level, compared to the local population, they could preserve their traditions, rituals and the unifying element, their language – the Old Spanish. The language of the Sephardim is called ladino and has been preserved for more than 500 years, and it bears local elements, as well as elements of the languages it has been in contact with for centuries. The Sephardim community managed to preserve the language in its own schools and in 1899, in Bulgaria, the first newspaper in ladino was published, El amigo del pueblo (The friend of the people). In 1940 in Bulgaria there were 48 000 Jews, 45 000 of whom were Sephardim. 3. The development of Hispanism in Bulgaria Mariana Dimitrova defines the years of the Ottoman presence in the Bulgarian territories as political, social and spiritual timelessness, which can explain the considerable slowness with which the European cultural trends, as well as the artistic and linguistic creations, were known to and accepted by the Bulgarians, especially the works coming from Spain. It was well after the beginning of the 19th century, with the first signs of the Bulgarian autonomous politics, when the interest of the Bulgarian intellectuals in foreign places began. In such a dynamic and emotional environment the first fragments of the Works of Miguel de Cervantes were translated in 1882, when in Rousse the first translation of Don Quixote de la Mancha was published. Yet, it was not the first work by Cervantes, nor was it the first Spanish one that was known in Bulgaria. Almost a quarter of a century previously, in a literary magazine, an excerpt from his novel El licenciado Vidriera (The Lawyer of Glass) was published, and it is regarded as the first translation from Spanish. It was published with the title Don Sancho’s Nephew. The translation was very far from the original text and was made using the French translation by L. Viardot, who rewrote it using both the exemplary novel and Don Quixote. The translation of Don Quixote in 1882 was made by Hristo N. Samsarov, a teacher and a Member of the Constitutional Assembly, who – very likely – also translated it from French. The Bulgarian translators of that time did not have good enough command of Spanish and for years had to translate Spanish works which, previously, had been translated in other languages. Soon afterwards, in 1884, the father of the Bulgarian letters, Ivan Vazov, prepared his Anthology – with texts meant for the secondary school students – where he introduced, as an obligatory text, chapter XVIII from Part One of the 202 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 BILATERAL COOPERATION novel, where Don Quixote encountered the flock of sheep and believed they were a military army. The Anthology could be considered as the beginning of studying Hispanic literature at the Bulgarian school. Before the end of the century, Kitanchev would publish the second edition of Don Quixote and the newspaper Liberty published excerpts from another of the exemplary novels by Cervantes – Rinconete and Cortadillo. The introduction of Don Quixote at schools was the ground on which in 1905 the famous philologist and historian of letters and a Minister of Education, Ivan Shishmanov (1862-1928), suggested to celebrate the three hundred years anniversary since the publication of the first part of Cervantes’ novel, with a series of commemorating events, and in order to do so, he sent a mailshot to the schools in the country, inviting them to reminisce the anniversary. Despite the fact that at the end of the 19th century a General Consulate was opened in Varna, and Spain and Bulgaria started their diplomatic relationship in 1910, Spanish works were not being translated in Bulgarian. The true spread of Spanish in the country started in the 30s of the 20th century. By that time, with the purpose to establish the first Spanish legation in Sofia in 1929, its leader, don Fernando Anton de Olmet, Marquis de Dosfuentes, initiated a project to send a professor in the Spanish language and literature to teach modern Spanish to the Sephardim, as well as to the Bulgarians in general. All those efforts bore fruit in 1933 when Jose Alvarez Prida became the first lecturer in Spanish at Sofia University “St. Kliment Ohridski” and taught lessons to 300 students, the bigger part of whom were Bulgarians, and not so many of a Sephardim origin. He was followed by Estanislao Quiroga y Abarca, a writer, microbiologist and researcher, with whom studied the veterinary, microbiologist and, above all, writer Dimitar Dimov, who later on would spend a year in Madrid, benefiting from a grant from Ramon y Cajal Institute. Quiroga would teach classes at the University, but also at the Spanish Club, which was an active cultural centre for the Bulgarian Sephardim. From 1937 on, the professor and outstanding novelist, Toma Tomov, started teaching at Sofia University; he was a disciple of Don Ramon Menendez Pidal and as of 1951 teaching Spanish had already extended to the School for Teaching Foreign Languages in Sofia, through Todor Neikov. Neikov was a key figure for the future of teaching and expanding of Spanish in Bulgaria. The son of a diplomat, he had arrived in Spain with his father in 1942, without knowing any Spanish. But shortly after his arrival he learned it and finally led the Spanish section at the Sofia Press agency. He also taught Spanish at the Language School in Sofia for eleven years and collaborated with the fellow Hispanist and professor, Emilia Tsenkova, and other authors, in making the Spanish-Bulgarian and Bulgarian-Spanish dictionaries. In 1970, he finished one of the best translations in Bulgarian of Don Quixote. It is commonly referred to as the “canonic” version, as it is the obligatory source for all references and quotations in the scientific field. He translated another of the most popular Cervantes’ works, Novelas ejemplares (Exemplary Novels), published in 1976. The Ministry of Culture of Spain recognized his dedication to the Spanish language with the National Prize for the Development of Translation. 203 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 BILATERAL COOPERATION Undoubtedly, one of the biggest milestones in the history of Hispanism in Bulgaria was the rise of Spanish as a bachelor’s degree major at the oldest and most prestigious university in the country – Sofia University “St. Kliment Ohridski”. The path of the Spanish Philology started in the autumn of 1961, owing to the support from Cuba, which sent lecturers and materials. 25 students finished the first course. In 2021, we celebrated the 60th anniversary since the creation of the Spanish Philology Department at Sofia University. In 1980, at the French Language School in Sofia, studying Spanish was promoted for a first time. Actually, the French Language School and the Spanish Language School were living under the same roof and were managed by the same headmaster, as the Spanish school was not still operating independently. Two years later, the Agreements for cooperation between Spain and Bulgaria in the fields of culture, education and science were signed, and this act made it possible to open, in 1993, the first Bilingual Section at 164 Language High School “Miguel de Cervantes” in Sofia. This would be the starting point for more Bulgarian schools to join the network of the Programme that is assisted by the Ministry of Education of Spain. 4. Establishment and development of the Bilingual Sections The programme of the bilingual sections is developed at the schools of the regulated education, specializing in teaching foreign languages, where the students study Spanish as a first language. These are secondary schools where first-year students receive up to 18 teaching hours in the target language. Subsequently, the students receive classes in other subjects, such as physics, biology, history, philosophy, which are taught in Spanish as a bridge language. After finishing the secondary level of education, the students who studied in such schools, have a very high level of linguistic competence in Spanish, commonly B2 or higher. In 1997, the IV Language High School “Frederic Joliot-Curie” in Varna joined the network of bilingual sections, to be followed later, in 1999 by the “Ivan Vazov” Language High School in Plovdiv, and later, by the “G.S. Rakovski” Romance Language High School in Burgas. Since then, there has been an expansion of schools that gradually become part of the Programme of Bilingual Sections the length and breadth of the country. Complementary to the teaching activities, in 1999, the National Festival for School Theatre started, and it has been held annually ever since. Following the schools that have been mentioned, the new century started with more schools that joined the network. In 2000 those were “Paisii Hilendarski” School in Dupnitsa and 22 “Georgi S. Rakovski” High School in Sofia, and the following year, the school year of 2001-2002, “Maxim Gorki” High School in Stara Zagora and “Peyo Yavorov” Language High School in Silistra. Faced with such a dynamics and with the interest that the Programme brought forward, the Ministries of Education of both countries signed, in 2001, the Agreement for the Establishment and Operation of Bilingual Sections with Spanish at the schools in the Republic of Bulgaria. The agreement defined the 204 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 BILATERAL COOPERATION conditions for cooperation and established the procedures under which schools could participate in the programme. In order to meet the needs of the schools and the teachers of Spanish in the language sections, a new step forward was made on the way of consolidation of teaching Spanish in the Bulgarian educational system. It was the establishment, in 2002, with a Royal Decree, of the Council of Education at the Embassy of Spain in Sofia, with Don Rafael Heredero de Pedro as its first counselor. In its operations this office depends on the Ministry of Education of Spain. Located in Sofia, it coordinates the activities of the attaché offices in Hungary and Romania, as well as the educational activities of the Spanish ministry in Moldova, Serbia and Turkey. It promotes and spreads the Spanish language and culture, manages the educational programmes that take place in Bulgaria, as well as in the other countries, and encourages cooperation in the field of education among the mentioned countries. The year 2022 will celebrate the 20th anniversary since the establishment of the Council. Moreover, the Council also accommodates the Resource Centre in Sofia, initially located in the Spanish Chancellery. The Resource Centre, since its very beginning, has been offering a constant support to teachers, students, Hispanists, friends of Spanish and lecturers at the universities. Owing to the constant growth of its fund, the many novels, film, materials on methods and about teaching Spanish like magazines and other linguistic works by Spanish, Hispanic and other authors were turned into a reference library for the community of students in Bulgaria. 5. The expansion of Spanish in Bulgaria in the new millennium The possibilities for development – personal, academic or professional – which presupposes the command of a foreign language spoken by more than 580 million people worldwide, as well as the allure of its culture, are some of the reasons for the unstoppable growth of studying Spanish in Bulgaria. It is not that difficult, in some cities in the country, to encounter people who are somehow connected with Spain. Spain has been a major emigration destination for the Bulgarians and it does not happen rarely for Bulgarian people to be connected to relatives and friends who have emigrated in Spain. Officially, there are around 120 000 Bulgarian citizens who reside in Spain. For this reason, the language and the culture of Spain are generally very well-accepted. The interest for Spanish has not ceased to grow and with it has grown the demand for learning. Meanwhile, the number of non-regulated teaching institutions that offer Spanish courses is growing too. The Institute Cervantes in Sofia All the above factors led to the establishment, in 2006, of the Institute Cervantes in Sofia. February 2021 celebrated the 15th anniversary since its official opening which was honoured with the presence of the then-Prince of Asturias, His Majesty don Felipe de Borbón, the actual king of Spain, and the then-President of the Republic of Bulgaria Georgi Parvanov. 205 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 BILATERAL COOPERATION Apart from offering courses in Spanish, the Institute Cervantes is in charge of organizing the exams to obtain the Certificates for Spanish as a Foreign Language (DELE). Some of the language schools are venues where applicants can sit the certificates exams. Prior to the pandemic of COVID-19, which has affected immensely the teaching in presence, the collected data about courses and enrollments show an ascending trend. For reference, below can be seen the results that the Institute Cervantes attained during the school year 2018-2019, when there were almost 200 courses with almost one thousand students. Applicants Exam centres Students Teachers Courses Enrollments for DELE DELE 977 19 195 1459 338 9 Source: Institute Cervantes in Sofia Apart from the courses, the Institute Cervantes in Sofia also organizes and collaborates in cultural events that are related to Spanish and the Spanish- speaking world, like concerts, exhibitions, talks and film screenings. The Council of Education and the Programme of Bilingual Spanish Sections As much as it refers to the regulated education, the one that encompasses primary and secondary education (classes from I till XII), the ascending trend in studying Spanish is obvious, and every year the number of students who are willing to learn Spanish is increasing, despite the fact that the general school enrollments in the country are decreasing every year. According to the data from the National Statistical Institute of Bulgaria about the last five school years, the number of students of each language in the regulated education – grades from I to XII – allows to prove that all other languages, but Spanish, experience a decline, and the rise is with 11.7 % despite the decrease in school enrollments for the period, with 6.7 %. In absolute terms, Spanish holds fourth place among the most wanted foreign languages, after English, German and Russian. 2016-2017 2017-2018 2018-2019 2019-2020 2020-2021 German 65 581 65 724 64 141 60 548 60 487 Spanish 17 066 17 297 18 292 18 758 19 074 French 21 552 19 397 18 417 17 200 17 528 English 507 575 494 288 496 423 486 348 486 590 Italian 3515 3804 3804 3643 3797 Russian 76 072 71 467 66 422 62 185 61 022 Evaluation of teaching languages in the regulated education in Bulgaria, 2016-2021. Source: National Statistical Institute of Bulgaria, 2021 206 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 BILATERAL COOPERATION During the school year 2020-2021, the total number of students of Spanish can be distributed as follows, according to the educational stage: Grades Grades Grades Job training Total I-IV V-VII VIII-XII Students of Spanish 662 4004 14 408 2229 21 303 Distribution of Spanish students in Bulgaria, 2020-2021, by educational stages. Source: National Statistical Institute of Bulgaria, 2021 As can be seen, the majority of students of Spanish is to be found in the secondary education, between grades VIII and XII. It is so because teaching foreign languages is truly developed in the Bulgarian educational system mainly in the secondary language schools. These schools are a modality within the specialized secondary education. They are regarded as highly prestigious and, thus, generate a demand in the families that cannot always be met, as is the example with 164 Language School “Miguel de Cervantes” in Sofia, where in the year 2018-2019 around two thousand pupils applied to study Spanish. Following this boom about Spanish, the network of schools that integrate the Programme for Bilingual Sections in the last years has been in constant increase, spreading in the whole country and making sure that the number of students that receive language training does not stop increasing. Currently, in the country there are fifteen schools that are teaching Spanish and are under the Agreement between the Ministry of Education and Science of the Republic of Bulgaria and the Ministry of Education and Professional Training in Spain. Bulgarian schools that are part of the Programme are the following Burgas: Language School “G.S.Rakovski”; Dimitrovgrad: Language School “Doctor Ivan Bogorov“; Dupnitsa: Language School “St. Paisii Hilendarski”; Haskovo: Language School “Prof. Asen Zlatarov”; Pleven: Language School “Dimitar Dimov”; Plovdiv: I Language School “Ivan Vazov“; Ruse: Language School “St. Constantine-Cyril Philosopher”; Sofia: Language School Nº 22 “Georgi S. Rakovski“; Sofia: Language School Nº 164 “Miguel de Cervantes”; Sofia: Language School Nº 157 “César Vallejo“; Sofia: Language School Nº 30 “Bratya Miladinovi”; Sofia: School 127 “Ivan Nikolaevich Denkoglu”; Stara Zagora: Language School “Maxim Gorki”; Varna: IV Language School “Fr. Joliot-Curie”; Veliko Tarnovo: Language School “Prof. Dr. Asen Zlatarov”. In the year 2020-2021, within the Programme, 3836 pupils studied Spanish as a first language and 1550 as a second language. The teachers give classes about the Spanish language, literature, civilization and culture. The majority 207 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 BILATERAL COOPERATION of the Spanish departments in the schools consist of Bulgarian teachers of language and literature, and also of Bulgarian teachers that teach other subjects in Spanish, like history, geography, physics, chemistry and biology. Yet, as a consequence of the above-mentioned Agreement, all those schools also rely on professionals coming from Spain. As of the current school year, in Bulgaria there are 16 native Spanish teachers, selected after a public announcement of the Ministry of Education and Professional Training of Spain. Support with training teachers Moreover, trying to alleviate the lack of teachers specialized in Spanish or of other non-linguistic subjects, the Ministry of Education and Professional Training developed the programme Europrof. This programme is a result of the collaboration of various institutions both Spanish and Bulgarian, and is based on the Memorandum of Understanding between the Ministry of Education of Spain and the Ministry of Education of Bulgaria, signed on 14 February 2012 and on a singularized Agreement made between the Spanish Ministry of Education with the Institute Cervantes, in order to meet the increasing demand for learning Spanish in the Bulgarian educational system and the need of new teachers through a double training: • Training in Spanish for teachers of other foreign languages, in which the same teachers acquire linguistic competence and pedagogical capacity required by the Bulgarian legislation in order to work as a teacher of Spanish language and literature at the schools in Bulgaria; • Training in Spanish for teachers of other, non-linguistic subjects, so that they acquire the linguistic competence required by the Bulgarian legislation in order to be able to teach the same subjects in Spanish at the school of the Republic of Bulgaria. The Agreement is an example of the collaboration between the Council of Education, the Ministry of Education and Science of Bulgaria, the Institute Cervantes and a local University, where the future teachers finish their training. Moreover, the Council of Education carries out other training modalities in collaboration with other institutions. Among them, it is worth mentioning the annual meeting of teachers of Spanish, which is organized in collaboration with the Institute Cervantes and the Sofia University. It also manages the courses and grants offered by Spanish schools and universities. In 2021, the University of Salamanca has given several grants to foreign teachers of Spanish in teaching from distance and from which five Bulgarian teachers have benefited. In the same way and also in 2021, the Council managed free training courses online, offered by the Ministry of Education and Professional Training of Spain. A dozen of Bulgarian teachers were selected to take part in the online course, which took place in the last quarter of the year. Professionals from the National University of Distant Education of Spain (UNED) led the courses. Before the pandemic, and now left with the hope to resume them shortly, the Council of Education was organizing training summer courses in Spain for 208 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 BILATERAL COOPERATION Bulgarian teachers of Spanish, mainly offered by the University of Salamanca and the Menéndez Pelayo International University of Santander. Furthermore, annually, the Council develops a training plan in accordance with the National Institute for Educational Technologies and Teacher Training and the Ministry of Education and Professional Training of Spain, which is taught in various training modules like congresses, seminars, working groups or courses, and which events are available to all Bulgarian teachers of Spanish. Of particular interest, in those times when teaching from distance has been developing tremendously fast, is an online course for teachers of Spanish or other subjects in Spanish, called “Computer Tools for Use in the Bilingual Sections of the Spanish Educational Activity Abroad”, where Spanish teachers from the Programme of Bilingual Sections in Bulgaria and Bulgarian teachers of the same sections took part in. All these activities are constantly improving the quality of teaching Spanish and other subjects, as a whole, and are facilitating the exchange of experience and professional development; they also show the high level of collaboration between the Bulgarian and Spanish schools. Activities for student support within the Programme of Bilingual Sections The students at the schools in the Programme of Bilingual Sections benefit from the activities organized by the Council of Education, where they are the first to participate. The activities go to the direction of increasing the motivation to study, and among these activities stand out the visits of professionals and writers, and the Festival of School Theatre in Spanish. The Council also organizes talks, conferences and activities for the pupils of different schools. To do so, it invites experts, writers and academics who come from Spain to visit the schools of the Programme to teach masterclasses or to speak with the students. Among the persons who have visited the schools and shared their knowledge and experience, we could name some of the most recent ones – the writers Lorenzo Silva and Manuel Vilas, the journalist Nacho Carretero, the illustrator Axier Uzkudun, the scientist Javier Cacho or the head of the Spanish Football League Jesús Pizarro. Furthermore, for the 110th anniversary since the establishment of the diplomatic relations between Spain and Bulgaria, in the last year – always with the imposed restrictions for the pandemic – were carried out, whenever possible, visits in the schools by His Excellency the Ambassador of Spain and the Counselor of Education. The competition for photography in Spanish is already a tradition, as well as the one about stories and micro stories, the international competition for short films, or the competition that started this year – for comics – the winners of which were selected to participate in the international competition for comics, organized by the Council of Education of Spain in Rome. The topics are always diverse, so that the competitions take advantage and go deep into topics that cross beyond education and thus help the students achieve a bigger sensibility in front of the global problems, and for which aim are chosen topics related to the Agenda 2030 of the UN. 209 DIPLOMACY 27/2022 BILATERAL COOPERATION Undoubtedly, the greatest manifestation of the Spanish presence in the schools in Bulgaria is the National Festival of School Theatre, which in 2020 had its 21st edition and in which about twenty schools took part. The festival has two categories – one for students of the Programme of Bilingual Sections and a second one for the schools that teach Spanish but are not part of the Programme. The festival is highly respected in the schools. It is an honour to participate and a privilege to receive an award in any of the categories. In the last edition the head of the jury was the Director of the Royal Higher School for Dramatic Arts in Madrid. There is another International Festival where come the winners of national contests in some of the countries of Eastern Europe where the Programme of Bilingual Sections is operating. At the moment, efforts are being invested into holding the edition of this international festival in 2022 in Sofia, if only in the region there is a positive development of the pandemic of COVID-19. Finally, the students with the best academic records from the Programme of Bilingual Sections compete for grants offered by various Spanish universities. As of today, the agreements signed directly with four Spanish universities offer five scholar grants and economic support for the students. The first part of Bulgarian students who won grants has already left for Spain in 2021. Meanwhile, negotiations with other universities are going on, in order to obtain new agreements from which the best Bulgarian students can benefit. 6. Conclusions Spanish teaching is ever more increasing worldwide. As much for the possibilities for personal and professional development that it offers in the vast economical horizon of chances that knowing it presupposes in a community of 21 countries, but also for the emotional pleasure of just learning Spanish and living its culture and the international significance it has. It is not surprising then that despite the decrease in school enrollments year after year, the number of those who search to study Spanish in the regulated education of Bulgaria is increasing. Regarding higher education – as Marisa Santos and Valentina de Antonio wrote in an article about the Spanish in Bulgaria in the 2021 annual of the Institute Cervantes – although it is not easy to know the exact number of students that study foreign languages at the universities in Bulgaria, when it comes to Spanish, there are two universities in Sofia that provide a degree in Spanish Philology and where every year 40 students finish their studies. The University of Veliko Tarnovo also provides specialization in Spanish as a part of its Applied Linguistics – where every year 15 students graduate. There are three other universities where Spanish is studied as a foreign language: in Varna, Plovdiv and the University of National and World Economy in Sofia, where Tourism and Economy teach Spanish. For the Council of Education at the Embassy of Spain, Bulgaria and especially its capital city, Sofia, with its strategic location in the Balkans, is considered as a central point of Spanish in the region. The National University of Distant 210 ДИПЛОМАЦИЯ 27/2022 BILATERAL COOPERATION Education is developing, along with similar institutions, a future establishment of the head office of an Associated Centre in Sofia, and all that confirms the reciprocal interest between organizations in both counties. The exceptional understanding with the Bulgarian educational authorities and the collaboration among the institutions that work in an environment of cooperation and respect only favours the perspective. It is for this reason that, although some of the other countries that the Office manages, have ten times more the population of Bulgaria or has a language which shares its Latin origin, Sofia is like the capital city of Spanish in the South-Eastern Europe. Angel Santamaria Barnola has a Bachelor´s degree in Art History and a Master´s degree in Teaching Spanish as a Foreign Language. A teacher and tutor of Spanish, he has held positions at the Ministry of Education of Spain in Australia, the UK and China. He was head of the Unit for International Relations at the Ministry in the Cabinets of the Ministers of Education Mercedes Cabrera, Angel Gabilondo and Isabel Celaá. He has published various methods on teaching Spanish to foreigners, articles and an analysis of the education in Spain called “Heducación se escribe sin hache” (“Heducation is spelled without H”). Since September 2021, he has been the Counselor of Education at the Embassy of Spain in Sofia.
